# Theodore Beza, *On Heretics to Be Punished by the Civil Magistrate* (1582)

> AI-assisted, unreviewed working translation. This is a complete close working translation from the controlling 1582 Latin witness, not a summary. No modern translation was consulted or copied.

[PDF page 2 | logical scan 1 | printed page 85]

## On Heretics to Be Punished by the Civil Magistrate, against Martin Bellius’s Miscellany and the Sect of the New Academics

Since at this time, if ever at any other, the Church of God is being attacked most persistently at home and abroad by innumerable forces of enemies, it is astonishing that the hearts of so few are moved to bring her aid. For some long for the peace and tranquility of former times—those, namely, who especially delight in that manner of life which is joined with the deepest ignorance. Others are wholly occupied in lamenting the calamities of this time and meanwhile give no thought to applying remedies. Some carelessly mock all these things. Very few, however, strive according to their strength to meet these evils; and whenever these latter, stirred up by the Spirit of God, confront the common evils, so far are they from being welcomed by most people that, on the contrary, as once happened to Moses the deliverer of Israel, they rouse against themselves an innumerable host of enemies, who oppose their efforts under a pretense of piety. We recently beheld a signal example of this ungrateful spirit, whose shamefulness has made me think that at this time I must by no means remain silent.

For a year ago, when God Most Good and Most Great, the most just avenger of injuries done to him, had snatched that most impure heretic Servetus from the jaws of the papists and, as it were, delivered him by hand to the Republic of Geneva, through which that man—the most impious and blasphemous of all who had lived up to that time—was to pay the penalty for his crimes, the most distinguished Senate of that city and its ministers, each for his own part, conducted themselves in administering the matter in such a way that you could rightly find wanting neither diligence and piety in exposing and refuting his impiety, nor charity and care in calling the man back to repentance, nor prudence and good faith in handling his case, nor constancy and greatness of spirit in vindicating the majesty of God.

*[Marginal note: Servetus, a heretic.]*

Therefore, when he had been cast into prison, certain emissaries of Satan at once began to cry out that it was most unjust that Servetus, that good and learned man (whom they themselves were not ashamed to call their excellent little brother), should plead his case in chains before those who boasted of Christian liberty. Then, when they perceived that these outcries moved no one except a few men like themselves, they dropped the case of Servetus himself and, carried away by a diseased mind and ungoverned ambition as if by some raging heat, began to disgorge all their bile upon those through whose vigilance, as they understood, Servetus had been apprehended, cast into chains, and finally convicted. These crafty men thought that a convenient occasion had been offered them for obscuring the reputation of those by whose splendor they had long been miserably scorched: namely, if they slandered them by saying that in prosecuting Servetus they had neither performed the duty of ecclesiastical ministers nor looked to any benefit of the Church, but had clothed their own private passion with the name of piety so that, under the pretext of religion, they might crush the man by whom they had been provoked.

These, at any rate, were the things they habitually asserted without disguise for as long as Servetus was in prison; and, like the Donatists of old, at one time they would ask whether any example of this sort existed in the Scriptures, and at another deny that the civil magistrate had any authority at all over heretics. Indeed, to the great disgrace of that Republic, whenever they met anyone who was coming here to us, they would laughingly say that he was setting out for a second pope, and they most shamelessly shouted that this Church, flourishing in examples of piety and doctrine—

[PDF page 3 | logical scan 2 | printed page 86]

—was a new workshop of restored papal cruelty. But neither could that most courageous Senate be deterred from performing its duty in vindicating the majesty of the Son of God, after taking the counsel of the other churches of Switzerland; nor could the ministers and pastors be drawn away by their worthless slanders from publicly undertaking an orthodox defense of the Trinity and at the same time declaring in a published book that heretics are rightfully restrained by the sword, and that the punishment of Servetus in particular had been rightfully exacted.

That book had scarcely appeared when these good and merciful patrons of heretics adopted a new plan. For, since they saw that his heresies had been condemned by the Christian churches with the greatest agreement, they judged that if they openly took up his cause, they would do so neither safely enough nor with any benefit. They therefore resolved to plead their case in such a way that they would say not a single word about the affair of Servetus, but would argue in general that heretics, as though they were sacred persons, ought to be touched by no one—or at least that the civil magistrate ought to be granted no power over them. They especially supposed that, because of Antichrist’s savagery, this argument would be welcome and plausible to most people; and once they had obtained this concession from them, they themselves would thereafter be free to assail the orthodox churches with impunity.

Yet they did not even attempt this except very craftily and cautiously. For they were unwilling either to put their own names on this book or to say most things in their own person. Instead, they published a certain miscellany, partly collected in bad faith from the writings of certain learned and pious men, both ancient and recent, and partly patched together from their own books and those of certain other fanatics. A preface was also added by the man who compiled that synopsis under the name of a certain Martin Bellius. I truly marvel that this man, though in other matters he is so bold and rash that he seems even to seek glory from it, was nevertheless so fearful in this defense of clemency that he dared not appear in public except in disguise. Finally, so that the later portions might correspond to the earlier, another man who shared this Bellius’s counsels added, under the name of Basilius Montfortius, a refutation of the things customarily said in favor of persecution. For that is the ingenious name these men give to the punishments inflicted upon the most harmful men. I am indeed not at all surprised that he preferred to appear under another’s name rather than his own. For since he understood that he was all too well known to those against whom he wrote, I grant that this fear of his was entirely justified.

But their extraordinary audacity is evident in this: both that they dared to dedicate that ignorant and impious preface to that most illustrious and truly Christian prince of Württemberg, and that they were not ashamed to put upon it the name of that most renowned and courageous city of Magdeburg. For, to say nothing of the book itself, that preface of Bellius is certainly of such a kind that, just as that excellent prince and the pious citizens of Magdeburg abhor blasphemies of this kind with their whole mind, so I do not doubt that they would by no means have permitted anyone to abuse their authority in publishing such blasphemies to the common people. I earnestly urge them to investigate the matter, because these men plainly deserve to have confirmed upon them by punishment and correction the very doctrine which I have undertaken to maintain.

To say something of myself: I confess that their crime has wounded me so deeply that neither my nature, which shrinks from every controversy, could prevent me, nor the weakness of which I am conscious restrain me, nor the gravity of this subject deter me from opposing their audacity and shamelessness. Moreover, the exhortation of certain men greatly inflamed my resolve. I have always attributed so much to their judgment that I believed I ought to take upon myself whatever burden they imposed on me. Added to this is the fact that those most renowned men, greater than every praise—whom, as I ought, I name for honor’s sake: Philip Melanchthon, John Calvin, Heinrich Bullinger, and Wolfgang Capito—have explained this same subject so fully and clearly that most things have had to be copied by me rather than newly devised. So far am I from being ashamed to confess that we have done this that, on the contrary, I most gladly rest in the name and authority of such great men.

Furthermore, such zeal as I possess will not (I hope) extinguish or retard the study and diligence of others, to whom I readily yield the first place, but will rather kindle and spur them on if the wickedness of our adversaries demands it. For my part, I have certainly determined to plead this case in such a way that, unless Bellius’s perverse arrangement compels me, I will say nothing beside the point; and I have resolved to observe such moderation that no good man may rightly complain that I have injured him. For what they appear especially to have sought—namely, to embroil the man who would prepare a defense with those good and pious men from whom they copied part of their miscellany—will be avoided by me with the greatest care and cau-

[PDF page 4 | logical scan 3 | printed page 87]

-tion, since I have resolved to defend, not attack, the very men whose protection they abuse. In this way, I think, their audacity and shamelessness will be uncovered, while the dignity and authority of those other men will be preserved safe and sound, as is right.

*[Marginal heading: The threefold division of this treatise.]*

Therefore, to come at last to the matter, since a threefold question has been proposed to us—first, whether heretics ought to be punished; next, whether this punishment belongs also to the civil magistrates; and last, whether they may even be punished with death—we shall discuss first heretics, then the magistrate, and finally the question itself. Of these questions, Bellius and his faction have set out the first in such a way that it is quite evident they sought this one thing: to cast darkness over the truth. The second, however, which was perhaps the chief question, they omitted altogether. Here, therefore, either Bellius’s ignorance or (as I rather suppose) his wickedness lays a double burden upon me.

*[Marginal heading: Who are heretics.]*

For his marvelous teaching makes it necessary for us to say who are not heretics before we can define heretics. Thus Bellius, after completing his treatment of the proposed question, only then comes to the definition of a heretic—although this, he says, ought to have been known first of all. Then, so as to entangle everything, he teaches that the name “heretic” is no more infamous than the names “miser” and “buffoon”; and here he fashions a new argument that heretics should not be killed because neither misers nor buffoons are killed. We shall discuss this elsewhere. He next complains that anyone who dissents from us is commonly regarded as a heretic; and he mints for us a certain golden coin that is to be current everywhere, whatever image it bears.

*[Marginal heading: The sum of the Academics’ theology.]*

“To believe,” he says, “in God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, and to approve the precepts of piety contained in the Holy Scriptures, is a golden coin, more approved and tested than gold itself. Yet up to now this coin has borne different images, while men disagree among themselves about the Supper, Baptism, and other matters of this kind—that is” (as he was not ashamed to write at the beginning of his preface) “about Christ’s own state and office, how he is one with the Father, likewise about the Trinity, predestination, free will, God, angels, and the state of souls after this life. These matters”—O the blasphemy of Bellius and his dreadful impiety!—“are neither so necessary to know in order to obtain salvation through faith, nor can they be known before we have a pure heart; nor, even if known, do they make a person better.” For this is the sum of their theology, which we shall explain more fully elsewhere.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation of the definition of a heretic that Bellius offers.]*

Let us now see how he teaches, not from common opinion but from Holy Scripture, who ought to be called a heretic. And here, reader, whoever you may be, consider with me, I beg you, what skill, facility, or faithfulness he shows in interpreting Scripture. “A heretic,” he says, “is one who, after being duly admonished, does not obey.” Is that really so, Bellius? Certainly, if matters stand thus, what remains but for us to say that regions and cities—why do I say cities? indeed, families and every single household—are full of heretics? But lest I seem to slander him, let us also hear the reasoning by which this definition is confirmed.

“Because,” he says, “Paul, writing to Titus, commands him to avoid a heretical man after one and a second admonition. This passage, moreover, corresponds to the Lord’s command written in Matthew: ‘If your brother sins against you.’ For the one whom Paul calls a heretic, Christ describes as one who ‘will not hear you or the others’; and what Paul calls ‘Avoid,’ Christ expresses as, ‘Let him be to you as a Gentile and a tax collector.’” *[Marginal references: Titus 3:10; Matthew 18:15–17.]*

I answer that the best method of explaining Scripture is indeed to expound it by comparing passages and to let the Holy Spirit be his own interpreter. Yet who can understand the mysteries of this man’s comparison? For this is how he reasons: To regard someone as a Gentile and tax collector is the same as to avoid him. “If he will not hear you or the others” has the same force as “not to obey a repeated admonition.” Therefore both Christ and Paul call the man who does not obey after being duly admonished a heretic. Yet even if we grant his premise, no sane person will grant this inference unless it is first shown that there is only one class of persons to be admonished and, if they do not obey, avoided. Otherwise we shall reply that these sayings can refer to different people, as indeed they must.

For who does not see that in Matthew the duty of every Christian is prescribed concerning private offenses, or at least offenses of such a kind that they are not publicly manifest? Paul commands public sins to be reproved publicly, and he himself followed this method in reproving Peter. *[Marginal references: 1 Timothy 5:20; Galatians 2:14.]* Writing to Titus, however, while instructing a bishop, he prescribes matters that belong to the public ministry, not to the private duty of each person, as appears from the whole epistle. Who does not see that these must be distinguished? Nor should it have been omitted that Matthew mentions three admonitions, each of a different kind, whereas Paul mentions only two. Finally, the very etymology of the word which Bellius supplies afterward could and should have taught him that the name “heretic” does not apply to all obstinate people, but only to those who, departing from the Church,

[PDF page 5 | logical scan 4 | printed page 88]

establish a distinct sect or faction. As for his bringing in here as well the saying, “Shake the dust from your feet,” it needs no refutation. *[Marginal reference: Mark 6:11.]* For it is sufficiently clear that this is said against those who utterly reject the gospel offered to them. How it applies to those who either commit some sin in private life or dissent from the common opinion on certain articles of religion, I cannot see. No one can deny that these things must be distinguished.

Finally, to complete this comparison, he also adds: “What Paul says—that such a man sins so that he is condemned by his own self—is what Christ immediately adds: ‘Whatever you bind on earth will be bound in heaven.’” Now I grant that heretics are rightly excluded from the fellowship of the faithful, that this judgment of the Church is ratified by God, and indeed that, in proportion to the magnitude of their crime and blasphemy, they must sometimes even be driven from the number of the living. Nevertheless, I deny that this passage of Paul concerns the public exclusion of heretics. Rather, Titus is admonished that when repeated admonition has failed to bring them back to sound doctrine, he should thereafter leave them alone: “because,” Paul says, “you know that people of this kind are perverted and sin, being condemned by themselves”—as if to say that nothing can be accomplished by disputing with such men. *[Marginal reference: Titus 3:11.]* For you argue in vain with a person who brings an evil conscience to the dispute; and if he will not yield to the judgment of his own conscience, how will he be overcome by the arguments of others? So much, then, for Bellius’s definition.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation of Bellius’s division of heretics.]*

Let us go on to see how much more successfully he divides than defines them. “There are,” he says, “two kinds of heretics or obstinate persons. Some are obstinate in morals, such as misers, buffoons, the licentious, drunkards, persecutors, and the rest who do not correct themselves when admonished. Others are obstinate in spiritual matters and doctrine, and to these the name ‘heretic’ properly applies. For *heresy* is a Greek word signifying a sect or opinion. Hence those who cling too tenaciously to a vicious sect or opinion are called heretics.” Bellius recognizes his own words, I presume. Since he illustrates his division by examples, come then, let us consider this part also. Speaking of the former, he says: “Such were the Jews, scribes, Pharisees, and Christ’s fellow countrymen, because of whose hardness of heart Christ could not perform many miracles there.” As an example of the latter he sets forward that Hananiah who is discussed in Jeremiah. *[Marginal reference: Jeremiah 28:1.]*

Yet there is much here to criticize. First, since this whole division rests upon his regarding every obstinate person as a heretic, and since we have already shown that to be false, this division must collapse. But come, let us grant that there are also some heretics in morals. For how can all who refuse to enter the path of true religion order their life rightly? The apostles therefore deservedly reprove them as guilty of every vice. Yet we flatly deny that everyone obstinate in evil conduct is thereby a heretic. Only those are so who, while professing true religion with their mouth, nevertheless confirm themselves and others in evil conduct by some false opinion. Such once were (to omit the rest) the Carpocratians, whose heresy the Libertines have revived in our age, abolishing every distinction between shameful and honorable. Such also are the Anabaptists, who condemn lawful marriages and magistracy and defend parricide by their fanatical revelations. These, I say, I grant to be heretics even in morals, although they deserve the designation “heretics” because of their depraved opinions rather than their deeds. For if one of our people commits the same things yet confesses that he is sinning, I will call such a man not a heretic but rather a criminal. The Jews and Turks, however, who have never embraced our religion, I shall call not heretics but unbelievers, even if in other respects they excel in every human virtue.

I say that the second member of the division is also defective because it includes everyone who clings too tenaciously to a vicious opinion. In addition to this, I maintain that in every kind of heretic it is required that they wage a civil war against the Church’s received doctrine. But Bellius errs still more openly when he numbers among those whom he wishes to be heretics in morals Jews, scribes, Pharisees, and Christ’s fellow countrymen, just as he wants Hananiah to be an example of those who are heretics in doctrine. First, I ask, from what sort of logic did such a division ever arise: Jews, scribes, Pharisees? The outward life of men of this kind was certainly for the most part commendable, as appears from the whole history of the Gospel. They are reproved not as openly criminal, but partly as hypocrites and partly because they obscured the true worship of God with their inventions. Therefore, when the Lord had said, “Do what they say; do not do according to their works,” he shortly afterward explains what he condemned in their works—namely, that they made a show of piety. *[Marginal reference: Matthew 23:3.]* They ought therefore to have been called hypocrites rather than heretics, unless account is taken of their doctrine, which we can truly call a heresy. As for his adding that Christ for this reason

[PDF page 6 | logical scan 5 | printed page 89]

avoided them when he said, “Behold, your house will be left desolate,” deserves severe censure as well. *[Marginal reference: Matthew 23:38.]* For since this refers to the destruction of Jerusalem by which the Lord justly avenged contempt for himself, who does not see that the passage is badly twisted to another purpose? What shall I say about Christ’s fellow countrymen? They rejected Christ’s doctrine, and for that reason the Lord performed few mighty works among them because of their unbelief. *[Marginal reference: Matthew 13:58.]* Thus this classification confuses not only criminals, hypocrites, and unbelievers with heretics, but opinions with conduct as well.

Concerning Hananiah, whom Bellius wishes to have been a heretic in doctrine, Jeremiah wrote the following. When Jeremiah, by the Lord’s command, announced an impending calamity, Hananiah predicted that everything contrary would happen. Shortly afterward, therefore, Jeremiah announced to him by the Lord’s command that, because he had spoken rebellion against the Lord, he would die within that year. *[Marginal reference: Jeremiah 28:1–17.]* Thus, because rebellion is mentioned here, Bellius immediately pronounces him a heretic, and a doctrinal heretic at that. Yet if you consider the particular deed, in which the dispute was not about religion itself but about a certain event that would or would not happen, I say that he should be regarded as a criminal or liar rather than a heretic. If Bellius instead considers the fact that Hananiah drew the people into a separate faction, I will nevertheless call him a schismatic, not a heretic, unless he is proved to have been a teacher of a different religion.

So much for Bellius. From these statements I have been so far from being able to learn from Scripture who ought to be judged heretics and how many kinds of them there are that, on the contrary, I cannot sufficiently marvel that a man who either knows nothing at all about the very matter in question or pretends that he does not know can nevertheless define the magistrate’s duty so haughtily. Setting up a false appearance of clemency and humanity, he is not in the least ashamed to accuse so many faithful churches, so many Christian princes, and so many pious and learned ministers of God’s Word as executioners and persecutors.

*[Marginal heading: Who truly are heretics.]*

But come, let us ourselves inquire who heretics are and how many kinds of them exist. I therefore distinguish human beings into two general classes. The first consists of those who openly reject the true and only religion, that is, the Christian religion, and are wholly alien to the Church. We call them by the special name “unbelievers.” Such in certain regions are idolaters, Turks, Jews, and any others who do not profess themselves Christians even in name.

*[Marginal heading: Unbelievers.]*

The apostle plainly testifies concerning the Ephesian idolaters: “You were,” he says, “without God in the world.” *[Marginal reference: Ephesians 2:12.]* A little earlier he had explained how this must be understood, saying that they were without Christ, or separated from Christ. If we follow this rule (as indeed we must), rejecting Bellius’s counterfeit coin, we shall number Turks and Jews no less than Ephesian idolaters among unbelievers, or the godless. “For whoever denies the Son,” says John, “does not have the Father either.” *[Marginal reference: 1 John 2:23.]* Now the Son is denied by anyone who does not acknowledge Christ as the eternal Son of God, who came in the flesh so that we might be saved through him alone. Therefore the Lord, speaking expressly of the Jews, says, “If you had believed Moses, you would have believed me also.” *[Marginal reference: John 5:46.]*

The other class consists of those who, having embraced the Christian faith, profess themselves citizens of God’s kingdom. Now because the Lord foretold—and the event afterward confirmed—that the kingdom of God is like a dragnet cast into the sea which gathers every kind of fish, it is evident that there is more than one kind of citizen in this kingdom. For, to set apart the truly faithful, who are very few in number—that is, those who possess a solid and sincere knowledge of Christian doctrine and have consecrated themselves wholly to Christ—

*[Marginal heading: The faithful.]*

—some, although they wish to be regarded as Christians, either follow the Christian life only hypocritically, imitating Judas the betrayer, or order their lives in such a way that their whole life testifies that not the Spirit of God but the devil reigns in them. Augustine calls them bad Catholics; Scripture calls them hypocrites, disobedient, children of the flesh, faithless, and indeed even unbelieving. *[Marginal heading: Hypocrites.]* For, as John says, “He who says, ‘I know him,’ and does not keep his commandments is a liar, and the truth is not in him.” *[Marginal reference: 1 John 2:4.]*

Again, there are some who both approve the doctrine and otherwise live blamelessly, yet, driven by some offense of mind, by fickleness, or by the slanders of others, create divisions in the Church—just as at Corinth in former times some boasted of Cephas, others of Paul, and others of Apollos. *[Marginal heading: Schismatics.]* When they acquire disciples, they breed schisms in the Church, which at length degenerate into heresies. For once their minds are alienated, Satan drives them to the point that, through a perverse hatred or love of certain persons and by fastening upon the persons themselves, they not only prefer some men to others, but gradually begin to hate the men themselves and indiscriminately condemn whatever they say or do. This is why Augustine somewhere said that heresy is an inveterate schism. Properly speaking, however, a schism is the separation of people who hold the same beliefs—a definition given by Cresconius which even Augustine himself does not greatly criticize. *[Marginal reference: Augustine, second book against Cresconius.]* Finally, some dissent not so much in conduct

[PDF page 7 | logical scan 6 | printed page 90]

as in doctrine from the orthodox. They do so sometimes through weakness of intellect, negligence, an ingrained habit of error received by tradition, or finally through the deceit of certain people who ensnare minds not yet sufficiently confirmed in the truth. At other times they do so through faithlessness and wickedness, when hatred of God, greed, ambition, lust, and other desires of this kind which drive away God’s Spirit seize and carry us headlong. *[Marginal heading: Those who err in the faith. Marginal references: 2 Peter 2:1; Galatians 5:19.]*

If we wished to compress the diversity of those who dissent in doctrine from the orthodox, reducing it step by step to fewer people and at last to individuals, there would arise a certain infinity of errors. But neither is this our purpose, nor perhaps can it even be accomplished, so prolific and fertile is the human mind in generating these evils. We may, however, divide all these counterfeit Christians of the third class in turn into two kinds.

*[Marginal heading: The weak.]*

Some permit themselves to be taught, and although they cannot immediately be moved from their opinion, they are nevertheless so little pleased with themselves that they raise no disturbances in the Church. Others, through unconquerable obstinacy and wickedness, being (as Paul calls them) self-condemned, not only resist the truth but tear apart the peace and concord of the Church. Scripture customarily calls both groups erring, but especially calls the former weak in faith and commands that they be tolerated until the Lord reveals to them the things they do not know. *[Marginal references: Romans 14:1; 15:1; Philippians 3:15.]* The latter, however, Paul calls heretics when writing to Titus and judges that, after one and a second admonition, even a bishop should avoid them. *[Marginal reference: Titus 3:10.]*

*[Marginal heading: Heretics.]*

Since our present inquiry chiefly concerns these, we shall distinguish them from the others accurately and carefully. Thus, if someone calls heretics unbelieving, ignorant, apostate, wandering from the truth, or obstinate, he will indeed adorn them with true titles, but titles which they share with many others whose religion, life, or both are rightly condemned. This, however, is the proper and distinctive mark by which they are distinguished from the rest: they open, as it were, a school for their faithlessness, which they defend obstinately, and, so far as lies in them, either bring into the Church of God a faction devoted to a different doctrine or foster one brought in by others. For if there is merely a disagreement of minds and not of opinions, you will call such men schismatics, not heretics—although all heretics are also schismatics, that is, destroyers of ecclesiastical harmony and agreement.

These two are distinguished in the First Epistle to the Corinthians, and indeed in such a way that heresy is regarded as the graver crime. For so I interpret the passage in which the apostle writes: “I hear,” he says, “that there are schisms among you, and in part I believe it; for there must also be sects among you.” *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 11:18–19.]* It is as if the apostle were saying that he readily believes they are not of one mind, since they are liable to an even graver evil in the Church of God, namely, disagreement of opinions, which he calls heresy. Elsewhere also he plainly distinguishes *dichostasiai*, that is, dissensions, from heresies, so that *dichostasia* has precisely the same meaning as *schisma*. Add, however, what the apostle joins to it in another passage, where he does not call the heretics by name but clearly defines them. After admonishing the brothers to watch those who cause dissensions and scandals, he adds, “contrary to the doctrine which you learned.” *[Marginal references: Galatians 5:20; Romans 16:17.]* Thus, when these two things occur together, there will be heresy according to this definition of the apostle, than which we should seek no better.

Elsewhere he depicts it by exactly the same marks, as in the First Epistle to Timothy: “If anyone,” he says, “teaches a different doctrine and does not assent to sound words.” *[Marginal reference: 1 Timothy 6:3.]* You see, Bellius, that in addition to obstinacy—properly in doctrine, not in morals—a teaching of different doctrine is also required. In the Second Epistle to Timothy, speaking of the heretics Philetus and Hymenaeus, the same apostle says not only that they wandered from the truth but also that they overturn the faith of some. *[Marginal reference: 2 Timothy 2:18.]* For this reason shortly before he compared their speech to gangrene. Otherwise, to err is human; but to rend the Church after a just and repeated admonition belongs only to those whom the apostle in the same epistle calls “deceiving and being deceived,” that is, people who err and lead others into error. *[Marginal reference: 2 Timothy 3:13.]* Peter too, when designating heretics, says that unstable souls are ensnared by them; and he gives a full definition of them wholly agreeing with Paul’s: “There will be false teachers among you who will secretly bring in destructive heresies or sects.” *[Marginal references: 2 Peter 2:14; 2:1.]*

From these words, to gather the entire matter into a few words, we may establish the following: A heretic is a person who indeed uses piety as a pretext, but who, after being admonished once and again by the sound words of the Church, not only refuses to assent to them but also, by proposing false doctrine or receiving it from others, breaks the peace and agreement of the Church. The very derivation of the word declares this. *Hairesis* is a Greek term which at first was even a term of praise, so much so that Paul somewhere does not disown it. But in time, through customary usage, it began to be used for a fault, meaning a vicious doctrine or faction which someone, in contempt of the judgment of the Church of God, chooses for him-

[PDF page 8 | logical scan 7 | printed page 91]

-self to follow.

*[Marginal heading: How many kinds of heretics there are.]*

But since there is more than one kind of heretic, let us consider how they may be distinguished. I do not know whether this can be accomplished suitably enough by anyone, much less by us, since that ancient adversary of ours possesses infinite arts of doing harm and a mind most fertile in producing every error. We shall therefore make no attempt to assess the various species of heresy from their own nature and character. I say only that, although the crime of heresy cannot fail to be extremely grave, certain degrees of it can nevertheless be established from the circumstances. The man who is the leader and author of a new or renewed heresy sins more gravely than one who has been driven into some heresy by the deceit of others; and the longer the obstinacy has persisted, the more atrocious is the crime. Likewise, one who has widely disseminated a heresy through published books is held guilty of a graver crime than one who has instructed only one or two people by speech.

Next, if we consider the nature of the matter itself, although every part of the edifice of the Christian religion is not merely useful but necessary if the structure is to stand complete in all its parts, nevertheless some parts are such that even if you remove them the building does not collapse. The crime must therefore be assessed according to the character of the matter in dispute. We must also take diligent care not to consider only the particular point in which the error lies, but also everything that follows from it. For there is a certain perpetual connection of causes with one another. If anyone weighs this carefully, he will often find that what could otherwise have appeared slight or tolerable is of such importance that, unless it is met in time, it brings with it the ruin of all religion. Those who have failed to consider this, although otherwise good and pious men, have very often either fallen through imprudence into dangerous errors themselves or, through an excessive natural gentleness, opened the way to the greatest errors. But since these and many other matters are such that they cannot be comprehended in one perpetual rule, they must be considered as each case arises.

*[Marginal heading: The origin, definition, and duty of the magistrate, from the books of the philosophers.]*

Having explained these matters, come, let us proceed to the definition and division of magistracy. In establishing these things, where could I more properly begin than with that chief end which a magistrate who has resolved to discharge his office rightly ought to regard? Since every council and every legally constituted association of human beings, which we call cities, looks to this final end—that they may live as happily as possible—and since, in order to reach that end, certain laws must be enacted and certain guardians and avengers of those laws appointed, whom the Greeks call *archai* by a general term and the Latins call magistrates, it is sufficiently clear, unless I am mistaken, that a magistrate is the person who by the consent of the citizens has been declared guardian of that public peace and tranquility. That peace depends upon observance of the laws, in which the welfare of the whole city consists.

Let us next consider whether that happiness of the city, which the magistrate regards as his goal, consists only in things pertaining to the public manner of life, or also in things belonging to each person’s private duty. It is agreed that the magistrate has this purpose: to make his commonwealth as excellent as possible (for, I think, the happiest commonwealth is also the best), and the best commonwealth is the one in which the greatest number are good citizens. Hardly anyone can be a good citizen who is not also a good man. Or, even granting that someone can, no one doubts that those who are good both privately and publicly are rightly preferred to those who, although otherwise bad men, are nevertheless regarded as good citizens. Since matters stand thus, I maintain that this ought to be one of the magistrate’s chief concerns: so far as it can be accomplished by him, all his people should be not only good citizens but good men.

Again, since only the man who performs his duty is a good man, and since this duty (as even pagan philosophers hold) consists chiefly in two things—the worship of God and helping other human beings—everyone sees that the magistrate must provide for all these things to be observed rightly and in good order in the commonwealth. Thus, although the education of youth and the upright and honorable government of individual households properly belong to parents and heads of households, it is nevertheless the magistrate’s duty to ensure that everyone performs the task laid upon him, insofar as the commonwealth has an interest in it—not merely that no one misuse his own property (as the jurists say), but that he not even misuse himself. Much less can it be doubted that the care of religion and piety belongs especially to the magistrate. Unless it is observed holily and incorruptly, the commonwealth cannot escape exposure to God’s wrath and consequently to every kind of calamity; and on this even the philosophers themselves agree with us. Therefore, unless I am mistaken, we shall define the magistrate more expressly if we say that he is the person constituted in a commonwealth to preserve justice in all matters, whether public or pri-

[PDF page 9 | logical scan 8 | printed page 92]

-vate, sacred or profane, so that the citizens of the commonwealth over which he presides may live as well and happily as possible.

*[Marginal heading: The same derived from the sacred books.]*

But since we have so far argued more philosophically than Christianly, we must see how to transfer these same matters to our own order, that is, to the order of the Christian Church. I therefore say that the world was created for this reason: that the human beings placed in it might each serve the glory and majesty of God their Maker. *[Marginal reference: Proverbs 16:4.]* Thus all who, in describing duties, have set human happiness as the ultimate end have necessarily missed the mark. For we say that God must be worshiped by everyone for his own sake and that every private and public duty must be directed to his praise and glory. This world is like a single city encircled by the ocean as by walls, in which all human beings ought to have formed one commonwealth proclaiming the praises of God.

*[Marginal heading: Two cities: one of God, the other of the devil.]*

But from the time of Cain and Abel (as Augustine teaches splendidly), human beings, enticed by the flatteries of Satan, were divided into two cities directly opposed to each other. One seeks only its own advantages and relies upon its own prudence. It either utterly spurns God or, imagining him according to its own fancy, worships him with a counterfeit, invented worship. It does this either wholly as a pretense, as do those who think that religion was devised only for the sake of the common people, or—if there are any among them not quite so mad and insane—only so that they may have appeased and favorable to them the one whom they fear or from whom they expect some very great good. They are surely mad to persuade themselves that God is blinder in choosing and approving friends than human beings themselves. The other city, however, is wholly devoted to its Creator, so that it believes it should do nothing except at his word and command and directs both its public and private pursuits to magnifying his glory and praise. I did not propose to discuss the former; but we shall speak somewhat more fully of the latter, so that we may understand not only the duty of the chief magistrate but also the function of each part of that city.

*[Marginal heading: On the city of God.]*

There is therefore in the whole world only one Church, like a certain Christian city, composed of many associations of citizens. By “associations” I do not here mean different ranks of citizens, as in the one city of Rome there were senators, knights, and plebeians—although we acknowledge ranks and distinctions among citizens in the Church as well. I mean that, because it was impossible for all citizens of the Church to live in the same place, they have been distinguished into different assemblies, each of which has, besides that civil law common to the entire city, a certain municipal law of its own.

*[Marginal heading: The twofold law of the city of God, that is, of the Church.]*

By “civil law” in this context we mean first the written Word of God, which all societies of the Christian commonwealth or Church obey equally; and then what jurists call the law of nations, that is, certain laws arising from common notions by which all societies of the human race are held together. “Municipal law” consists partly of ceremonies pertaining to the outward polity of religion, which must vary according to places, times, and persons, and partly of the laws peculiar to individual cities which concern civil life and the mutual dealings of human beings. Thus both laws, civil and municipal, are partly divine—that is, pertaining to the worship of God and conscience—and partly concerned with human affairs and outward life.

Since, moreover, citizens must first of all assent to these laws, which they cannot do unless they first understand what the laws are, and since one task (as someone rightly said) is best accomplished by one person, the Lord our God, who founded this Church, wished first of all that duly appointed interpreters be established for that civil law which concerns conscience and was instituted by him. In the individual assemblies these interpreters were to lead the consciences of the citizens, partly by voice, example, and prayer, to know and approve those laws; and partly by teaching and certain restraints, whose form he himself established, to keep them in duty according to the judgment of the ecclesiastical senate.

*[Marginal heading: Ecclesiastical jurisdiction.]*

Then, since laws are written in vain if there are no guardians and avengers of them, the same God Most Good and Most Great willed that in every human community certain people should appear as a kind of image of his highest and supreme power. Speaking of these, he says, “I have said, You are gods.” *[Marginal heading: Civil jurisdiction. Marginal reference: Psalm 82:6.]* For why should you not call him a kind of second god, when everyone living in a commonwealth must be subject to him not only because of wrath but also because of conscience, and when he has received from God the power of life and death? Yet here too a distinction must be applied, so that we understand the boundaries within which the power of magistrates must be confined. Although it is the highest power on earth, it is by no means infinite; nor, when this right was divinely entrusted to them, was this done as though God were yielding the government and administration of the earth—

[PDF page 10 | logical scan 9 | printed page 93]

—or rather so that he himself, whose splendor and majesty human beings cannot bear, might reign among us in the person of human beings. Accordingly, since human life is, as it were, twofold—one part wholly outward and civil, the other inward and concerning conscience—God must be the leader and governor of both. As regards the former part, God wished, on the one hand, that remnants of that original light should survive in human minds and shine before them so that they might pass this life peacefully. On the other hand, lest anyone attribute this whole function to the common nature of humanity, he has been accustomed to adorn certain people with special gifts, intending to use them as instruments for governing the rest. *[Marginal heading: Human laws.]*

Their function, therefore, is to enact political laws, that is, laws pertaining to outward life, yet with such moderation that they establish nothing contrary to that divine civil law which holds the chief place throughout the Church. Otherwise the magistrate is a tyrant and abuses the power granted him by God. But in matters that concern both the worship of God and the mutual charity of human beings, the competence of magistrates, whatever their rank, does not extend so far. For God has given no human being power to enact laws for conscience, nor can he endure anyone but himself alone to rule human minds.

*[Marginal heading: Divine law.]*

I therefore maintain that the divine laws—or that law which is at once civil and purely divine—were first delivered orally and by entirely unambiguous oracles to the ancient fathers, and then, by God’s command and through the work of certain people, written in the books of both Testaments. This is the law according to whose prescription our one Lawgiver will judge the whole world. Next I maintain that absolutely no other laws are to be imposed upon human minds and that no one can add anything to or subtract anything from them without sacrilege and profane audacity, except insofar as they are political or ceremonial, for in this respect they were prescribed only to one nation and only for a time.

I nevertheless acknowledge that, just as in writing those laws, so also in explaining them, adapting them to human use, and indeed vindicating their authority and dignity, God has from the beginning been accustomed to use human ministry. Yet these tasks are distinguished in such a way that the first two belong to those whom we showed a little earlier to have been specially appointed by God to this ministry, while the third is entrusted to magistrates. Thus magistrates are to protect both the ministers of the Word themselves and other pious people with suitable safeguards; ensure that the doctrine of the Word is faithfully expounded and, once expounded, observed by all their people; confirm by their authority the outward ceremonies established by the counsel of the Church according to times and places; and finally restrain, according to the magnitude of their crime, bad citizens who resist ecclesiastical discipline and disturb the concord and peace of the Church.

Therefore, to gather the whole matter into a few words, magistrates are established in the individual assemblies or commonwealths of the Christian Church as vicars of God, to lead the rest entrusted to their care in proclaiming God’s glory and praise. To accomplish this, they ought chiefly to ensure that everything pertaining to the worship of God is not only established holily and religiously from his one Word, but also preserved. They must then either enact the most equitable and just civil laws—that is, laws not at variance with God’s Word—or defend those enacted by others, and administer both public and private affairs according to them.

In the first part of this jurisdiction, however, the ecclesiastical office must be carefully distinguished from the magistrate’s authority. Princes must understand that, just as in matters pertaining to outward polity they have all their people, with absolutely no exception, subject and answerable to their power, so with respect to spiritual jurisdiction they themselves are bound to God’s Word in two ways. First, they too must order their lives according to its prescription. Second, whenever the public tranquility of the Church demands it, they must by every means protect the Word itself, its interpreters, and the whole ecclesiastical discipline against the audacity and wickedness of unbelievers, the scandalous, heretics, and every enemy.

It remains to confirm from suitable testimonies of Scripture that matters stand as I say. As for civil polity, since this part does not belong to the proposed question, we shall make no effort to prove it. In the cause of religion, however, because some deny that any authority has been granted by God to the political magistrate, I shall produce only two passages from Paul, from which it is clear that the magistrate’s rule extends much farther than they think; then we shall approach the question itself. Paul testifies that the magistrate is God’s minister, bearing the sword to avenge those who do evil. *[Marginal reference: Romans 13:4.]* Therefore, if the magistrate has no authority over heretics, one of two conclusions is necessary: either they do no evil—which, even if Bellius’s faction denies it, nevertheless does not, I think, require

[PDF page 11 | logical scan 10 | printed page 94]

refutation—or Paul’s general statement must be restricted to a particular class of crimes, namely, those which they call bodily sins. I hope to discuss this distinction among crimes more fully in its proper place. For now I shall use Paul to refute this exception. In the First Epistle to Timothy he says: “I exhort that prayers be made for kings and all who are in high position, so that we may lead a peaceful and quiet life in all piety and dignity.” *[Marginal reference: 1 Timothy 2:1–2.]* Here we hear expressly that magistrates were instituted to keep their peaceful citizens not only in serious and honorable conduct—that is, in matters pertaining to the mutual duties of human beings—but also in piety and the worship of God.

From this I conclude, with that most learned man Dr. Philip Melanchthon, that magistrates have been appointed by God as guardians and avengers not only of the second table, but also, and chiefly, of pure religion insofar as outward discipline is concerned. *[Marginal reference: Melanchthon, little book on the duty of princes.]* I say “outward discipline.” As for the explanation of doctrine itself and the whole ecclesiastical jurisdiction, so far are we from wrenching it from the Church and transferring it to the political magistrate that, on the contrary, we affirm that God has imposed upon the magistrate chiefly the care of protecting and preserving it.

*[Marginal heading: First part of this treatise: Whether heretics ought to be punished.]*

Since this is so, I think it is now easy to judge that part of the question: whether Bellius’s indefinite and general assertion that the punishment of heretics does not belong to the magistrate is true. But lest he complain that we have passed over any arguments, I have resolved first to refute their arguments—not confusedly and disorderly, but in order and one by one, with an answer fitted to each part of the proposed question—and then at last to confirm our own opinion in the same order.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation of the arguments by which our adversaries attempt to prove that heretics ought not to be punished.]*

They will not obtain from me what they nevertheless hoped would happen: that I would undertake a quarrel with the very men whose authority they have abused in order to dazzle the eyes of the simple. Rather I say, Bellius, that you shamelessly accuse the most renowned and holy magistrates and ministers of God’s Word, distinguished for piety and learning, as cruel, savage, and monstrous. Since you cannot do this without the gravest injury to those under whose name you hide as under the shield of Ajax, when I examine their arguments and soften them by a true and suitable interpretation, so far am I from despising or reproving the men to whom I certainly attribute far more than you do that I instead defend and protect their reputation and honor, which you have craftily violated.

Yet, Bellius, even if I adopted this manner of writing so as to show openly but modestly, and by producing true and solid arguments from God’s Word, that I dissent from them, and then opposed many more authorities to the authority of two or three, do you think that the very men with whom you are trying to embroil us would condemn what I have done? Would they not rather, in their extraordinary modesty and piety, yield to the truth? But let us come to the matter.

*[Marginal heading: Martin Luther.]*

You immediately place in your front rank against us Master Luther, that most courageous hero. If he were alive, good God, how would he receive you Academics, who keep saying that even Turks and Jews believe in God and who hold that controversies about religion cannot be decided from Scripture? It is agreed that, shortly before the publication of this book from which you collected certain things, Luther translated the New Testament into German. When Duke George of Saxony and several other princes prohibited it from being read, Luther, moved by the injustice of the deed, wrote the little book in which he showed that both the so-called secular magistrates and tyrannical pseudo-bishops customarily sin gravely in the cause of religion, for neither has the right to enact laws for conscience. Thus the former acted wrongly because (as he says) they wished to punish faith and the Word of God and, employing threats, compel their people to believe this or that at the decision of others. The latter reigned no less tyrannically in the Church, because they did not cut down and burn up heresies by the Word of God, but attacked God’s truth with naked and open force. The whole little book demonstrates that this is the sum of Luther’s opinions and indeed that these are his own words; and we think no differently.

For when we judge that heretics ought to be punished by the magistrate, we do not oppose Luther. We neither rob the Church of her own right, whose peaceful and moderate inquiry and discipline we wish to precede any action, nor confuse jurisdictions, nor remove distinctions of times, places, and persons. Instead, we say that this is the magistrate’s duty: not to compel faith (which not even ministers of the Word can accomplish), but to provide that faithless and desperate apostates do not disturb the peace of the Church and tear apart the agreement of piety; and also to punish dreadful blasphemies. For although an opinion of the mind is not to be punished (as someone rightly said),

[PDF page 12 | logical scan 11 | printed page 95]

a pestilential and impious profession must by no means be tolerated.

*[Marginal heading: Johannes Brenz.]*

I come to another man, whose opinion that Anabaptists ought not to be killed you have not only copied but repeatedly pressed in many places. By God’s grace he is still alive. I know him to be too pious to assent to your faction, and I do not doubt that he is too modest to oppose his opinion about punishing Anabaptists to the agreement of so many churches in the way you do. It is clear that he deals only with a heresy so bare that it does not break out into any evil and harmful deed, does not overturn religion, and finally deserves the name of error rather than heresy. You could have gathered that this is so even from his most recent book, in which he writes verbatim: “Let magistrates strive vigorously that all may live peaceably, honorably” (and I add from Paul, piously) “and harmoniously. For let anyone who sins against the common tranquility and the magistrate, whether he is a baptizer or a rebaptizer, know that he must suffer from him the punishment he deserves.” So he writes.

Far be it also from us to arm the magistrate against those who sin through simplicity rather than wickedness, without the ruin of others and notorious blasphemy, or who dissent from the received opinion in explaining some passage of Scripture. We are not so foolish, Bellius, nor have we cast off every feeling of humanity. Indeed, even in contagious and deadly diseases we wish every milder remedy first to be tried and everything to be directed to God’s glory and love of neighbor. You will prove by competent witnesses that we have never acted otherwise.

*[Marginal heading: Erasmus.]*

I proceed to Erasmus, from whom I freely profess that I dissent on very many points. I cannot discover what in him I should chiefly follow, for he is so changeable that it is sufficiently evident that he preferred to conceal rather than to state what he believed. Yet you, Bellius, oppose him to us, so that by his prejudgment so many holy churches may be accused of cruelty! But even if we accept him to some extent as a judge, what can you gather from his writings except that trials of heretics are conducted wrongly and cruelly by the papists, because the office of bishops is not to burn and destroy, but to teach and heal? “But what prevents,” he says, “a prince from removing heretics who disturb the public tranquility?” And a little later: “I by no means say these things in favor of heretics, nor will anyone have me as a milder advocate, provided I recognize a true heretic—that is, one who errs maliciously, is factious, and cannot be healed.” When you set Erasmus against us, therefore, who would not think that you are betraying your own case?

*[Marginal heading: John Calvin.]*

I come to Calvin, whose name you thought fit to employ for many reasons which I shall expose. You could have learned his opinion not only from his excellent book written against Servetus, but even from his commentaries on the First Epistle to Timothy and the Epistle to Titus, had you not habitually despised all these things in comparison with your own. Nor were you ignorant of it. From the day Servetus was cast into prison, you never ceased reproving that deed in such a way as to direct by your slanders the entire odium of the affair upon Calvin alone. Indeed, this very miscellany of yours was collected by you and your Basilius Montfortius for the purpose of casting out of the Church of God the two most faithful pastors of two very celebrated churches, Zurich and Geneva. I shall make this clear even from Basilius’s refutation.

Yet because you were afraid to act openly, and thought that your defense of that most cruel clemency had to be undertaken in such a way that it could not expose you to harm, you not only concealed your true names in this miscellany and in the letters by which you recently attempted to excite a deadly sedition in the Genevan church; you also believed that your plan could be concealed if Calvin’s name appeared among the other men on whose authority you rely, or at least that by these arts you would cause him to be accused before everyone of manifest fickleness. But this device is too crude not to be readily noticed, and the slander too open to require a more elaborate response. From the things you thought should be selected from his writings you can gather this one conclusion, which especially tells against you: that the man whom in your little dens you habitually call bloodthirsty is as far removed as possible from all violence and cruelty. You should therefore understand that whatever he wrote against papal tyranny was also spoken against your faction, since the most cruel people of all are those who think wolves should be spared so that they may assail Christ’s flock with impunity.

*[Marginal heading: Lactantius.]*

I come to the ancients—for I shall speak elsewhere of other recent writers—Lactantius, Chrysostom, Jerome, Augustine, and Hilary. Among these, Lactantius pleads a different cause, inveighing against those who attacked the Christian religion with sword and fire.

[PDF page 13 | logical scan 12 | printed page 96]

*[Marginal headings: Chrysostom. Jerome. Augustine.]*

Chrysostom does not forbid heretics to be punished by the magistrate, but forbids them to be rooted out; and he relies only upon the single parable of the tares, which we shall discuss more fully elsewhere. Jerome goes even further, as we shall show. As regards the first two parts of the proposed question, Augustine supports me so fully that I could dissolve all your arguments with his words.

*[Marginal heading: Hilary.]*

Hilary rebukes the bishops of his time because, forgetting their own office, they fought against heresies with the weapons of princes alone; and, intent upon the one matter he was then treating, he was carried somewhat too far by his zeal for piety. Thus, whether we ask if heretics ought to be punished or if they ought to be punished by the magistrate, there is no reason for you to oppose those fathers to us—especially Augustine, from whom you have excerpted only the passages in which that good man, a person of the most moderate temper, exhorts Caesar’s prefects to preserve clemency. Many things which the same man truly and piously argued against the Donatists, who were pleading this very cause with you, you have partly passed over craftily and partly left to your colleague Basilius to touch upon in a few words. We shall expose this fraud in the proper places.

*[Marginal heading: Those who punish heretics are not cruel; rather, those who protect them are.]*

But come now, Bellius, let us close hand to hand with you and your faction. In this contest we shall set out each of your arguments in its naked form so that even children can understand what force it possesses. I wish, however, first to warn the reader about your customary prefatory claim: that blood is opposed by us and defended by you. In this you act not only slanderously but against your own conscience. You know who we are; and not only the present age but posterity will judge how we are disposed toward the Church.

I ask, what kind of clemency is this—to caress the most savage wolves so that they may tear apart with impunity the innocent sheep redeemed by Christ’s blood? What kind of patience is it to permit the Lord’s vineyard to be devastated by the most monstrous creatures? What kindness is it to look on indifferently while the name of the Lord our God is assailed with every insult? What then? Are these things not done by those to whom the name “heretic” applies? Are they not the very men of whom the apostle says that savage wolves will enter, not sparing the flock, and that men speaking perverse things will arise to draw disciples after themselves? *[Marginal reference: Acts 20:29–30.]*

If perhaps you answer that you do not favor heretics but exhort everyone to preserve moderation, tell us, I ask, within what boundaries you define that clemency. If you do not wish people who are wholly unbelieving, criminal in conduct, weak in faith, or erring through simplicity rather than wickedness and without the ruin of others to be regarded as heretics, we certainly agree with you. If you wish distinctions to be made even among heretics according to matters, times, places, and persons; the cause to be duly and peacefully heard by the Church from the pure Word of God; ecclesiastical discipline to be applied first; and even the magistrate first to try every milder remedy, know that we do not disagree with you. For not even you can rightly deny that we teach this both orally and in writing and that, so far as it has been possible, this has until now been observed in our churches.

*[Marginal heading: First argument.]*

The first argument by which our adversaries attempt to prove that heretics ought not to be punished: All the matters customarily disputed are not so necessary to know; they cannot be known except by those who are pure in heart; and, if they are known, they do not make a person better.

*[Marginal heading: A false description of the Christian religion.]*

This, then, is the foundation of your opinion: you locate the Christian religion in an innocent life, and the innocence of life—which you call the white garment—only in the mutual duties of human beings toward one another, doing so philosophically rather than Christianly. The first and chief thing, the worship of God, you sometimes entirely omit from your description of that white garment. At other times you will have it consist only in that knowledge of God which certainly cannot be taken away even from the devils. But that other knowledge which is peculiar to the elect and which we call faith—against which the devil has strained every sinew from the beginning of the world—you reject not only as entirely unnecessary but even as the parent of pride and cruelty.

What is the purpose of this? It is this: once you have persuaded people that the matters which have so long been disputed among Christians, and about which inquiry will not cease until the Lord Jesus has put all things beneath his feet, are of little necessity for salvation, it may then follow that those hitherto regarded as heretics are not only not to be punished severely but are to be tolerated with impunity. For you wish anyone who says with Jews or Turks that he believes in God, and furthermore that Christ is the Son of God and the lord and judge of the world, and who orders his life so that he is neither thief, adulterer, murderer, nor polluted by some such crime about which no disputations or books are produced, to be able beneath the covering of that white garment to conceal every mon-

*[Marginal heading: What faith is according to our adversaries.]*

[PDF page 14 | logical scan 13 | printed page 97]

-strosity of error and to think, write, and teach whatever he pleases about the remaining matters—that is, about the whole Gospel. That this is your opinion is plainly established from your own words. I have copied them in the utmost good faith so that everyone may understand that I invent nothing here.

Recognize, therefore, Bellius, how you define the Christian life: “Christ,” you say, “is the prince of the world, and when departing from the earth he foretold to human beings that he would return at an uncertain day and hour. He commanded them to prepare white garments for his coming, that is, to live piously and amicably without contentions and to love one another.” Entirely correct—if you would explain what it is to live piously. Yet elsewhere I see you depict your white garment somewhat differently. For a little later you wrote: “If meanwhile there is anyone who endeavors to prepare for himself a white garment, that is, to live innocently.” What follows from this, then? Plainly that there are two marks of Christian, that is, true religion: piety and innocence of life.

Let us now hear what you call piety. “The dispute,” you say, “is not about the way by which one can come to Christ, that is, about correction of life, but about Christ’s own state and office: where Christ himself is, what he does, how he sits at the Father’s right hand, and how he is one with the Father; likewise about the Trinity, predestination, free will, God, angels, the state of souls after this life, and other matters of this kind. These are not so necessary to know in order to obtain salvation through faith (since tax collectors and prostitutes were saved without knowing them); nor can they be known before we have a pure heart (since to see these things is to see God himself, who cannot be seen without a pure heart, according to the saying, ‘Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God’); nor, if known, do they make a person better, since Paul said, ‘If I know all mysteries and have not charity, I am nothing.’” Elsewhere he likewise says: “To believe in God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit and to approve the precepts of piety contained in the Holy Scriptures is a golden coin, more approved and tested than gold itself. Yet up to now this coin has borne different images while people disagree among themselves about the Supper, Baptism, and other matters of this kind.”

That you require no knowledge of God in Christians beyond that which they share partly with Jews and Turks (as you at least maintain) and partly with the devils themselves is also clear from other passages. “There was once,” you say, “among almost every nation an opinion that there are many gods. Christ removed this error by his coming, so that now neither Turks nor other nations doubt that there is one God, and in this all agree with Christians. If anyone denies God, he is impious and atheistic and deservedly abominable by every judgment. The Turks also possess this: they believe in the God of whom Moses wrote, and in this they agree without controversy with both Jews and Christians.”

I come to the knowledge of Christ, which you distinguish from the knowledge of God. You wish it to be partly common to Christians and Turks, as did that fanatic Postel, and partly peculiar to Christians. For you say verbatim: “The Turks too attribute much to Christ, in which they surpass the Jews, and they have this in common with Christians.” What knowledge of Christ you make peculiar to Christians appears partly from the impression upon that golden coin of yours and partly from what immediately follows. For you speak thus: “Christians surpass all others in this: that they believe Jesus Christ to be the Son of God and the lord and judge of the world; and this is common to all Christians.”

For you, therefore, the sum of piety is constituted, as it were, in three steps. The first is to confess with all nations that there is one God. The second is to acknowledge with Turks and Jews that one God of whom Moses wrote. The third is to believe that Jesus Christ is the Son of God and the lord and judge of the world. To these you stitch the observance of the second table. In this division of piety, however, we say not only that the chief things are omitted—namely, the whole ministry of the Gospel—but also that very many things are altogether foreign.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

For if you regard as a Christian, that is, as a pious and religious man, anyone who confesses those three things, what prevents us from confessing that the devils are the most Christian of all? They certainly doubt far less than human beings that there is one God, and that he is the God of whom Moses wrote. With Muhammad they profess Christ not only to be an excellent prophet, spirit, word, and power of God, but they also cry out that he is the Son of God and consequently the lord and judge of the world. For they add this too: “Why have you come to torment us before the time?” They believe all these things, I say, and tremble. *[Marginal references: Article 46 of Muhammadan theology; Matthew 8:29; James 2:19.]*

Are they then pious and religious? Not at all, you will say, for in addition I require an innocent and blameless life—that is, approval of the precepts of piety found in Scripture. But we wish, Bellius, that you had defined what those precepts of piety are. You seem, however, to have closed your own way to teaching this when you said by name that the chief heads of piety are neither necessary for obtaining salvation through faith nor, if known, make a person better. From this it surely appears that you locate Christian faith in historical knowledge and measure innocence of life either from the precepts of philosophy or at least from observance only of the second table—

[PDF page 15 | logical scan 14 | printed page 98]

—as it was explained by Christ, the best interpreter. You do both wrongly. Christian faith is far different from what you suppose; and philosophy has not explained what the chief virtues are—namely, faith, invocation, and charity, not just any charity but that which rests upon God’s Word—nor from where they are to be sought or to what end they are to be directed. Moreover, unless observance of the second table proceeds from a mind sanctified through Christ apprehended by faith, it can bring nothing but sin and death, of which the law separated from Christ is the minister; for whoever does not believe in the Son will not see life, but the wrath of God remains upon him. *[Marginal reference: John 3:36.]*

If you locate the worship of God in that bare knowledge of Christ, who does not see that the devils surpass Christian human beings in the chief part of religion by as much as they possess a more excellent knowledge of God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit? Since this is utterly false and absurd, your principles too must totter and waver. But far from us be these monstrosities of error, for we have learned to define the Christian religion very differently. You are mistaken, Bellius, deeply mistaken: first, because you confuse God with an idol, that is, truth with falsehood; second, because you omit the definition of true, that is, Christian faith; and finally, because in describing innocence of life you have omitted the very thing without which a good work cannot rightly be distinguished from sin.

*[Marginal heading: How God is distinguished from an idol.]*

As for the first point, although a certain sense of divinity is engraved by nature upon every mind, I maintain that whatever this is, it is so thin and weak that, unless something else comes to it from outside, it leads even the wisest people not to knowledge of the true God but to the manufacture of an idol. Besides the teaching of experience itself, we shall best recognize, unless I am mistaken, that this is so in the following way. All who have established a manner of worshiping God—and it is agreed that there have always been many such people, not merely in individual regions but even in individual cities—have necessarily regarded God’s nature as their goal. They taught that God must be worshiped in the way they thought would please him, that is, in a way consistent with his nature. Thus each followed a manner of worshiping God corresponding to the opinion of God which he held.

Among people who do not draw wisdom from God’s Word there have always been, and still today are, infinite forms of worshiping God. Since there is a perpetual correspondence between the worship of God and one’s persuasion about God, it follows that the opinions of peoples about God are no less diverse. Yet there is one God, and consequently he approves one form of religion, namely, the one he himself prescribed. There must therefore be at least as many idols as there are forms of religion devised by human beings. Among these I number the Jewish religion also—I mean the one the Jews now practice. It follows that all who have turned aside from that true and evangelical worship of God adore not the true God but an idol.

Far be it from us, therefore, to say that Christians worship no other God than the one worshiped by Jews and Turks. We prefer to agree with Paul, who, writing to the Galatians, testifies that they did not know God, calls the Ephesians “without God,” and says to the Corinthians, “The world through wisdom did not know God.” David likewise says, “The gods of the nations are demons.” *[Marginal references: Galatians 4:8; Ephesians 2:12; 1 Corinthians 1:21; Psalm 96:5.]* These statements do not conflict with what is said in Romans 2, where the apostle, just as he commends the testimony about God visible in the works of nature, in turn laments the miserable corruption not of one or two persons but of the entire human race. He thereby shows what we affirmed shortly before: the obscure knowledge of God in our corrupted nature has this much force in us, that we can offer no adequate excuse, but not so much that by following it as a guide we can penetrate to the truth of that divine light and understand, with the aid of nature or from contemplating the world, who and what God is and how he must be worshiped.

If you think these passages which I have cited from Paul do not pertain to the present matter because the people Paul addresses once worshiped many gods, I answer that they are censured not only because they had many gods but because they did not know the true God. Augustine, from Varro and many others, teaches that they worshiped one particular god under many names—that is, an idol whose nature they measured by their own fancy. Yet Christ, speaking of the Samaritans, who nevertheless boasted that they worshiped the God of Abraham, says, “You worship what you do not know.” Indeed, he says even of the Jews themselves: “If you had believed Moses, you would have believed me also”; and elsewhere, “Whoever denies the Son does not have the Father either.” *[Marginal references: John 4:22; 5:46; 1 John 2:23.]*

*[Marginal heading: What the faith of Christians is.]*

With what shamelessness, then, have you undertaken to teach us what true religion is when you have not even learned to distinguish an idol rightly from God? Come now: how do you define Christian faith? As that by which we believe Jesus Christ to be the Son of God and lord and judge of the world. For this is that golden coin of yours. But what are we to think of the rest—to name a few of many—Christ’s nature, state, and office, the Trinity, and justification by faith?

[PDF page 16 | logical scan 15 | printed page 99]

what will be taught in the Church? What will believers think? Whatever they please. For this coin bears different images—that is, as Muhammad says, “The law or faith of all is indeed one, but the rites of different peoples are naturally different.” *[Marginal reference: Second article of Muhammadan theology.]* Do you hear this blasphemy, kings, princes, and magistrates?

The knowledge which these men require in Christians—namely, that by which, without understanding Christ’s state and office, we believe him to be the Son of God and lord and judge of the world—every believer knows can produce nothing in us but despair, unless we know the promises, that is, Christ’s own state and office, in such a way that we embrace them as our own particular possession. For this is the sum of the Gospel, which is the power of God for salvation to everyone who believes: to apprehend Christ as made by God our wisdom, righteousness, sanctification, and redemption. Thus must be understood what Scripture often says: to believe that Jesus Christ is the Son of God and to confess that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh—namely, with the faith which embraces the testimony that God has given us eternal life and that this life is in his Son. *[Marginal references: Romans 1:16; 1 Corinthians 1:30; Matthew 16:16; Acts 8:37; 1 John 4:2; 5:10–12.]* For only then does a person believe that Jesus is the Christ: when, taught by the Holy Spirit, he firmly determines that everything freely promised to us by God through the Messiah belongs properly and particularly to him. Hope and charity necessarily follow that faith.

So that their opinion may be declared as openly as possible, we shall examine even each of their statements, since this is necessary. “The dispute,” they say, “has not been about the way by which one can come to Christ, that is, about correction of life, but about Christ’s own state and office.” I ask, to say nothing here about this way by which one comes to Christ, how am I finally to understand that Christ is the Son of God and lord and judge of the world if it is not necessary to inquire into his state and office? These things are so joined that they cannot be torn apart.
