But even if they could, what benefit is it to know Christ as judge of the world unless I also know whom in general he will acquit (for I do not speak of individuals), whom he will condemn, and by what means I can avoid his wrath and gain his favor?

“Correct your life,” you will say, for this is the one way by which one comes to Christ. What if I reply with Paul that I am sold under sin, that when the commandment came sin revived, and that what was designed for life, because of the weakness of the flesh, results in death for me? What if I go further and say that I do not do the good I will, but the evil I do not will—although this is the voice only of believers and the regenerate? *[Marginal reference: Romans 7:7–24.]* If I stop here, will I not either attempt everything in vain or be driven to despair? Christians therefore follow an altogether different way and find in Christ’s state and office the one remedy against these evils.

For we say that just as there is one way by which one comes to God, namely Christ himself, so there is one path that leads to Christ, namely faith. *[Marginal reference: John 14:6.]* This faith embraces all those doctrines which you reject as unnecessary in such a way that all who are truly faithful not only assent to them as true—which even the devils do—but also conclude from the testimony of the Holy Spirit and of conscience that God’s promises belong properly and particularly to them. That is, they believe not only that Christ is the Son of God, coeternal and consubstantial with the Father, and all the other things narrated historically in both Testaments concerning the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, but also that he is God for them. They believe Christ to be not only the judge of the world but their own advocate and reconciler; I say, they acknowledge by the faith freely given them by God that Christ was made for them by God the Father wisdom, righteousness, sanctification, and redemption. *[Marginal heading: Christians must necessarily know what Christ’s state is and what his office is.]*

But let us hear why Bellius does not think the question concerning Christ’s state and office so necessary for obtaining salvation through faith. “Because,” he says, “tax collectors and prostitutes were saved without this knowledge.” Really? Were sinners saved through one whom they did not invoke? Did they invoke one in whom they did not believe? Did they believe in one whom they had not first known? Did they know him, I say, not only as the Son of God and lord and judge of the world, but as one freely given by God particularly to them, so that because they believe in him they should not perish but have eternal life? *[Marginal references: Romans 10:14; John 3:16.]*

This office of Christ is so repeatedly taught on every page of Scripture, so clearly, fully, and lucidly explained in each of its parts, that anyone who says its knowledge is not necessary for obtaining salvation through faith, or that if known it does not make people better, does not know the matter and substance of faith and may rightly seem to accuse the Holy Spirit of being garrulous and tiresome. Since this cannot even be contemplated without horror, what judgment must finally be made of you who dare to affirm it even in published books? You do not wish it to be known where Christ is, what he does, and how he sits at the Father’s right hand. *[Marginal heading: Where he is, what he does, and how he sits at the Father’s right hand.]* In vain, therefore,

[PDF page 17 | logical scan 16 | printed page 100]

the angels testify that he ascended into heaven and will descend in the same way. In vain it is written that we have a great High Priest who passed through the heavens, above every principality, power, might, dominion, and every name that is named. In vain we are commanded to lift our eyes to heaven. *[Marginal references: Acts 1:11; Hebrews 4:14; 7:26; Ephesians 1:21.]* On the contrary, our consolation rests upon this very fact: he ascended to the Father in heaven so that he might open our access to the Father, so that we might sit with him in the heavenly places, so that he might give gifts to human beings and the pledge of our inheritance, and so that we might know that he is there interceding for us with the Father, made higher than the heavens. *[Marginal references: Ephesians 2:6; John 14:2–3; Ephesians 4:8; Hebrews 7:25–26.]*

*[Marginal heading: Knowledge of the Trinity is the foundation of the Christian religion.]*

But you also make the very knowledge of the Trinity useless, so that nothing at all may remain safe in the Christian religion. Thus, if someone compares you with Arius, Sabellius, Aëtius, Eunomius, Apollinaris, Mani, Marcion, Eutyches, Macedonius, Paul of Samosata, Nestorius, Servetus, and the other monsters of this kind, does he not wrong them? Although they profaned the inviolable mystery of the Trinity in infinite ways, they nevertheless did not advance to such shamelessness as to place knowledge of the Trinity among things which are unnecessary and which, if known, do not make a person better.

Are we really to judge that you fathers, who for so many years steadfastly maintained the mystery of the Trinity against the savagery of so many kings, princes, and heretics—not only by words and speech, but by so many books which will never perish, by continual labors and every kind of calamity, and finally by the shedding of your own blood—were neither prudent nor courageous, but ignorant and obstinate in a cause of little necessity? I ask you, Athanasius, why did you wander so long throughout the entire world? Why did you compose that creed of yours of marvelous brevity, if, in order to refute heretics, we may not show from what passages of Scripture we gather that Christ is one with the Father, that three hypostases are distinguished in one essence, and that true God and true man are neither mixed nor confused in the one Christ, the Son of God, but so united that one Christ exists?

Would that people were content with simple assent, so that good men were not compelled to debate these things more subtly. But since Satan, using the curiosity of certain people, has devised almost infinite arguments by which partly to establish his errors and partly to overturn God’s Word, are these things to be tolerated? Should believers not rather be confirmed by the true and genuine interpretation of God’s Word? *[Marginal heading: What compelled Christians to debate the Trinity.]* You, however, not only do not wish these things to be investigated, but even affirm that, if known, they do not make a person better. O blasphemy!

Then let it be erased—let that most divine prologue of Saint John be erased—in which the Son of God’s consubstantiality and distinct hypostasis, and the union of the divine nature with the human in one Christ, are declared so clearly and plainly. Let the baptismal formula in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit be erased, although it was confirmed in Christ’s baptism by the testimony even of the outward senses. *[Marginal references: Matthew 28:19; 3:17.]* For the Father’s voice was heard; Christ was declared by the Father’s voice to be true God and true man; and the Holy Spirit was seen by human eyes in the form of a dove. If these men are to be believed, none of these things was necessary for our salvation. Let that divine opening of the Epistle to the Romans be erased, in which the Son is so clearly distinguished from the Father and both natures in Christ are explained.

If these are erased, where is our faith? Where is our consolation? Unless the hypostases of Father and Son are distinguished, shall I believe that I have been reconciled to the Father through the Son? Unless Christ is true God, and therefore coeternal and consubstantial with the Father, is he Savior? Sanctifier? Victor over sin, death, and the devil? Unless he is true man in everything except sin, is he the mediator who was born, suffered, was buried, raised, and returned to heaven so that in our flesh he might see the possession of heaven acquired for believers? Is he our brother, flesh of our flesh and bone of our bones? Is he a faithful High Priest able to help those who are tempted? *[Marginal references: 2 Timothy 3:5; Hebrews 4:15; 5:9–10; 2:17–18.]* If there are two Christs, is there one Advocate for us? Is he himself our righteousness and sanctification? If the natures are confused, could he thirst, hunger, and die for us? *[Marginal references: 1 John 2:1; 1 Corinthians 1:30; John 19:28; 4:6.]*

Again, unless the Holy Spirit is distinguished from both, does the Son ask the Father to give us the Comforter? Does the Son send us that same Spirit of truth who also receives from the Son? And unless these things are so, through whom shall we know that we are children of God? Shall we be God’s temple? Where is the pledge of our inheritance? Where is consolation in afflictions? Who will stir up those inexpressible groanings in us? Who will cry, “Abba, Father”? Will Christ sanctify us? Will the Father and Son make their dwelling with us? Finally, shall we have the mind of Christ? If we did not believe these things, what would be more wretched or more calamitous than the Christian people? *[Marginal references: John 14:16–17; Romans 8:16; 1 Corinthians 6:19; Ephesians 1:14; Galatians 4:6; John 14:23; 1 Corinthians 2:16.]*

*[Marginal heading: The question of predestination and free will is very necessary.]*

What more? You also make the question of predestination and free will empty, except for producing pride and cruelty. Yet the one source of true humility here

[PDF page 18 | logical scan 17 | printed page 101]

is to know that, as regards salvation, you are nothing, will nothing, and can do nothing by yourself; and that if you have anything, you have only what you freely received from him who gives us both to will and to do. *[Marginal references: 1 Corinthians 4:7; Philippians 2:13.]* Our whole hope in afflictions rests upon God’s free and immutable election. We do not seek this in God’s secret and inscrutable judgment, but infer it *a posteriori*, that is, from faith, which is the effectual working of calling in us. In turn, we recognize faith partly from the testimony of a good conscience and partly from its effects, through that Spirit by whom the children of God are led. *[Marginal references: Romans 8:28; John 2:19; 1 Peter 3:9.]* Therefore on almost every page of the doctrine of justification by faith, that is, of the Gospel, Paul lays beneath it as its base and foundation God’s eternal and free purpose and good pleasure. *[Marginal references: Romans 8:28; Ephesians 1:4; 1 Peter 1:2; 2 Timothy 1:9.]* Yet you dare to say that this doctrine on which we stand is a path to pride and cruelty?

I certainly acknowledge that bare knowledge of these matters, unless applied through faith, is to us an aroma of death unto death. I also acknowledge that the greatest modesty and sobriety must be used in all mysteries of the Christian religion, and especially in this greatest mystery, the one most remote from human senses. But what sobriety is it to criticize God’s Spirit for teaching useless matters? What humility is it, when the Spirit of God speaking through the apostle takes you as if by the hand and leads you to the point that you are compelled to exclaim, “O man, who are you to answer back to God?” *[Marginal reference: Romans 9:20.]* nevertheless to resist and to accuse of curiosity and rashness those who do not step outside God’s Word, but follow its footprints with fear and trembling?

Come now: how will you finally explain free justification? How will we answer Pelagians, papists, Anabaptists, and the other heretics who teach works unless we teach what free agency is, what necessity is, what compulsion is, what human corruption is, and what our powers and faculties are? Finally, how shall we know God’s goodness unless we seriously know the magnitude of our own vice?

*[Marginal heading: To what extent questions about God and angels are necessary.]*

You also place, Bellius, knowledge about God and angels in the same list. I confess that I cannot see how this agrees with what you said about that golden coin. If you were censuring human curiosity in treating questions of this kind, you would be entirely right. But since you accuse people generally for inquiring into these subjects and add as the reason for your censure that they are neither so necessary to know nor, if known, make a person better, it is evident that you do not merely require that these things not be disputed curiously and outside God’s Word. Rather, your opinion is that they should not be treated at all and that Christians can safely despise and remain ignorant of them.

I think you sin most gravely in these matters, because it is altogether necessary that these subjects be discussed soberly and from God’s Word, whether to teach us, confirm the weak, or refute heretics. Here is the chief and legitimate use of the liberal arts, if they are employed in treating these questions, provided that word-battles and all sophistical contentions are avoided and that within God’s Word—which alone is self-authenticating and answerable to none—we restrain our wanton reason.

*[Marginal heading: Whether it is necessary to inquire about God.]*

Certainly, since knowledge of God through Christ is, as it were, the material of our faith, I cannot sufficiently marvel by what judgment you say that knowledge about God is not so necessary for obtaining salvation through faith. A little earlier, Bellius, you said that correction of life, not faith, is the one way by which one comes to Christ, that is, to salvation; yet in describing your golden coin you said it is required that one believe in the God of Moses. I cannot conceive how these statements cohere.

*[Marginal heading: On angels.]*

If sober knowledge of angels—for I do not approve that Dionysius’s speculations which wander from God’s Word—does not make a person better, why does Scripture commemorate their nature, office, and even certain names? Is it because the Holy Spirit wishes to chatter about useless things? Why does Christ teach that the angels of children see the Father’s face? Why is the heavenly host mentioned, which Elisha and his servant also behold? Why does Daniel mention the angels of the Greeks and Persians? Why does David so often say that we are surrounded by the protection of angels? Why does the apostle call them ministering spirits sent out to serve those who will inherit salvation? *[Marginal references: Matthew 18:10; Luke 2:13; 2 Kings 6:17; Daniel 10:6, 13, 20; Psalm 34:8; Hebrews 1:14.]*

Is it not so that believers may receive extraordinary consolation from this, as someone rightly wrote, when they hear that the heavenly armies are assigned as ministers to procure their salvation? For to have them continually at work on our behalf is no ordinary pledge of God’s love toward us. From this too flows a remarkable confirmation of faith: our salvation, fortified with such defenses, is beyond danger.

*[Marginal heading: On the state of souls after this life.]*

We say that knowledge of the state of souls after this life must necessarily be treated

[PDF page 19 | logical scan 18 | printed page 102]

—even against those fanatics who, when they suppose that souls sleep after death, partly dissolve the very substance of souls, partly destroy a great part of believers’ consolation, and partly pervert innumerable passages of Scripture to confirm this dream. So much for these blasphemies individually. Their origin appears to be, first, that these men confine knowledge of God within excessively narrow bounds; and second, that they either do not themselves know that faith by which alone we are Christians or wish to root it out of human minds. We demonstrated the former point by examining each part. The latter is clear even from their saying that correction of life is the one way to Christ.

*[Marginal heading: What the faith of Christians is, and what the way to Christ is.]*

If this is true, come, let the law alone be proclaimed and let us concern ourselves with fulfilling it, so that after we have corrected ourselves we may come, now sound and strong, to the physician who will confirm our health. Let us confess Christ to be the Son of God and lord and judge of the world who, of course, will accuse or condemn us according to our deeds. Let no inquiry even be made into the distinction between Gospel and law, the free remission of sins, or the imputation of Christ’s righteousness. “For it is just the same,” they say, “as asking whether some prince will come in a carriage or on horseback, dressed in red or white.” Let everyone therefore be free to think whatever he pleases about these matters. “Let us judge no one, lest we ourselves be judged.” Let us cast aside Paul’s disputations about these subjects as not so necessary to know, “since tax collectors and prostitutes were saved without them,” as contributing nothing to integrity of life, and indeed as guides to pride, savagery, and persecution.

But we have learned that the way to God is far different: namely, that eternal and free love of God by which he chose us in Christ before the creation of the world. Having loved us in the Son while we were still enemies, he gave us to his Son in his own time; and so that we might come to him, he himself drew us by his Spirit and made us willing from unwilling. That Spirit then created in us the contrite heart, broken by the recognition and consideration of our crimes. *[Marginal references: Ephesians 1:4; Romans 8:4; John 6:44–45; Galatians 4:6.]*

We therefore say that this is the true and one way by which we come to Christ, and through Christ to salvation: by God’s grace, understanding the magnitude of our disease, we flee trembling to the physician. Yet he knew us first and, coming to us, revealed through his Spirit that he is our physician. *[Marginal reference: 1 John 4:19.]* Nor do we stop at that stage, for it is not enough to have known one’s disease and the method by which it is cured unless one uses the medicine. Thus this good and sole physician of so many lethal diseases—to whom we were given by the Father—both offers his medicine, that is, himself, to us the elect and creates in us the hand by which we embrace him as he comes. I mean faith, that free and perpetual gift of the children of God, by which individual believers not only confess as true all those things which you reject as of little necessity, but also—and this is the chief thing, peculiar to Christian faith alone—apply them all to themselves as intended properly and particularly for themselves. They experience in reality that correction of life is the effect of this faith. *[Marginal reference: 2 Thessalonians 3:2.]*

Therefore, although no one comes to Christ who does not amend his life—for we require that knowledge and understanding with which faith, hope, and charity cohere by a perpetual and indissoluble bond—we nevertheless deny your assertion that correction of life is the way to Christ. Rather, since we are by nature wild olive shoots, we say that we must first be grafted by faith into the olive tree so that, as its sap exercises its power in us, we may bear good fruit. We do not come to Christ because we have corrected our life. On the contrary, because Christ first loved us, came to us, is in us, and we are in him, we therefore acknowledge our vices in such a way that we make daily progress in amending life and, having once been justified, grow daily in Christ as faith increases and are justified more and more. *[Marginal references: Romans 11:17; 1 John 4:19; Ephesians 4:13; Revelation 22:11.]*

Thus when you wish a pure heart—that is, as you interpret it, correction of life—to be brought to the knowledge of religion, and to confirm this cite the testimony “Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God,” first of all you either require from a person what he cannot himself accomplish, namely, that before he believes he should amend his life, or you restore the most pernicious papal doctrine concerning the remnants of free will. For we must bring to the knowledge of God’s grace and salvation not a pure heart, but one crushed by God and afterward to be purified through faith. Unless perhaps you wish us to approach God because we are pure, or so that he may perfect our purity, rather than that he may sprinkle and wash us who are, as David says, thoroughly polluted with every filth. Thus God’s love in Christ precedes in order our knowledge, knowledge precedes faith, and faith precedes sanctification, as an efficient cause precedes its effect. For he first loved and chose us, not when we were holy (as Augustine rightly noted), but so that we might be holy. *[Marginal references: Psalm 51:7; 1 John 4:19; Ephesians 1:4.]* We nevertheless acknowledge that these are so connected that they cannot be torn apart, and that each is daily increased and con-firmed by the other. Next, the testimony you bring forward is plainly twisted by you, since there the Lord, by setting forth a gracious reward—as he often does elsewhere—exhorts us to purity of life. Nor in that passage does “to see God” mean “to know God,” but to behold the heavenly inheritance acquired by Christ’s blood for those who live piously and innocently.

[PDF page 20 | logical scan 19 | printed page 103]

Let us now come to that third degree of Christianity which you have proposed. Who, then, is said by you to lead an innocent life? Evidently one who believes in one God and in Christ as lord and judge of the world, strives to benefit everyone, and harms no one. *[Marginal heading: What an innocent life is, and how good works are to be evaluated.]* From these principles it follows that if Socrates, Aristides, Phocion, Fabricius, Cato, and Atticus had believed what the devils believe, or if the devils lived as those men lived, eternal life would be owed to both groups. Yet we can discover neither faith nor a good life in the one group or the other.

As regards faith, since it is not a bare or simple knowledge of God but a firm assent by which believers embrace Christ offered to them by the Father, we cannot attribute this faith to devils, to whom the promises are not offered; nor to Socrates and the others, to whom Christ was unknown; nor to people of wicked life, in whom the Spirit of God does not dwell; nor to false brothers, whose faith is both counterfeit and temporary. *[Marginal references: 1 John 2:24, 19, 23.]*

As for the good life, since we have learned to call good only that life which God approves; since without faith it is impossible to please God; and since God’s wrath remains upon the one who does not know the Son—I say “knows,” not as devils or unbelievers know him, but as the elect alone know him, who through Christ are both justified and sanctified, because anyone who says “I know him” but does not keep his commandments is a liar—it follows from these things that those who lack that faith can do nothing before God except sin. *[Marginal references: Hebrews 11:2, 6; John 3:36; 1 John 2:4.]* Therefore the apostle does not simply say that love is the end of the law, but only that love which proceeds from a pure heart and a good conscience. He then explains what a good conscience is when he adds, “and an unfeigned faith.” *[Marginal reference: 1 Timothy 1:5.]*

Yet we do not compare Scipio or Cato with Catiline or Nero, for we acknowledge distinctions and definite degrees of vices and virtues. But when the subject is that innocence of life which is approved by God, we seek the ground on which works are approved not even in the works of the justified themselves, still less in the precepts of philosophy, but in the righteousness of Christ alone. Once we are so united to this Christ through the faith of which we have spoken that we become one with him, only then do we have no doubt that the fruits sprung from this root please God graciously and liberally.

Nor, again, do we rely in any respect upon those works, however graciously accepted, as though they were the material of our salvation. For the apostle, speaking of Abraham himself, says, “If he was justified by works, he has something in which to boast, but not before God.” *[Marginal reference: Romans 4:2.]* Much less, therefore, can you affirm this concerning people who stand as far from Abraham as unbelievers stand from the father of believers—that is, at an infinite distance.

Since matters stand thus, and since we have made it plain that by your doctrine the true God is confused with an idol, the foundations of religion are utterly overturned, and an upright and innocent life is wrongly defined, everyone sees, I think, how far it is from being true, as you wished to persuade us, that those whom all orthodox churches from the very beginnings of the church have regarded as heretics were not to be punished because they disputed about matters of little necessity. On the contrary, their memory is worthy of the hatred and execration of all good people, and the magistrate’s office requires that those who root out the Christian religion from its foundations and awaken their buried errors be punished with the utmost severity.

Second argument of the adversaries: Heretics are not to be punished because the controversies which have now been debated in the church for so long cannot be decided from the written Word of God.

I now come to your second argument, in which no less blasphemy—and certainly grosser impiety—appears than in the preceding one. You say in part that the questions now in controversy are still obscure and not yet certainly settled; and in part that religion has been delivered to us obscurely and enigmatically. From this it follows—as one of your party says, and when I address him I address you yourself, Bellius—that “if there are controversies in the matter of religion, we must follow the counsel of Judas Maccabaeus. When he had no way to determine what should be done with the altar of burnt offering, he placed its stones in a suitable place on the temple mount until some prophet should come and report an oracle concerning them.” *[Marginal source: Castellio, preface to the Latin Bible.]* Or rather, we should follow Moses, although it had just been expressly commanded to him that if anyone deliberately committed an offense against the law, he should pay the death penalty; nevertheless Moses refused to kill the man who had gathered wood on the Sabbath until a specific oracle had been received about the matter. Or we should follow Gamaliel’s counsel. Or Paul, who forbids us to condemn one another. Or the Roman law concerning provisional possession. I ask, however, why do you wrap these things in so many circumlocutions?

[PDF page 21 | logical scan 20 | printed page 104]

Come, state plainly and openly what you think: because everyone is ignorant of what the truth is, and the Scriptures are so obscure that the controversies by which the church has now been exercised for so long cannot be decided from them, no one is to be condemned as a heretic. Instead, a new revelation from heaven must be awaited, or at least a new exposition of the Scriptures so clear and plain that no one can doubt it. Meanwhile, so far as religion is concerned, let everyone be free to believe and teach whatever he wishes.

But perhaps you will say that you condemn human wickedness and impiety, not Scripture, which indeed one of your party has lately polished so well that it now speaks not barbarously, as before, but in Latin—so far are you from despising it. Just as I acknowledge that you say these things, however, I also affirm that you say them only to deceive the simple, and I confirm this from your conclusion. For you wish as a general rule that new oracles be awaited in controversies of religion. Thus you cannot deny one or the other of these alternatives: either you reject the Scriptures as obscure and not sufficiently fit for deciding controversies, or you accuse all people of every age—and even the apostles themselves—of ignorance.

Of the apostles’ time the same man says, “If that perfect knowledge of God and piety existed then, I ask why it was so scanty and vanished so quickly? But if he says that it exists in our time, we shall marvel at this peace which forges swords out of our plowshares and pruning hooks, and converts household furnishings into cannon and fortifications.”

If you prefer to concede the latter rather than the former, I say that you rush into the same precipice. Anyone who accuses the prophets, the apostles, and all the others by whom the records of both Testaments were written of ignorance must necessarily charge not only those people but also their writings with the same offense—unless we wish them to have been madmen who both spoke and wrote things they did not themselves understand. Perhaps this is also why that excellent man of yours calls them not “prophets” but “seers.” Therefore, when I say that you regard God as one who makes sport of humankind and in reality abolish the Scriptures, I neither slander you nor invent anything. I merely state plainly and simply what you cunningly conceal.

But it is worth seeing by what arguments you are induced to dare affirm so impious and pestilent a doctrine. “The controversies of religion,” says Clebergius, “have not yet been certainly settled. For if they had been settled, debate would have ceased, nor would anyone be so mad as to endure death in order to deny a certain matter.” And another of your party says, “Indeed, if we wish to confess the truth, our age is still plunged in the profound darkness of ignorance. The surest testimony of this fact is the disputes that are so grave, so stubborn, and so destructive.”

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

An excellent argument indeed! Because people have always doubted about the meaning of Scripture, no certain decision of controversies can therefore be established from the Scriptures; instead, while the case remains pending, provisional possession must be awarded in favor of liberty, and a new sentence from the Judge awaited to settle the controversy. Tell me, then, you champions not of public liberty but of license: is there no truth simply because it always has been, and will be, unknown to most people? If this reasoning holds, what will you finally establish—not only in divine matters, but even in human ones? For what is there about which someone does not doubt, or on which everyone agrees? What therefore remains but that you renew the Academic doctrine of incomprehensibility, so that, the knowledge of certainty being as it were despaired of, each person follows what seems probable to him?

Since this doctrine is as directly opposed to the Christian religion as truth is to falsehood, if you are believed the Christian religion must plainly perish from its foundations. For since it rests on the Word of God alone, if anyone insists that this Word is so obscure that controversies of religion have never yet been certainly settled from it, then, once the firmness of God’s Word has been removed, on what foundation will the church rest? Indeed, even the few things you alone had left to us must necessarily totter. For, I ask, if you depart from the Word, from where will you prove to us that there is one eternal and omnipotent God by whom the created world is governed? If you say, “From contemplating created things,” I shall reply that this is deceptive and uncertain, and I shall infer it from your own argument. For Bellius’s assertion that every nation confesses one God is not true.

Even today the whole nation of Epicureans, dispersed throughout the entire world, loudly declares either that there is no God or that he does not care for the world. If travelers are to be believed, many peoples still worship several gods. The Peripatetics persuade themselves that the world is eternal. The Stoics subject their god to fate. Those opinions have not been driven from the world, nor will they be driven out before Christ judges the world. “But God’s Word testifies to these things.” What if I answer that the impious have always disputed whether the Word itself is God’s Word? What if I mention the two gods of the Manichaeans? What if I mention Valentinus’s Aeons and similar monstrosities? There is no doubt that these men too employed Scripture to confirm their errors.

[PDF page 22 | logical scan 21 | printed page 105]

Now, as regards Christ, from where except God’s Word shall we finally know that the Son of God is lord and judge of the world? These things not only surpass every human faculty, but are so contrary to our reason that Paul rightly said he proclaimed foolishness to the Gentiles and a stumbling block to the Jews; and elsewhere he often says that this mystery was hidden from all ages. It is revealed, as Christ says, not by flesh or blood but by the heavenly Father. *[Marginal references: 1 Corinthians 1:23; Ephesians 3:9; Matthew 16:17.]* How, then, does he reveal it? Plainly by opening our heart through the Holy Spirit by means of the outward preaching of God’s Word. *[Marginal reference: Acts 16:14.]*

If the Scriptures are not sufficiently plain and clear to confirm and explain the things we believe about Christ, what remains except for you to think that our assertions concerning the one God and his providence, and likewise concerning Christ, are merely probable and not necessary, since the impious have always doubted about these things? What shall I say of the other matters—justification, free choice, baptism, the Lord’s Supper, and invocation of saints—which you deny can be decided from the Word, although they have been recorded in plain and clear testimonies of Scripture? This, plainly, is the license being sought: that with only a few things excepted—which are useless in themselves and even totter if we follow your doctrine—any monstrosities whatsoever may be brought into God’s church, and Academic opinion may be established in place of faith, likelihood in place of truth, and probability in place of necessity.

But may this be far from all the godly, who not only believe but also understand what they believe and are ready to give from God’s Word a reason for their faith; who, finally, are children not in understanding but in malice; and who by habitual use have their faculties trained to distinguish good and evil. *[Marginal references: 1 Peter 3:15; 1 Corinthians 14:20; Hebrews 5:14.]* For neither the multitude of doubters, which always has been and will be very great, nor the long duration of disputes—for the quarrel between God and Satan’s faction is most ancient—can make it impossible to establish anything certain in religion. These things instead convict the world of blindness and unbelief, and they ought to sharpen believers’ diligence, so that by the light of that most brilliant and inextinguishable lamp of God’s Word they may distinguish good from evil and truth from falsehood.

Next—to grant you nothing that belongs to another—among whom, finally, have those controversies which you wish to be indifferent remained so long in doubt? In the true church of God? Certainly not. For God has always had some church in which his voice was not only heard, but also recognized and understood, so that it never doubted about those matters without whose knowledge no church can exist. For God has always preached these things plainly and clearly to his church, though after Christ’s advent he did so much more openly than before.

I nevertheless do not deny, but rather by God’s grace profess that I recognize in myself, that even the elect themselves, of whom the true church of God is constructed, are called by the Lord and made participants in the light of the Gospel at definite times and by degrees. But it is one thing to speak generally about the true church, whose members are now mature—insofar, at least, as people can be mature here—that is, trained through habitual use to distinguish good and evil, and by whom the doctrine of the Gospel is recognized and understood. It is another thing to speak about the individual people of whom the church consists, among whom there are many who are weak, many who are feeble in faith, and many who are even ignorant, whom the Lord perfects in his own time.

Otherwise, how, or to what purpose, does God say that he will be with his church until the consummation of the age? Why, in speaking to us, did he so greatly accommodate himself to our capacity? Was it so that, although the words were familiar, their meaning could not be understood? Or was it so that he might exercise us like another Sphinx in untying the knots of riddles? I do not deny that the Scriptures cannot be understood unless the Spirit of God teaches, or that this Spirit is not given to everyone. But did Christ promise him only to the apostles, by whom they would be led into all truth, and not rather to the whole church? *[Marginal references: Isaiah 59:21; Matthew 28:20; John 16:13.]* If the Spirit of God does not lead into all truth—that is, the truth of those things which it is absolutely necessary to know for salvation—where, finally, is truth? And if it is nowhere, what is the Christian religion but an empty and futile dream?

Was Moses a liar when he told the Israelites that, in order to seek God’s will, there was no need either to ascend into heaven or cross the sea? *[Marginal reference: Deuteronomy 30:11.]* Was David mad when he testified that the law—that is, the doctrine contained in God’s Word—was a lamp to his feet? *[Marginal reference: Psalm 119:105.]* Why do Paul and Peter call us back to this very Word which they proclaimed, if it is as obscure as you wish it to be? *[Marginal references: Romans 10:8; 2 Peter 3:15.]* Why does Christ himself confirm his doctrine from the written Word, frequently citing testimonies which the Jews even today do not cease to assail? “He gave apostles,” says Paul, “pastors and teachers, so that we should no longer be carried about by every wind of doctrine or be deceived by men, but so that, illumined by that true knowledge of God, we might all together at-” *[Marginal reference: Ephesians 4:11, 14.]*tain the unity of faith.”

[PDF page 23 | logical scan 22 | printed page 106]

Tell me, then: teachers of what? Certainly of the Gospel, other than which, if anyone preaches another, let him be accursed. *[Marginal reference: Galatians 1:8.]* But if this Gospel of Paul was first preached orally and then written down by hand so obscurely and enigmatically that, as the Epicureans are accustomed to say, it can be bent this way and that like a waxen nose; and if it is insufficient to explain necessary questions—I omit curious ones—about God, the Trinity, Christ’s state and office, angels, the state of souls after this life, predestination, free choice, justification, the sacraments, invocation of saints; and, to add other questions which you deliberately and knowingly omitted lest you offend the papists whom you have long been flattering, about the Mass, purgatory, indulgences, the primacy of the pope, original sin, the worship of images, confession, and the other counterfeit sacraments; if, I say, it is insufficient to explain these and similar questions, how will Paul not be a liar? He would be acting just as if he required us to lean upon a reed so that henceforth we might not be tossed and waver.

Why, moreover, does that same man testify that Antichrist will be destroyed by the Spirit of God’s mouth? If after Isaiah you understand that passage to mean the written Word of God, why do you deny that controversies can be decided from it? *[Marginal references: 2 Thessalonians 2:8; Isaiah 11:4.]* But if you mean some other word, tell us and show what it is. For our part, we believe that these things were written about that Word which, since it is divinely inspired, is useful for teaching, refutation, correction, and training in righteousness. Paul wishes pastors to be equipped by it not only so that they are fit to teach, but also so that—as is written of Apollos—they may refute opponents. For this reason he also calls the church the pillar of truth. *[Marginal references: 2 Timothy 3:16; Acts 18:28; 1 Timothy 3:15.]*

Finally, to make an end of speaking about this matter, if in the last days God spoke through his Son so obscurely and enigmatically, why does he command us to hear him? How will faith arise from hearing the Word? *[Marginal references: Hebrews 1:2; Matthew 17:5; Romans 10:17; 1 Corinthians 2:16.]* To settle the matter in a word: either Paul must be a liar—which may it never be—when he says that we have the mind of Christ, or, as is true, you are blasphemers, unless perhaps you can show that the Holy Spirit customarily teaches God’s church in some way other than through the written Word. You will persuade no one of that unless he is utterly insane.

Tell me, therefore: who has always doubted? Plainly those whom God hardened like Pharaoh in order to display his power in them; those whose eyes the god of this age has blinded, so that seeing they do not see and hearing they do not hear; those who willingly remain ignorant of the truth. Finally, we answer in a single phrase with Paul: if the Gospel is still veiled, it is veiled only among those who perish. We acknowledge that such people are often mingled within the church, since Antichrist himself sits in God’s temple. *[Marginal references: Exodus 9:16; 2 Corinthians 4:4; 2 Thessalonians 2:4.]* But though they are in the church, they nevertheless are not of the church. What madness, therefore, is this—to wish to tear down the truth of Christian doctrines because some people not yet sufficiently firm customarily question them for a time, while unbelievers and the impious always doubt them?

Clebergius, however, marvels that debate about these matters has not ceased and thinks no one can be so foolish as to endure death in order to deny a certain matter. Yet he ought to have reflected that there will never be a lack of people who are blind in the face of manifest truth and who—whether through hatred, like those who said that Christ cast out demons by Beelzebub’s aid; or through ambition, lust, envy; or blinded by God’s judgment and entangled in the devil’s snare—stir up controversies about the surest and clearest matters, controversies so bitter that they do not hesitate even to undergo death on their account. *[Marginal references: Matthew 12:24; 2 Timothy 2:9.]* If you do not believe me, believe the experience of every age.

What is more certain than that Christ is God, by the testimony even of unbelieving Thomas? *[Marginal reference: John 20:28.]* And yet—to say nothing of hypocrites—very few people have ever believed this, if compared with the remaining multitude. How many Jews will you find prepared to undergo death for their Messiah, how many papists for their transubstantiation, how many Muslims for Muhammad? And—to pass over all ancient examples—the recent example of that execrable Servetus shows how powerful inveterate obstinacy is. What is more certain than the future resurrection? Yet—to say nothing of Sadducees and Epicureans—Philetus and Hymenaeus said it had already taken place, and today the Libertines follow them. *[Marginal reference: 2 Timothy 2:18.]*

Shall we therefore say that adulteries, murders, thefts, and robberies are not to be punished because the Anabaptists make wives common, excuse murders by their revelations, abolish private ownership of property, and die obstinately in these errors? For, if you do not know it, the devil too has his martyrs, as Augustine rightly teaches when speaking of the Circumcellions, who have everything in common with God’s martyrs except the cause. Yet we shall never concede on that account either that Christian doctrines are uncertain or that the devil’s inventions are certain. What, moreover, says that good little brother of Servetus? “If there is one Spirit,” he says, “and one truth, all in whom the same Spirit dwells must necessarily think the same in spiritual matters.”

[PDF page 24 | logical scan 23 | printed page 107]

I certainly agree with you on this point, and I say that truth itself has been forced from you against your will. For the catholic church of God, because it is governed by one and the same Spirit, has always held the same view with one and perpetual consent concerning those principal articles which, contrary to your opinion, comprise the sum of Christianity. You should, however, have established certain degrees among spiritual people—that is, believers.

For I grant you this: because the Spirit of God does not yet possess us entirely and sin, though it has indeed ceased to reign in us, has not ceased to dwell in us, those who know still know only in part, and much darkness and ignorance remains in every person. But I repeat what I said before: God speaks so clearly to his church, and imparts so much light to it, that concerning those matters whose knowledge is necessary in the church there has always been doctrine as certain and clear as was required for it to be a true church. *[Marginal references: Romans 7:13; 1 Corinthians 13:9.]*

For the matters on which even those in whom Christ has now grown to maturity sometimes disagree are always of such a kind that, while the sum of religion remains safe, different opinions do not produce different churches. Indeed, I go further in continuing to answer that dilemma by which you wished to ensnare us. I say that both in the apostles’ time and in every intervening age, that fullness of Christ the head overflowed into his members, to such a degree that even the worst people have sometimes been endowed with a great light of understanding, so that they might be without excuse and so that God might show how immense and infinite his power is, since whenever he pleases he compels even his enemies to contribute their labor to constructing the church’s building.

That this is so is evident even from the Lord’s statement that the servant who knows his master’s will but does not do it will be beaten with many blows. Thus there must be some who possess the Lord’s will for others, not for themselves. *[Marginal reference: Luke 12:47.]* Understanding and faith must therefore be distinguished, yet in such a way that whoever posits the latter necessarily posits the former as well, though not conversely. For faith comes from hearing the preached Word—hearing of such a kind that it produces full assurance and applies to each of the elect the doctrine offered universally. *[Marginal reference: Romans 10:17.]* Although understanding too does not come from nature—for “the natural man does not understand the things of God”—it is nevertheless numbered among those gifts which the Lord sometimes imparts even to the impious and reprobate for definite reasons. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 2:14.]*

Therefore, your statement that human wickedness and depravity are an obstacle which prevents the Scriptures from being understood is utterly false, unless it is qualified by suitable distinctions. For even in the elect themselves, as long as they live here, that native wickedness and depravity is never wholly extinguished, although it is not imputed. Yet it does not prevent them from having the mind of Christ, insofar as is expedient for salvation. Moreover, even the most wicked people sometimes have some understanding and knowledge of things necessary for obtaining salvation through faith, although it is useless and fruitless as regards themselves. So far are we from conceding that obscurity of God’s Word and that diabolical incomprehensibility.

Nor does your question deserve an answer when you ask why, if the apostles’ age possessed that overflowing knowledge promised by the prophets, it was so scanty and vanished so quickly. For the church has always possessed as much light as it needed. It is not ours to measure the times and seasons in which it appears now to triumph and now to be utterly cast down and oppressed. As regards the matter itself—that is, the prophecies of Isaiah, Ezekiel, and Malachi and those of the Apocalypse, which you insist have not yet been fulfilled—I could first answer that your inference is unsound. Because certain things were foretold which have not yet happened, you therefore demand that no judgment be rendered concerning any controversy of religion, but that some new sentence of the Judge be awaited. *[Marginal references: Isaiah 11 and 60; Ezekiel 36; Malachi 4; Revelation 20.]*

But I have a very different answer. First, you err in thinking that things to be understood spiritually—that is, the taming of our wild nature through God’s Word and the forming of God’s kingdom from citizens of different dispositions and of different nations hostile to one another—must be expounded by you crudely and in Jewish fashion. Thus you ask when Christ will restore that kingdom to Israel which, of course, Virgil described most beautifully in his tenth Eclogue. *[Marginal reference: Acts 1:6.]* Next, as everyone practiced in reading the prophets knows, the kingdom of Christ is often described by the prophets not as it will be at every individual moment and point of time, but as it will be in its own—so to speak—fullness, which indeed will never exist in this world, unless perhaps we prefer to follow the dreams of Chiliasts, Anabaptists, and rabbis rather than Christ’s prophecy about his own kingdom, which you nevertheless fre- *[Marginal references: John 18:36; Luke 12:51.]*quently have upon your lips. For what does Christ say? “My kingdom is not of this world.” And elsewhere, “I did not come to send peace upon the earth.”

[PDF page 25 | logical scan 24 | printed page 108]

I pass over the fact that he is called a stone of stumbling and a sign spoken against, and that everywhere he announces the cross and endurance to his followers. *[Marginal reference: Luke 2:34.]* Nor do these things belong to only one time or age, since he foretells that he will come to judgment like a thief and will not find faith on the earth at that time. Thus whatever those distinguished prophets foretold concerning the church’s highest and perfect peace is spiritual and pertains only to the true children of God. Next, their prophecies look to the church’s future condition as it will be when, all enemies having been subdued, Christ hands the kingdom to the Father so that God may be all in all. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 15:28.]* Meanwhile the church tastes this happiness even in the midst of afflictions, patiently awaiting the time when it will no longer see through a mirror in an enigma. Yet that saying of Paul must be understood comparatively, with regard to the future manifestation. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 13:12.]*

Therefore, finally to conclude this argument of yours: it is not true either that controversies of religion have not yet been settled with sufficient certainty or that they cannot be decided from God’s Word. Nor should judgment about these matters be suspended, or a new sentence from the Judge awaited. Falsehood has up to the present been distinguished from truth by the written Word of God, and must always be so distinguished.

Third argument: From the example of Judas Maccabaeus.

But it is time to explain those examples too which you misuse to confirm your counsel that heretics are not to be touched before a new sentence of the Judge. The first is that of Judas Maccabaeus, who, when he could not determine what should be done about the altar of burnt offering, placed its stones in a suitable place on the temple mount until some prophet should come and give an answer about them. *[Marginal reference: 1 Maccabees 4:46.]*

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

First of all, we rightly reject the authority of that book for establishing any doctrine or example by which we could be bound. Then, lest you think that we lack a suitable answer, we say that you compare utterly dissimilar matters. When the old altar of burnt offering had been polluted, Judas pulled it down; the displaced stones were set apart, and an oracle was awaited, because the Lord had not declared in the law what he wished to be done with them. What can rightly be inferred from this except that nothing is to be undertaken in the worship of God without his Word? In the case under discussion, therefore, since we have God’s express Word and command by which blasphemies are ordered to be punished, and since, as we showed above, it is moreover a principal duty of the magistrate to see that sins against the first table are punished, why is this action of Judas proposed to us as though we had either no Word of God or one insufficiently express for deciding controversies of religion?

Fourth argument: From the example of Moses.

The second example is that of Moses. Although a little before he had been commanded that if anyone deliberately committed an offense against the law, he should pay the death penalty, he nevertheless refused to kill the man who had gathered wood on the Sabbath until a specific oracle had been received concerning the matter. *[Marginal reference: Numbers 15:30 and following.]* But in this example, Bellius, you go astray in two ways.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

First, because where you translate “deliberately,” the Hebrew text has “with a high hand.” You have certainly corrupted this Hebraism, as you have many others. For you thought that sins committed with a high hand are so opposed to sins committed by error that there is nothing in between, and thus that the law is violated in only two ways: first when we sin unwittingly, and second when we sin deliberately—that is, knowingly and consciously. Even if that division were true, Moses does not follow it in this passage. This is evident from the fact that this law establishes capital punishment against everyone who sins with a high hand. If you interpret that phrase as referring to every sin in which an evil intention intervenes, there will be no provision of the political, moral, or ceremonial law that is not capital, so that what was formerly said of Solon might truly be said of God: namely, that he wrote his laws in blood, not ink.

Consequently, monetary fines and the other noncapital penalties added to most laws would pertain only to those who sinned by mistake. Besides the fact that everyone sees this is utterly false, it can also be established clearly from the passage which describes atonement even for sins involving fraud and violence. *[Marginal reference: Leviticus 6:2.]* What then? Moses did not undertake there to explain every species of violating the law, but only two: one, when someone sins through error or thoughtlessness; the other, when someone sins with a high hand—that is, as the common translator rightly renders it, “through arrogance,” plainly with open disdain and contempt of God. Since this crime is more diabolical than human, it is no wonder that it is punished capitally even if the sin is otherwise committed in a matter of the slightest importance. For then the very majesty of God is truly violated with utmost contempt even in the smallest matter. But if someone, moved by avarice, lust, hatred, or necessity, knowingly and consciously injures another person; a new species of violating the law arises, one which Moses does not treat here.

[PDF page 26 | logical scan 25 | printed page 109]

For although this cannot happen without God being despised—that is, without God being valued less than he ought—nevertheless, because the sin is not committed from hatred of God himself, about whom a mind drunk with its own desires perhaps gave no thought at all, it is therefore called a sin against one’s neighbor or the commonwealth rather than against God. Thus different penalties have been established according to the gravity of the injury and offense. Since, therefore, the man who violated the Sabbath might have committed this crime from necessity or for some other reason, it was also possible that he was not liable under this law. *[Marginal reference: Exodus 31:14.]*

But lest you think I evade the matter because another written law existed against those who violated the Sabbath—a law under which that man was undoubtedly liable—I reply that he was put in prison not because Moses doubted that he should suffer capital punishment, but because he was uncertain about the kind of punishment, which had not been explained in that law. In the same way, Moses once sought an oracle from God when condemning a foreign blasphemer because the Lord had not yet written any law concerning that act. *[Marginal reference: Leviticus 24:12.]* Otherwise, if Moses had sought some new oracle from God in addition to God’s express Word, as you wish him to have done, he would certainly have inflicted a grave injury upon God’s majesty.

Why, then, do you bring this forward to abolish judgments against heretics, just as though controversies of religion were not yet clear? Indeed, who is so blind that he does not see this consequence: if it was unlawful to spare someone who had despised the ceremonial law of the Sabbath, much less are heretics themselves, left unrestrained, to be tolerated without punishment?

Fifth argument: From the authority of Gamaliel.

I come to Gamaliel’s counsel, which you are accustomed, as I hear, to set up like a shield. Is it because your opinion of apostolic doctrine is no different from Gamaliel’s—namely doubtful and hesitant? *[Marginal reference: Acts 5:34 and following.]* I answer in the words of one whose writings, if you had read them as diligently as you are accustomed carelessly to despise them, would have prevented you from going so shamefully astray on many points.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation. Marginal source: Calvin, Commentary on Acts 5.]*

“Because Gamaliel is uncertain what is right, like a blind man in darkness he dares to go neither this way nor that, but suspends judgment. Meanwhile, from true principles he draws a bad consequence, namely that no counsel should be applied because God will protect what is his, while what is from men will perish. Yet although the Lord is the only farmer, he nevertheless sends laborers into his vineyard. Although he alone gives growth, he nevertheless wishes there to be people who plant and water. Although he is the architect of his temple, he nevertheless joins ministers and workmen to himself to labor on this building. Finally, although he is the defender and vindicator of his works, he nevertheless uses human beings as ministers.”

You, therefore, may praise Gamaliel’s counsel as heavenly and divine for as long as you please. We shall adhere to Paul, who declares accursed those who preach another Gospel. *[Marginal reference: Galatians 1:8.]* Nor do we think the church of Thyatira was rebuked by the Holy Spirit without cause because it permitted Jezebel, who called herself a prophetess, to teach and to seduce God’s servants. *[Marginal reference: Revelation 2:20.]*

Sixth argument: From Paul’s authority.

You go no less astray in explaining the passage of Paul in which he commands: “If anyone is weak in faith, receive him.” And a little afterward: “Who are you to judge another’s servant? To his own master he stands or falls.” Again, “Why do you judge your brother? Or why do you regard your brother as nothing?” And once more, “Let us no longer judge one another.” *[Marginal references: Romans 14:1, 4, 10, 13.]* Anyone who considers Paul’s purpose will readily understand by what right all these statements are twisted to this cause.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

First, he speaks not of heretics, whom elsewhere he calls dogs and the mutilation, but of those weak in faith who through infirmity were still clinging to the elements of the law. We, however, are discussing heretics—that is, those who err maliciously and are factious. *[Marginal reference: Philippians 3:2.]* Moreover, Paul there discusses drink and food, that is, things indifferent. But we are discussing the very substance of religion and its principal articles, which you nevertheless number among indifferent things.

Next, it is worthwhile to consider the time when Paul wrote these things: namely, at the very beginnings of the preached Gospel, when something had to be conceded to the weakness of those from the circumcision who had believed. But what, finally, do those people have in common with heretics who stubbornly dissent from God’s church about matters contained in God’s express Word and explained so fully and clearly even in our own memory?

Finally, in Paul both here and elsewhere, private and rash judgments are forbidden, by which we condemn our neighbor over some trifling matter as though rejected by God—not in love, but from a certain curiosity, or hatefully and with sinister intent. Christ too very sharply rebukes this sickness of mind. *[Marginal references: 1 Corinthians 4:2, 5; Matthew 7:1.]* We, however, are discussing public judgments, both ecclesiastical and civil, in which God himself sits as judge. If anyone abolishes these, not only can no church, but no commonwealth either can be safely preserved.

[PDF page 27 | logical scan 26 | printed page 110]

Indeed, private judgments themselves are necessary to some extent so that people may exercise the duties of love toward one another. For we are taught both to evaluate a tree from its fruits and to restore gently to the way, insofar as possible, those whom we judge to be in error. So far is Paul from rebuking the use and custom of public judgments, as Augustine explained fully in many places. *[Marginal sources: Augustine, book 2 On Remission; book 10 On Faith, chapter 23.]*

Seventh argument: From the description of love.

I come to another argument, one that is certainly very absurd. “Love,” says the apostle, “covers a multitude of sins; it bears all things, believes all things, hopes all things, endures all things. Therefore heretics must be taught, not punished.” *[Marginal references: 1 Peter 4:8; 1 Corinthians 13:4.]*

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

What therefore remains but that the magistrate violates love when he does not tolerate a thief, when he does not assent to a false witness, or when he does not leave an adulterer a longer interval to change his life? But if these things do not conflict with love—indeed, if a judge who does not punish such people fails to show love to his citizens—then you must certainly define love so that it does not rejoice in injustice but rejoices in the truth, as the apostle himself expressed a little before. Next, you must acknowledge with Ambrose that there is a certain unjust mercy, since the law says, “You shall not pity him”; and confess with Augustine that one form of love is severe, another gentle.

For there is indeed one love, but it works differently in different cases, as that most holy man rightly teaches against Parmenian while treating this same cause. Hatred and love are opposites, yet both are commanded by the Lord. We must therefore hate certain things and love others. The Lord shows this generally when he says, “Hate iniquity, and love good,” for the Lord himself loves righteousness and hates iniquity. *[Marginal references: Amos 5:15; Psalm 45:7.]* Therefore, either show us that heretics are not evil, or know that this love of yours is not Christian—just as among robbers there is not friendship but conspiracy.

If you deny that they are evil, why does Paul wish them cut off? Why does he say that they are condemned by their own judgment? Why does he say their conscience has been seared with a hot iron? Why does he warn that they must be so completely avoided that you should not even eat with them? *[Marginal references: Galatians 5:12; Titus 3:11; 1 Timothy 4:2; 1 Corinthians 5:11.]* Why, then, does Paul attribute these qualities to love? Certainly not to remove from Christianity that part of justice by which criminals are punished, but rather so that judges punish criminals from love of good and hatred of vices, not of people. Nor is it so that the severity of judgments may be weakened, but so that nothing may be done precipitately or from a corrupt affection. Nor is it so that diligent inquiry into matters and discernment of spirits may be removed in secular affairs, much less in sacred ones, but so that suspicions may not be indulged; so that we do not confuse the weak and feeble with the malicious, factious, and inflexibly obstinate; so that we do not establish no distinction even among the wicked themselves; and finally, so that, insofar as the person and the matter permit, everything we do publicly and privately may be directed first to God’s glory and the preservation of the church, and then also to our neighbor’s salvation.

But how does this serve to establish that impunity of yours? Away, then—away with what is not love but extreme cruelty, which judges that the whole flock of Christ should be exposed as prey in order to spare a few wolves. Faithful magistrates and ministers of the Word, keep watch instead so that you drive far away the wolves invading the Lord’s flock. Indeed, whenever the magnitude of either the crime or the danger demands it, draw courageously and steadfastly against these human monsters, for the flock’s safety and after the example of Moses—otherwise the gentlest of men—the sword delivered to you by God to vindicate his dignity, so that you may truly serve God. *[Marginal references: Exodus 32:27; Psalm 2:12.]*

Eighth argument: Because Christians ought to be meek and merciful.

I take up another argument wholly connected with the preceding one. These men are accustomed to call themselves advocates of mercy and blood, but to call us executioners, bloodthirsty men, and persecutors. Whatever is written in God’s Word in favor of gentleness and mercy and against persecutions, they twist against us. Indeed Monfortius, one member of that faction, paints standards, military passwords, shields, belts, cuirasses, boots, and swords, like that Phormio whom Hannibal rightly ridiculed, so that it is astonishing that an advocate of mercy and peace can be so warlike.

Clebergius, meanwhile, arguing against his own shadow, repeatedly cries his “O God!” and constructs an elegant classification for us. For he contends that all who persecute—that is, punish—others in a case of religion do so either from envy, ambition, avarice, or zeal for God joined with ignorance; and he then concludes that the rest sin from malice. Next he twists together remarkable enthymemes, which I have transcribed so that everyone may understand by what spirit these men are driven.

[PDF page 28 | logical scan 27 | printed page 111]

1. “Do not fear, little flock; I send you like sheep among wolves.” Can a little flock persecute a great flock, or a sheep a wolf?

2. “The world will rejoice, but you will weep.” Which group rejoices? Is it not the persecutors? Which group weeps? Is it not those who suffer persecution?

3. “Blessed will you be when people persecute you.” How will we be blessed if we ourselves persecute others?

4. “Whoever wishes to come after me, let him take up his cross.” How shall we follow Christ if we ourselves impose a cross upon others?

5. A lioness pursues a wolf, and a wolf pursues a sheep, not the lioness. The sheep is last: it only suffers; it knows only how to benefit, not to harm, for it has nothing below itself.

O how marvelously meek and merciful a man! Indeed, he even rebukes the punishment of thieves and murderers as cruel. For this is how he argues:

6. Whoever believes that Jesus is the Christ and has come in the flesh is born of God. Therefore, whoever kills a person who believes that Jesus is the Christ kills a person born of God.

Why say more?

7. “Whoever suffers persecution because of faith,” he says, “either thinks rightly or errs. If he thinks rightly, he ought not to be harmed. For if Christ prayed for pardon for those by whom he was crucified because they erred, how much more for those who endure being crucified for him? And if the law of Moses grants pardon to those who have killed someone inadvertently, how much more to those who through error endure being killed?”

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

Who is so insane as not to distinguish simple error from willful ignorance? Who is so ignorant of affairs that he does not see that in this way a door is opened to all heresies, however monstrous and impious? Bellius himself also proposes Christ’s mercy to us as an example, and even adjures him like that Caiaphas.

Augustine long ago answered all these arguments when he treated this same cause against the Manichaeans and Donatists, in these words: “These things would rightly be said by you as people seeking the glory of martyrs, if you had the cause of martyrs. For the Lord does not call those blessed who suffer those evils, but those who suffer for the Son, who is Christ Jesus. You, however, do not suffer for him, but against him.” *[Marginal source: Augustine, Against Faustus.]*

Against whom does Augustine say this? Certainly against people who, if compared with these men, were infinitely better. For, offended by the vices of many, they had separated from the church while otherwise retaining in most matters the same doctrine as the catholics. But when these men say that knowledge of the Trinity and questions about Christ’s office, predestination, free choice, justification, baptism, and the Lord’s Supper are neither necessary nor useful, what do they leave us by which we are Christians? Plainly that one thing which they could not wrest away even from the devils.

The same Augustine says: “And we confess with you that the statement you cite, ‘Blessed will you be when people persecute you,’ was not spoken to the apostles alone. For it pertains not to everyone after them who suffered, suffers, or will suffer any kind of persecution, but to those who suffer for righteousness as they did. For a little before he had also said, ‘Blessed are those who suffer persecution for righteousness.’” *[Marginal source: Augustine, in the same book.]*

From the third book against Parmenian’s Letter: “Let them first consider what they do, and afterward observe what they suffer. But if they suffer anything for iniquities of that kind and refuse to be corrected, why should they dare to boast?” So far Augustine.

Yet when I say these things, I certainly concede these men more than I should. For what can they say has been done in these churches that gives them reason to call us cruel, savage, and bloodthirsty? I certainly know two leaders of that faction—although I have not wished to contaminate this little book of ours, such as it is, with their names—who long ago experienced for themselves the mercy of our magistrates and the kindness of our ministers. Yet now both call us brutal and savage persecutors.

What heretics will they name who were treated too harshly by our people? Two years ago a single man was banned from one city because of his singular shamelessness, plainly worthy of a monk, when, after often being warned and convicted, he openly disturbed the church with a most pestilent doctrine drawn from the puddles of Pighius and Siculus. But I seem to see what disturbs them more: namely, that pitiable slaughter of the most innocent people—that is, the Anabaptists—and then the most cruel death of Servetus.

As regards the former, first of all, the church of Geneva is not liable to what they call this crime—not so much because it differs from other neighboring churches as because God’s singular kindness has so far preserved it almost immune from that plague of people. In the other churches, however, I have no doubt that the princes are so prudent and godly that they have always employed the greatest moderation and possess the best reasons for their counsel; and that the pastors are so godly and learned in God’s Word that, if the Anabaptists could not be punished with a good conscience, they would have warned their princes freely and in a Christian manner.

Therefore, when these men accuse these magistrates and pastors, let them know that they act not only imprudently but seditiously. Yet what wonder is it that they dare these things when they plead their own cause? For does not Bellius openly in his present ques-tion concerning infant baptism conclude it so that he says those who dispute about it with the Catabaptists act just as if they debated, “Should the washing take place before noon or after noon?”

[PDF page 29 | logical scan 28 | printed page 112]

For this masked Bellius, lest he appear to use unwashed language—or rather, to show in reality that he valued baptism not much more highly than other washings—has everywhere produced “genii” for us from angels and “washing” from baptism. From these things one can understand what sort of monster is being nourished by these men.

I therefore exhort everyone to consider carefully what form of churches we shall finally have if these men, under the pretext of conscience and simplicity, obtain license to think and teach anything whatsoever: churches in which neither the ministers propose the same doctrine nor the people follow it, but, in order to avoid seditions, as that Clebergius says, there are as many opinions in religion as heads, provided that agreement is preserved in other matters.

Let no one therefore wonder that these merciful men resent fish being fed on heretics’ blood more than they resent so many myriads of people perishing through this contagion into sects. For they plead their own cause. If they dared speak openly, they would not merely say that the people whom our churches exclude as heretics err through simplicity in matters not so necessary to know; they would openly call them the best and holiest of men.

What, moreover, shall I say of Servetus? Except for these men, no one has yet been found who did not think this human monster should be severely restrained, although some judge that for certain reasons—of which we shall speak later—he should have been treated somewhat more gently. But what wonder is it that these men call his most just punishment cruelty, when knowledge of the Trinity itself—that is, the one foundation of religion—is numbered by them among indifferent things?

Why, then, I ask, do they call us bloodthirsty? Is it because we wish everyone who differs from us on any point to be regarded as a heretic? But this is the most open and shameless slander. Is it because, in the manner of the papists, we abolish calm and lawful examination from God’s Word and drag people, their case unheard, to water, slaughter, and flame? Let them say when this has ever been—not merely done, but even attempted—by any of us. Is it because we abolish the distinction between ecclesiastical discipline and the magistrate’s sword, between doctrine and morals, between unbelievers and apostates, between the weak and the obstinate, and finally between heretics themselves? Yet in speech, in published books, and in deed itself, we testify to the contrary. Or finally, is it because we drive the magistrate, as he slanders us, to cruelty?

This lie is refuted even in Servetus’s own case by the testimony of the most illustrious senators of Geneva. They can testify that the very man against whose destruction this faction has long conspired in vain with the devil—the man whom they falsely allege, to the greatest disgrace of that commonwealth, to have fulfilled the parts of both accuser and judge in this case—labored as much as he could, first to draw Servetus away from those impious errors, and then, when he had already become incurable, strove in every way at least to exempt him from burning. But that man’s madness was so great and his impiety so execrable and horrifying that the Senate, otherwise most merciful, judged it could be expiated only by flames. The matter itself cries out what has driven these men to breathe out these slanders; the outcome will show it.

Ninth argument: Heretics should be tolerated because no class of people is less to be feared.

Moreover, they also use this argument: “Heretics should be tolerated because no class of people is less to be feared.” When they say this, who would think them serious? Yet one leader of that faction expressly wrote as follows: “No class of people is less to be feared. For a person who would rather lose his life than say what he does not think—for he would sin if he did so, and whoever compels him compels him to sin—is not, I believe, to be feared as someone who could be corrupted by money or any other thing. And I for my part am persuaded that no one is more obedient to princes and magistrates than those who simply fear God and remain faithful and con-sistent in what they know.”

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

Who would not think this man speaks as a Christian when he proclaims simplicity of faith and conscience? But if you ask what it is “simply to fear God,” and ask of what conscience he speaks, then at last he must reveal himself. For among these men, as we showed above and they themselves do not deny, “simple” people are those who believe with the Turks and Jews in one God, and further believe that Christ is the Son of God and lord and judge of the world. They willingly remain ignorant of everything else without exception.

But if you ask what conscience is among them, they will answer that it is any cognition or knowledge engraved upon the mind by which our actions are accused or defended. They certainly say something true. Yet when they wish us to measure all our actions by it as a rule, they omit the principal point, as I hope we shall explain.

*[Marginal heading: The distinction between simplicity and ignorance.]*

Therefore, as concerns that first thing which they call “simplicity,” I call it ignorance of the true God. What they call “simply fearing God,”

[PDF page 30 | logical scan 29 | printed page 113]

I call being alienated from God. For I say that anyone who knows God no differently than the Turks and Jews and Christ no differently than the devils is ignorant of God, as we also showed above. Christ, however, wishes us to worship what we know in Spirit and truth, not what we do not know with the Samaritans, since our faith comes not from natural knowledge but from hearing the Word through the Holy Spirit. He wishes us to be not only simple but also prudent. He wishes us to be children in malice, not in understanding. He wishes us to believe in such a way that we understand what we believe and are prepared to answer anyone asking about the hope which is in us. *[Marginal references: John 4:23; Romans 10:17; Matthew 10:16; 1 Corinthians 14:20; 1 Peter 3:15.]*

Yet I do not require the highest or an equal perfection in everyone, since we ought to make daily progress and God’s gifts are various. But I do require knowledge of those principal matters which a person cannot lack and be a Christian. That is, I distinguish simplicity from ignorance and modesty from stupidity, lest those who restore that scholastic doctrine of implicit faith deceive us.

For Clebergius expressly says: “If someone found a wandering sheep and brought it to your house because he thought it was yours, would you not love him because of his good will, even if the sheep were not yours? If you, who are evil, do this, what will God do? Will he not love those who defend in good faith what they think is true, even if perhaps they have erred? Will he not pardon them?” But what if you, Clebergius, brought the sheep not to its owner but to the thief himself, whether through negligence or depravity?

For this is a Muslim doctrine: that everyone is saved in his own religion.
