In those who are outside Christ, however, we cannot recognize that “good faith,” but only that horrible corruption in which we are all born children of wrath and which Christ alone heals in us through his Spirit. Outside Christ there is no salvation, because whoever does not believe in the Son will not see life, but God’s wrath remains upon him. *[Marginal references: Ephesians 2:3; John 3:36.]*

*[Marginal heading: What conscience is, and how far it must be obeyed.]*

I do not at all wonder that these men speak so ignorantly about conscience, since they discuss a matter unknown to them. I answer briefly that I agree with their belief that conscience must not be violated. For I learned from Paul that whatever does not proceed from faith is sin, and that a weak conscience too is defiled. *[Marginal references: Romans 14:23; 1 Corinthians 8:7.]* But I deny that from these facts there follow the two assertions they make: namely, that whatever is done from conscience is right, and that everyone should be left to his own conscience.

For if a person who follows conscience as his guide cannot err, let them say what kind of zeal it was by which Paul persecuted Christ. If it was not vicious, why does he cry out that he was a blasphemer and unworthy to be called an apostle because he persecuted God’s church? *[Marginal references: 1 Timothy 1:13; 1 Corinthians 15:9.]* Why does the Lord pray for those by whom he was crucified, if those who do not think they sin commit no sin? *[Marginal reference: Luke 23:34.]* Is this not, I ask, to restore the “good intentions,” as they call them, of the papists? Is it not to establish the Libertines’ doctrine of opinion?

“But,” Clebergius will say, “I do not deny that they sin; I say that they are pardoned.” Nor do we deny that pardon is granted, but with the condition added that they have put on Christ through faith. I mean the faith proper to Christians, which these men certainly do not acknowledge. For unless conscience learns from God’s Word, not from natural knowledge—which as regards the true worship of God is even hostile to God—what pleases God, what remains except for it to plunge us with itself into destruction? Do we suppose that Christ will judge the world according to our opinions rather than according to the rule of his own Word? Therefore, before we measure our actions by the standard of conscience, conscience itself must first be measured by God’s Word.

For the Lord requires not merely a conscience, but a good one. The apostle explains a good conscience to be not one buried in the darkness of ignorance, which these men call simplicity, but one arising from unfeigned faith, so that these four—God’s Word, knowledge, faith, and a good conscience—cohere, joined to one another by the closest bond. This is the conscience in which the apostle boasts; this is the conscience he requires and calls the end of the commandment. *[Marginal references: 2 Corinthians 1:12; 1 Timothy 1:5.]* Therefore the “simplicity” and “conscience” of these men are nothing but masks which they have put on to deceive the unwary.

Now, with these masks removed, let us consider from what they finally reason that heretics are the least to be feared of all people: “Because,” they say, “those who would rather die than say what they do not think are the most obedient of all.” Remarkable obedience indeed: to be so enslaved to your opinion that, even when convicted by God’s Word, you would rather allow yourself to be killed than change your view—as though there were no difference between constancy and obstinacy.

The magistrate orders the citizens to take up arms. The Anabaptist will resist and will die rather than cease shouting that Christians may not wage war. In this way a sedition will be stirred up and, insofar as it lies with him, the whole commonwealth will be overthrown. Is this not an outstanding example of obedience? The magistrate will see that God’s Word is faithfully taught in his jurisdiction. Everyone will assent. At length some madman will arise, nevertheless feigning simplicity and piety, and attack the principal doctrines of religion. After he has been duly warned privately and publicly and finally restrained by ecclesiastical dis-cipline, he will regard all these things as nothing. If you present God’s Word, he will either ridicule it, shamelessly slander it, or offer some allegedly revealed interpretation. If you oppose to him the church’s consensus, he will say that everyone except himself alone is wrong; and the more practiced he is at dissembling, or the more blinded by God’s just judgment, the more he will complain that violence is being done to him.

[PDF page 31 | logical scan 30 | printed page 114]

Finally, to all the arguments of everyone and the clearest testimonies of Scripture he will oppose only an invincible obstinacy. He will disturb the church, attach disciples to himself, plot against faithful ministers, and despise ecclesiastical discipline. If he is corrected by the magistrate, he will boast of martyrdom. I do not now ask what should be done with such a person. I ask whether he is obedient, and not rather a rebel against God and human beings; whether he is not so much more to be feared by the commonwealth than thieves and murderers as the one who kills a citizen’s soul sins more than one who kills his body; whether he is not more deadly than any plague and pestilence, since the Spirit expressly testifies to the church at Pergamum that he was hostile to it because it had certain Nicolaitans, and to the church at Thyatira because it allowed Jezebel to seduce God’s servants. *[Marginal references: Revelation 2:15, 20.]*

They therefore can no longer deny that, since God’s wrath is customarily most gravely kindled because of heresies—indeed, although heresies are necessary so that the elect may be proved, they are nevertheless manifest testimonies of God’s wrath—no class of people in the world is more to be feared and should be driven with greater care and concern from the society of other people, and indeed sometimes from life itself. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 11:19.]*

But if we follow the contrary doctrine, heretics will not merely be people who must not be expelled or even feared; they must in fact be decorated with every highest reward, as people who display uncorrupted obedience among the other citizens. To such a degree have these perverse advocates of mercy cast off all shame and humanity. I say that they have cast off humanity because whoever thinks that the most bitter enemies of human salvation should not merely be left unrestrained but even honored must necessarily be possessed by the greatest hatred of humankind.

Tenth argument: From the example of Christ’s mercy and kindness.

Why say more? These men even set Christ himself before us, so that we may cherish heretics after his example. Here they plainly triumph and stir up astonishing tragedies.

“When I consider Christ’s character and doctrine,” Bellius says, “who, although innocent, always pardoned the guilty and commanded that pardon be granted even seventy times seven, I do not see how we can maintain the name of Christians unless we imitate his mercy.”

And a little afterward: “It would therefore be advisable that, amid so great a multitude of sins by which we are all overwhelmed, each person should return to himself and be concerned with correcting his own life, not with probing other people’s lives.”

Again elsewhere: “Who would not think that Christ is some Moloch or some god of that kind if he wished living people to be sacrificed and burned to him? Who would wish to serve Christ on the condition that, if amid so many controversies he differs on any point from those who hold power over others, he should be burned alive by Christ’s own command, more cruelly than in the bull of Phalaris, even if in the midst of the flames he loudly proclaims Christ and shouts with full voice that he believes in him?

“Imagine that Christ himself is present as judge, pronouncing sentence and applying the torches. Who would not regard Christ as Satan? For what else could Satan do than burn those who invoke Christ’s name?

“O Christ, creator and king of the world, do you see these things? Have you become so different, so savage, so contrary to yourself? When you were on earth, nothing was meeker than you, nothing more merciful, nothing more patient of injury. Like a sheep before its shearer, you did not even mutter. Cut by scourges, spat upon, mocked, crowned with thorns, and crucified between thieves with the greatest disgrace, you prayed for those who inflicted all these insults upon you. Have you now changed so much?

“I implore you by your Father’s holy name: do you command those who do not understand your ordinances and commands as our masters require to be drowned in water, cut with scourges down to their entrails, then sprinkled with salt, mutilated by iron, roasted over a slow fire, and tortured with every kind of punishment for as long as possible? Christ, do you command and approve these things? Are your vicars those who perform these sacrifices? Do you come when summoned to this disemboweling, and do you feed upon human flesh? If you, Christ, do these things or command them to be done, what have you left for the devil to do? Do you perform the same deeds as Satan? O blasphemies! O criminal audacity of men who dare attribute to Christ things done by the instigation and command of Satan!”

Reader, I have transcribed all these things from Bellius’s own preface so that it may be clear I do these men no injustice.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

Who, on hearing these things, does not seem to hear a pastor so solicitous for the flock’s salvation that he forgets himself? Yet, to say nothing else, those profane comparisons of Christ with Moloch and Satan and that rash adjuration of Christ by his Father’s holy name betray a rash and inconsiderate zeal. But come, let these things be conceded to men who wish everything to be permissible for themselves. First, however, let them know that what they boast concerning cruelty does not pertain to us, since by God’s grace we neither regard as heretics everyone who disagrees with us on some matter nor usurp power over oth-ers. We have neither burned alive—nor even regarded as a heretic—anyone who invoked Christ’s name.

[PDF page 32 | logical scan 31 | printed page 115]

As regards Servetus, whom they themselves do not dare name: with what shamelessness do they say that in the midst of the flames he loudly proclaimed Christ and shouted with full voice that he believed in him, when no prayers of the church could extract from him as he was about to die an acknowledgment that Christ was the eternal Son of God, and no adjurations or almost tears of the ministers present could induce him to take thought for himself?

For even at the very point of death he could not forget the tricks he had practiced throughout his life. Thus when he was diligently admonished to give some sign of repentance, he said: “I confess that Christ is the Son of the eternal God.” But when his deceit was exposed and he was asked to acknowledge not only that Christ was the Son of the eternal God, but also that the Son of God was eternal, he preferred to fall silent rather than give the Son of God the honor owed him—partly disturbed by the horror of death, about which he had never before thought seriously, and partly, as I judge, struck by God’s just judgment.

Shall we think that Christ is invoked by people of this kind? Far be it. On the contrary, as often as they pronounce Christ’s name, so often the Son of God is blasphemed by them, since they stubbornly embrace not Christ as he is and as he declares himself to us in his Word, but an idol in place of the true and only Christ, fashioned as they imagined him. Otherwise, there has never been any heretic who did not use Christ’s name as a pretext for his errors and boast that he built upon Christ. But they did so in vain, since by rejecting Christ’s doctrine in reality they repudiated Christ himself.

This accusation of cruelty therefore has nothing to do with us, unless it was savagery, after lawful and calm examination from God’s Word, after frequently repeated exhortations, and after all gentler remedies had been applied in vain, to punish with a just penalty Anabaptists who overturn the whole order, both civil and ecclesiastical—though this has never happened in this church—and that blasphemous and impious Servetus, whose impiety could not be restrained over a period of thirty years. Unless, I say, they call it savagery that a courageous and steadfast magistrate is deterred by no threats from defending the churches committed to him, and that the godly pastors of these churches keep watch against those ravenous and famished wolves.

These accusations therefore fit the Turks and papists, who, destitute of God’s Word, persecute with mere force, most cruelly, not only heretics but true Christians. But let us come to the point. They set before us Christ, the Son of God, as an example. Quite rightly. For we do not doubt that he is the most perfect and absolute example.

*[Marginal heading: How Christ is set before us for imitation.]*

But how Christ is to be imitated by us is precisely the question. They observe no distinction here. We, however, apply different distinctions because we consider Christ in different respects. Insofar as he is mediator between God and humankind, he had a peculiar office which inheres in him alone and which he most abundantly fulfilled. If anyone wishes to imitate him in this respect, besides attempting it in vain, he will rightly be regarded as foolish and insane.

Next, insofar as he was the minister who explained the will of God the Father, he possessed a power which he shares with no human being: namely, to establish the new covenant after abrogating the old, and to bind consciences by his own authority. Neither prophets nor apostles were permitted to do this except insofar as they were God’s interpreters.

Further, the highest fullness was in him. In us, however, the gifts of the Spirit are neither so numerous nor so great. Thus he did many things which he himself neither requires us to imitate nor, even if we wished, can we imitate: for example, fasting forty days and nights and healing every kind of illness. For these and similar things are not necessarily and perpetually joined with the ministry of the Word.

Moreover, he accommodated his ministry to certain people and also to a certain time. To that extent it is not at all necessary for ministers to imitate many of his unique deeds, since while living on earth Christ was a minister of the circumcision. *[Marginal reference: Romans 15:8.]*

But insofar as he was the most perfect example of obedience, I confess that every person, of whatever rank and condition, should look to him alone as the one goal and end. Thus, just as in everything he placed the glory of God the Father before all other things and then strove in every way to benefit even the most hostile people, so everyone is bound to do—but not in one and the same way, since functions in the church are diverse.

The magistrate, therefore, will imitate Christ first if he applies whatever means of protection he has received from the Lord to the care and preservation of true religion; and next if he removes to the best of his ability everything that prevents his subjects from peacefully and quietly exercising the mutual duties of love. Thus, when he punishes criminals and decides controversies, directing everything to the preservation of God’s honor and the common-wealth, he will be said to imitate Christ even though Christ did not condemn the adulteress because he was not a judge, and refused to be an arbiter in dividing an inheritance.

[PDF page 33 | logical scan 32 | printed page 116]

Likewise, ministers imitate Christ by baptizing and restraining the obstinate through ecclesiastical discipline, even though Christ is not recorded as having done either. Finally, all private persons who, according to their calling, assist the commonwealth in reverence and fear of God imitate Christ. But if, on the contrary, a prince acquits someone convicted of a crime, refuses to divide an inheritance, or wishes to teach in the church, he will not imitate Christ, although Christ did all these things.

The same must be understood concerning the other orders, none of which Christ exercised. He is set before us for imitation to this extent: each person is bound to set before himself, in proportion to his calling, not every individual deed of Christ but his absolutely perfect obedience in general. If, as these men seem to wish, nothing is to be done which Christ did not do, why do magistrates hang thieves? Why do they decide controversies? Why may Christians litigate? Why does Peter kill people by a word? Why does Paul blind a man? Why does that same Paul appeal to Caesar, when Christ did not even mutter before Pilate? *[Marginal references: Acts 5, 13, 25.]*

There is also another matter of the greatest importance in this controversy which these men do not consider. For they estimate Christ’s kingdom and nature only from that interval of time in which he lived on earth in the manner of men, as though Christ did not even today reign in the individual members of the church whenever they duly perform the office enjoined upon them by God.

What then? When a magistrate, after invoking Christ’s name, delivers some criminal into the executioner’s hands, shall we not regard it as though Christ himself does this? When ministers in his name and from his Word duly teach, rebuke, and console the people, shall we not judge that Christ speaks in their person?

Why, then, do they set Christ’s nature before our eyes? Is punishing the wicked abhorrent to Christ’s nature? On the contrary, did he not establish the magistrate in his church precisely so that first the ministry of the Word might be defended by his lawful protection, and then so that Christ himself might punish criminals through the magistrate? Otherwise, why must the magistrate be obeyed not only because of wrath but also because of conscience? *[Marginal reference: Romans 13:5.]*

“But Christ was most merciful.” Who among us does not condemn savagery and cruelty? “But Christ was most patient.” I acknowledge it; yet he is a just judge against those who store up wrath for themselves. *[Marginal reference: Romans 2:5.]* “But Christ, cut by scourges, spat upon, mocked, and crucified, did not even mutter.” I acknowledge it, and would that more people imitated this patience under private injuries.

Nevertheless, that same Christ once seized a whip. When the appointed time for vengeance arrived, what a horrifying example of his wrath, I ask, did he display—and does he still display today—against the Jews? *[Marginal reference: John 2:15.]* What? To pass over the disasters of the Jews long since past, what else are that Turkish devastation and papal tyranny but the terrible wrath of Christ avenging contempt for his Word? Is it not God who daily vindicates his majesty by the devastation and destruction of countless kings, princes, peoples, and regions?

I do not exempt from guilt the tyrants by whom these things have often been done. Indeed, I have no doubt that they wickedly carried out the things which God had justly decreed. But tyrants alone did not do these things; faithful servants of God also did them, and by God’s command. I infer from this only that these men act wrongly when they refuse to distinguish public vindication performed by God’s authority from private vengeance, and cruelty from just judgment.

What an elegant consequence this is, I ask! Christ, sent here to wash away our sins, resisted no injuries and did not appeal to the magistrate against heretics. Therefore it is unlawful for the magistrate to punish self-condemned heretics by whom Christ’s majesty is profaned, the church disturbed, and souls led away to destruction. Therefore ministers who have tried everything in vain may not ask the magistrate somehow to restrain a famished wolf who is moved less by the ministers’ voice than a stone.

For if this is done, these men will immediately cry out that Christ did not do it and that we make Christ into Moloch and Satan. Indeed, they will ask Christ himself, if he does this, what he leaves for the devil to do. Blasphemous and shameless mouth!

Certainly God caused Dathan and Abiram to be swallowed alive because of their sedition, so that he might avenge the injury done to himself in the person of Moses. He burned Aaron’s sons alive because they brought strange fire, so that he might declare how great the authority of his commands is. Finally, he did not pardon our sins unless they were first expiated by the punishment of the cross in his Son, as he had decreed from eternity. He was not on that account Moloch or the devil—something I cannot contemplate without horror—but the most just vindicator of his own majesty. *[Marginal references: Numbers 16; Leviticus 10:2.]*

Nor is Christ transformed on that account, whose dreadful judgments against the Jews—at least those inflexible and obstinate despisers of his Word—we still behold and shall one day behold more fully, when he sentences the reprobate to eternal pun-ishments.

[PDF page 34 | logical scan 33 | printed page 117]

Eleventh argument: That magistrates ordinarily do not punish many other people, however wicked.

These men further say: “If we allow not only Turks and Jews to live among us but also tolerate revilers, the proud, the avaricious, the envious, the unchaste, drunkards, and other kinds of wicked people of this sort, and live, feast, and rejoice with them, we ought at least to allow this common air and breath to those who profess the same name of Christ with us, harm no one, and have such a mind that they would rather die than say or do anything other than what they judge ought to be said or done.”

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

First of all, even if I concede that most magistrates are too negligent in punishing all these crimes, is it rightly inferred from this that impunity should also be granted to heretics? Certainly, what else does the person who asks this request from the magistrate than what the poet says: that anyone who has once crossed the bounds of modesty should be thoroughly and vigorously shameless—that is, should become worst from bad?

Next, what if I answer, as is true, that heretics profess Christ’s name emptily and spread this shadow of piety as a pretext for their obstinacy; that they are more destructive than Turks and Jews by as much as everyone fears feigned friendship more than open hatred; and that they are more justly punished than any proud, avaricious, unchaste, envious, or drunken people by as much as it is a greater crime to scorn God’s own majesty than to commit some offense against oneself or one’s neighbor, and to sin in doctrine rather than in morals?

But we are not compelled to seek even these defenses. For by what right, finally, would a magistrate punish Turks, Jews, or others who openly reject the Christian faith? Only if perhaps someone had deliberately attacked Christ with some outstanding blasphemy, for this manifest insult to Christ is certainly intolerable to a Christian magistrate within his jurisdiction; or if someone knowingly and consciously defected from Christians to the Turks or Jews, for such an impious and blasphemous apostate is rightly punished by his prince; or if someone were found soliciting Christians among whom he lives to defect, for whoever does this is rightly punished according to the gravity of the offense; or finally, if someone stubbornly opposed a just edict of the magistrate, for he should be punished as seditious.

The magistrate is also bound to repel injuries against Turks and anyone else, even by the protection of arms when it cannot be done otherwise. But the case of heretics is different: they are domestic enemies and maliciously afflict God’s church. For willful ignorance and obstinate devotion to factions are, especially in a cause of religion, the surest proofs of the highest depravity.

As for the others with whom they falsely and spitefully say that we live, feast, and rejoice, I answer briefly that the magistrate punishes not bare vices but only those which have erupted into crimes, since he cannot judge hidden things. Nevertheless, insofar as possible, he ought to administer the polity so that those vices from which crimes must afterward arise are also rooted out. Xenophon indeed rightly praises the Persians because they were more concerned to train citizens so that they would abhor crimes than to punish criminals. *[Marginal source: Xenophon, Cyropaedia, book 1.]*

Thus, in Christian churches schools have been established in which children are trained first of all in good and sincere religion. Then the catechism is explained. Besides public sermons in which everyone is openly instructed, admonished, stirred up, and rebuked, provision has also been made before the presbytery, in which those who depart from the duty of Christians are more severely admonished or even restrained by ecclesiastical discipline from God’s Word.

But if vices produce some crime—for example, if a reviler or buffoon injures another’s reputation, if a drunkard strikes someone, if a proud person injures another, if an avaricious person diminishes someone’s property by evil arts, or if a lustful person fornicates or corrupts another man’s wife—excellent and most holy laws have been written according to whose rule the magistrate may punish these crimes.

What, therefore, do these men rebuke here? We showed above that heresy is numbered by us among crimes, and indeed among the gravest. Thus we may turn this same argument back against them: since even the smallest injuries are customarily punished by the magistrate, no reason can be given why self-condemned heretics should not be severely punished.

Twelfth argument: That no one can or should be compelled to faith.

There remains, unless I am mistaken, one argument which the adversaries oppose: “Faith wishes to be persuaded,” they say, “not compelled.”

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

We certainly acknowledge this to be so. This is not the place to debate free choice, and we have learned even from Epictetus’s Handbook that opinion and desire are in our power. We also rightly rebuke the cruelty of the Turks and papists, who attempt to establish their superstitions by naked force. But how do these things pertain to our cause?

[PDF page 35 | logical scan 34 | printed page 118]

For who is so ignorant as to suppose, when the magistrate punishes a heretic, that he does it specifically in order to compel the man by force to reform? Certainly, if this were the magistrate’s proposed goal, he would establish capital punishment against no one, however wicked.

“But,” these men will say, “a case of religion, in which someone can easily go astray, must be separated from crimes about which no one can doubt.” First of all, I ask them from what teacher they have learned to distinguish a violation of the divine majesty so subtly from other crimes that they think it does not pertain to the magistrate. We shall debate this later in its proper place.

Next, I say that we are not discussing those who are weak in faith—for we have shown in deed that these people must be taught, not punished, although their weakness must not be fostered—but self-condemned heretics, that is, inflexible and factious domestic enemies of the church. These, therefore, are the people against whom inquiry must be made, certainly not in order to extort a feigned profession or repentance, but so that magistrates may truly serve God, whose ministers they are, and kiss the Son. That is, they are to avenge an insolent injury done to God’s majesty, whose vindicators they are in this world, and preserve the external polity of doctrine and morals.

This cannot be done unless its obstinate disturbers are restrained, so that, when necessary, they may provide for the safety of the whole body by cutting off the putrid member; so that, by setting an example in one person, they may keep the rest in duty; and so that, if there are any from whom neither threats nor entreaties can obtain agreement with the others according to God’s Word, they may at least by the power they have from God extort from them either that they move elsewhere or that they do not corrupt the whole lump with a little leaven. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 5:6.]*

Do these things really seem so slight that they supply no just cause for punishing heretics? Or is it just to grant in sacred matters a license for insolence which no one except someone utterly mad and insane has ever approved in civil affairs?

Moreover, although people are inclined to every kind of error, I readily confess that they go astray in religion—not only because the natural man does not know the things of God and the disposition of the flesh is enmity against God, but also because even the saints themselves know only in part. Yet just as we tolerate simple ignorance for as long as obstinacy, a factious disposition, and the other characteristics common to all heretics are absent, so we do not think those people should be tolerated who willingly remain ignorant of what a Christian must know and confess and who also lead others into error. *[Marginal references: 2 Peter 3:5; 2 Timothy 3:8.]*

What of the fact that among Anabaptists and Libertines it is questioned whether thefts, adulteries, and murders are crimes? Shall we therefore also say that they err in good faith and consequently must not be punished, lest they be compelled to better conduct?

I pass over the fact that Augustine, taught by experience itself, so often testifies against the Donatists and Circumcellions that most people have such a disposition that nothing keeps them in duty better than severity; so much so that they afterward willingly avoid the very thing which at first they avoided from fear of punishment, and testify that the former harshness was most salutary to them. Therefore, writing to Boniface, Augustine rightly concludes that pastoral diligence includes restoring to the Lord’s fold, by the terrors or even the pains of whips if they resist, even those sheep which had not been violently seized but gently seduced away from the flock and had begun to be possessed by strangers.

Does not the Lord himself use this method in chastising his children, reforming Phrygians with blows but moderately restraining good children when they misbehave? If there is no use for rods, why does the church loose and bind? Is it to make hypocrites? That is what the fanatic Montfortius says, pretending that Augustine did not foresee this difficulty.

But Paul acts very differently. Not content with the doctrine of the Word, he exercises that other faculty of his by delivering the incestuous man to Satan—not so that the man would pretend to repent, but so that his spirit might be saved in the day of the Lord Jesus, a hope which did not disappoint him. *[Marginal references: 2 Corinthians 6:2; 1 Corinthians 5:4; 2 Corinthians 2:7.]* If it is objected that Paul treats morals and not doctrine, I could indeed appeal to that division of Bellius which we refuted at the beginning and by which he also makes some people heretics in morals. But I answer that those who think this chastisement pertained only to those who erred in morals go very far astray.

For we also have a notable example of the same thing in Hymenaeus and Alexander, because they made shipwreck of the faith and overturned the faith of some by saying that the resurrection had already occurred. Paul therefore delivers them to Satan because they were heretics—not in order to inflict force upon their conscience, which was very evil, as is plainly the conscience of all heretics, but so that they might learn not to blaspheme. *[Marginal reference: 1 Timothy 1:18.]*

Therefore, when they establish that “religious toleration” of theirs, wishing no one to be compelled but everyone left to his own opinion, they cannot deny that they are directly opposed to Paul’s doc-trine.

[PDF page 36 | logical scan 35 | printed page 119]

What that Eleutherius says—responding perfectly to his own name—is this: “Christians are called free and under their own authority because their business proceeds from no coercion of any law, but from the free impulse of the Spirit himself, who works faith in them, teaches, and kindles love.” As this statement is true in itself, so, unless it is explained by a suitable interpretation, it is the foundation of all heresies.

For there is no nation of heretics which does not boast of God’s Spirit, to such a degree that some even repudiate the Word. If some embrace Scripture, they do so in order to deceive the simple. But the true church of God, of which we do not doubt we are members, joins the Spirit, Word, and faith to one another by such a perpetual bond that from the Word it discerns both the Spirit of God from the spirit of Satan and the world, and faith from opinion and empty imagination.

We therefore say that all who were elected before the foundation of the world are drawn so effectually by this Spirit, freely given, that, released from sin, they begin to understand, will, and accomplish the things of God. For those who are led by God’s Spirit and joined to Christ through faith are children of God. The law no longer terrifies them, but teaches them graciously and with the greatest fruit—yet in such a way that a great and most bitter struggle still remains until they are fully delivered from this body of death through death itself. *[Marginal references: Romans 7; 8:14; 7:24.]* These are the boundaries by which we define and circumscribe Christian liberty from God’s Word. How does this serve to confirm that license of spirits and introduce the greatest disorder?

If they deny that the examples we adduced pertain to the civil magistrate and cry out that chastising is one thing and killing another, I certainly concede both points, but recall them as they run beyond the stated question. For we are not asking here whether this punishment of heretics pertains to the civil magistrate, nor whether it is permissible to kill heretics; we shall treat those things individually in their proper place. The question is whether it is unlawful to punish heretics because in this way some force seems to be inflicted upon their consciences, while God, as that Eleutherius says, wishes to have a free and uncoerced heart.

They say this is unlawful. I affirm that it occurs necessarily and usefully in God’s church and has always occurred. Finally, to make an end of discussing this part of our question, I answer in Augustine’s own words: “As for what seems true to you, that the unwilling should not be compelled to truth: you err, knowing neither the Scriptures nor the power of God, who makes them willing while they are compelled unwillingly.” *[Marginal source: Augustine, Against Gaudentius, second letter, book 2, chapter 17.]*

The arguments by which the adversaries teach that the punishment of heretics does not pertain to the civil magistrate.

These, then, are those strongest arguments—as many as could be gathered from Bellius’s medley—by which these men thought God’s church could be persuaded to regard heretics as sacred and inviolable. Now let us hear by what arguments they contend that, even if they conceded to us that heretics should be punished, this punishment nevertheless does not pertain to the civil magistrate.

First argument: Christ’s spiritual kingdom is not of this world. Paul also says that the weapons of our warfare are not carnal.

“It is absurd,” they say, “that a spiritual war should be waged with earthly weapons. The war is Christ’s and must be waged with Christ’s weapons. The Lord testifies that his kingdom is not of this world.” *[Marginal reference: John 18:36.]*

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

First I ask these men whether they truly confess that Christ is lord and judge of the world. If they believe this, I ask with what shamelessness they circumscribe the principal power of the world within such narrow bounds that they wholly separate it from religion, as though the magistrate’s sword were not God’s sword, whose minister he is. *[Marginal reference: Romans 13:4.]*

We indeed acknowledge that the duties of ecclesiastical office and the civil magistrate are distinct. But although they are different, are they therefore contrary to one another as the spirit is opposed to the flesh and the world? Do they not rather reach one and the same goal as though by different paths?

When these men explain in this way the passage in which Christ testifies that his kingdom is not of this world, so that they consequently judge that the civil magistrate must be removed from the care of religion, they attempt to wrest from us a most beautiful testimony of Christ, one full of the highest consolation, and to deprive the church of a most useful and, whenever God has so willed, plainly necessary protection.

For if they take “kingdom” to mean that which is possessed by a king, all authority in heaven and on earth has certainly been given to Christ. Whoever is joined to Christ through faith becomes a participant in that right, however abject and despised he may seem. This is also a distinguished consolation of believers in this world: although the rest enjoy created things only through tyranny and robbery, those who in Christ have recovered the right they lost in Adam have all things clean and holy through God’s Word and prayers. *[Marginal references: Matthew 28:18; 1 Timothy 4:5.]* Yet because the world does not acknowledge this dominion of Christ—that is, unbelievers and hypocrites do not—and because that heavenly kingdom in which Christ now reigns, to which he calls us, and in which in his own time he will subject the remaining enemies under his feet is by far the most excellent, Christ, considering his present condition and that of his people, when charged with the crime of violating Caesar’s majesty, testified that he was indeed a king, but in his church, not in the world—or rather, not on earth but in heaven.

[PDF page 37 | logical scan 36 | printed page 120]

Yet this too must be understood so that we know that even now he rules over devils and the impious against their will and despite their futile resistance, and that whenever he has pleased he has adorned his church even with external dominion. So far is the administration of the civil magistrate from being alien to Christ’s kingdom.

But if by the term “kingdom” they understand the manner of reigning itself—to say nothing here of unbelieving tyrants—we have often said that civil and ecclesiastical jurisdiction must be distinguished. Yet their inference that the magistrate, even if Christian, must be so removed from the church’s affairs that he cannot punish heretics is no less absurd than if someone reasoned that the eye does not need the aid of hands or feet because these members have distinct functions.

This reasoning is inept because all the members, although distinct in their uses, nevertheless look to the preservation and safety of one body. The same is true of the church, for whose preservation both civil authority and ecclesiastical ministry were established by God. Yet whenever God has pleased, he has preserved the church nevertheless when civil authority not merely failed to assist but resisted, and when, as happened in recent years, the public ministry was virtually extinguished—just as he preserves many people safe and alive in this life although blind and lame.

Christ therefore speaks not insofar as he is the eternal God, ruling and administering all things, but with regard to that office which he exercised among human beings and still exercises today as the one head of the church and the high priest who reigns inwardly. For it is true, Montfortius, despite your most unjust slander, that the church assumes nothing proper to the magistrate and that the magistrate does not accomplish what is performed by the church.

Thus Christ openly acknowledges before Pilate that he is king, but in such a way that he makes no attempt against Caesar’s majesty, which itself is from God, and detracts nothing from the king’s authority, to which he himself did not refuse voluntarily to submit.

There are also other reasons why Christ’s kingdom is said not to be of this world: namely, the reasons why believers are said not to be of the world and Christ himself does not pray for the world. The kings of the world fill the earth with mutual slaughter and plunder in order to satisfy their lust. Christ, however, conquers tyrants, even in our own age, by suffering. Kings rely on human defenses. Although Christ uses the ministry of human beings to propagate and preserve his kingdom insofar as he knows it expedient, he is so far from relying on those defenses that instead he usually chooses the most abject people by whom he defeats the most powerful princes. The kings of the world look to the advantages of this present life. Although Christ adds all things necessary to those who seek God’s kingdom, he does not keep us on earth, but rather raises us through the cross into heaven. *[Marginal reference: Matthew 6:33.]*

Christ therefore rightly testifies that his kingdom is not of this world. But how does this pertain to the matter we are discussing? For it is plainly most unjust to measure the magistrate’s power by the practice of tyrants. Although Christ is attacked by the world and conquers by suffering, it by no means follows that, if a godly magistrate has been granted to the church by God, he has no part in defending the ministry of the Word against inflexible heretics.

Although Christ’s kingdom does not rest upon human aids, it does not follow that, if they are offered by the Lord, they must be rashly rejected, or that ministers of the Word act outside their office when, after the example of the ancient church, they seek the lawful protection of a Christian magistrate in order to vindicate God’s majesty and the authority of God’s Word—not cruelly, as these men slanderously claim, but with just severity—against those for whom it is sport maliciously to afflict God’s church and tear truly faithful pastors of faithful churches to pieces with slanders. Their aim is to attack the church’s flock when deprived of the protection of its dogs, just as Philip once attacked the Athenians. *[Marginal source: Council of Carthage.]*

Moreover, from what we have said it can readily be understood how the apostle’s statement that the weapons of his warfare are not carnal but divinely powerful should be understood: namely, that he relies not on human but on divine power. *[Marginal reference: 2 Corinthians 10:4.]* Certainly no general conclusion can be drawn from this as though God, in propagating the Gospel, never requires any of the things by which earthly kingdoms are preserved. Otherwise, by the same reasoning, together with the magistrate’s external protection, the outward preaching of the Word and knowledge of languages and the liberal arts would also be banished from the church; as useless, or at least not so necessary.

[PDF page 38 | logical scan 37 | printed page 121]

Indeed, we have heard one member of this faction continually say, “Christians ought to read only the Bible.” When someone happened to ask why, although he held this view, he had a few days before so eagerly accepted a public office teaching the Greek authors, the man replied, “As though shoemakers were accustomed to sew shoes for themselves rather than for others every day.” I have chosen to recount this incidentally so that everyone may know clearly what judgment these men have about the greatest matters. But let us return to the subject and hear a certain unpublished enthymeme of Clebergius too.

Second argument: Theologians can defend their doctrine without the magistrate’s protection, since the other arts can do so.

“Be content,” says Clebergius, addressing the magistrates, “with the sword God delivered to you. Punish robbers, punish traitors, false witnesses, and the others of that kind. As regards religion, defend the godly against the injuries of others. This is your office. The doctrine of theology is not to be handled by your sword. Otherwise, if theologians obtain from you that you handle their doctrine with arms, a physician will rightly be able to demand the same—that you defend him with arms against the opinions of other physicians. The dialectician will demand the same, as will the orator and the other arts. If you cannot handle those arts with the sword, neither can you handle theology, since it consists in words and spirit no less than they do. If a good physician can sufficiently defend his doctrine by knowledge without the magistrate’s protection, why cannot a theologian do the same?”

So far Clebergius. I principally rebuke three things in his argument.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

First, he assumes the very point in question. Second, he exempts heretics from the class of robbers, traitors, and false witnesses, although these names fit them much better than any other people, however wicked.

For what sacrilege is greater than fraudulently leading innocent souls away and putting them up for sale, even peddling God’s Word itself? What treason is more faithless than pretending to be the church’s closest friend while, insofar as lies with you, exposing it to be afflicted and plundered by Satan? What falsehood is greater or more dangerous than corrupting by the falsest interpretations the records of both covenants and of eternal life? And yet, if Clebergius is to be believed, heretics inflict no injury upon godly people.

Indeed, if any godly minister seeks the magistrate’s lawful protection against their audacity, which repeated admonitions have kindled rather than extinguished, he will immediately be called cruel and savage.

Third, he spitefully slanders us as though we judged that Christian doctrine should be handled by arms and iron. From where—or from what deed of ours—could he not merely infer, but even suspect this lie? For we have never wished the Christian law to be established or propagated by arms, but to be diligently and faithfully explained from God’s Word alone. Yet while other crimes are punished by the magistrate’s authority, we do not wish heretics more execrable than any thieves, adulterers, robbers, and even parricides to ravage God’s church with impunity under the guise of simplicity and mercy.

Since experience itself teaches that these men are not moved at all by the church’s judgments, we therefore appeal to the magistrate’s lawful power, as Augustine says to Demetrius, not so that we may persecute them, but so that we may be protected by God’s lawful defense. Nor, as the same Augustine writes to Boniface when speaking of Maximianus of Vaga, is it for the sake of avenging ourselves, but of protecting the church which God has entrusted to us.

As for the enthymeme itself, although, I think, no one is ignorant that when Clebergius compares the other disciplines with theology, he draws the most unjust comparison between utterly dissimilar things, I nevertheless do not hesitate also to affirm that, because it greatly concerns the commonwealth that those liberal arts plainly necessary to its safety and adornment should be faithfully taught, care of these matters too pertains especially to the civil magistrate.

Indeed, all good princes who love their citizens have both established schools and embraced professors of the liberal arts with every kind of generosity. Following their example, the most illustrious Commonwealth of Bern a few years ago opened in this city of Lausanne a school of the three languages and all the liberal arts, offering a most honorable salary and writing most holy laws according to whose rule the academy is governed. Nor does this occur only in the no-bler disciplines, but even in the humblest trades of every kind. In well-ordered cities it is customary, by edict of the magistrates, that no one may rashly practice any craft he pleases, but only those whose skill has been examined by the people called masters of the craft.

[PDF page 39 | logical scan 38 | printed page 122]

What, finally, can this man rightly rebuke in these things? Will he not instead be convicted by the example of all good kings? To pass over Constantine, Theodosius, Marcian, and Justinian, whom they accuse of excess: will they also rebuke those whose action is praised by the voice of the Holy Spirit? What then? Did David act beyond his office when he established the orders of Levites? Did Solomon, when he removed Abiathar from the priesthood? Did Asa, when he issued a capital edict against those who would not seek the God of Israel? Did Jehoshaphat, when he sent priests through the individual cities and saw that the people were instructed? Did Hezekiah, when he cleansed the Lord’s house? Did Uzziah, when he compelled all the Israelites to serve the Lord?

Here I know what Montfortius is accustomed to answer: “The sword of the Old Testament was a figure of God’s Word, whose sword was to be employed in the New Testament.” I shall discuss this invention more conveniently elsewhere. For now I give this one answer: Christ detracted nothing at all from the right of the civil magistrate, and we are not asking here whether a magistrate may kill heretics, but whether he may punish them. These merciful Academics deny this while daring to affirm nothing about the other matters. We, however, affirm that this is a principal duty of the magistrate, and that all the godly kings repeatedly performed it, approved by God’s own testimony. Let the church now judge whether these men should be believed rather than the Holy Spirit.

Third argument: From the example of Christ and the apostles.

Let us proceed to another argument of the same Clebergius. “Christ could defend his doctrine without the magistrate’s protection,” he says. “The apostles could do so; their imitators will also be able.”

Montfortius too says: “The magistrate must protect good people against force and injury”—although according to these men heretics inflict no force or injury—“but he must not make people good by force or hand religion over to the sword. Otherwise, it follows that Christ himself was not sufficiently armed and did not sufficiently arm the apostles.”

And a little afterward he says, “Let us be content with those weapons which Christ delivered to his apostles, especially since we continually declare that the Holy Scriptures are perfect and that nothing is to be added to or subtracted from them.”

So say they.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

In order to refute this slander, we first admonish everyone that we are not asking here what Christ, the apostles, or the true church can do. We do not doubt that Christ’s power to preserve his church is infinite, and we understand well enough that not only the apostles but all faithful ministers up to the present, relying upon God’s grace alone, have stood fearless and unconquered against every assault of the world. We are not asking about these things, which are not at all doubtful.

What we have determined to discuss is how Christ wishes the church to be administered: whether by the ministry of the Word alone or also by another external power, whose minister is the magistrate. I therefore say that from the very beginning of the world God established this order in the church: that besides the ministry of the Word it should also be armed against the impious with an external power which we call by the general term “the sword.”

I nevertheless acknowledge that the civil magistrate has not always been the minister of this power and sword in the church. Sometimes, when princes were inactive or attacking God’s church, God sent angels themselves to fulfill this office against the church’s enemies. At other times, by a certain singular privilege, he armed certain ministers of the Word with both ecclesiastical and civil power to defend the godly. The Old Testament supplies us with very many examples of punishments of both kinds, which there is no need to recount.

But because the adversaries evade these examples as temporary, come, let us consider whether since Christ’s advent the church has ever, at any time, lacked the defense of external and civil power, even when it was most cruelly afflicted by the Caesars. Tell me, therefore: by what right did Christ twice seize the whip? By what right did Peter kill Ananias and Sapphira? By what right did Paul blind Elymas? By the right of ecclesiastical ministry? Certainly not, unless you con- *[Marginal references: John 2:15; Matthew 21:12; Acts 5:5; 13:11.]*fuse the jurisdictions. Therefore it was by the right of the civil magistrate, for there is no third alternative.

[PDF page 40 | logical scan 39 | printed page 123]

I acknowledge, however, that this power was extraordinary in ministers of the Word and that the manner of exercising it was plainly divine. Therefore, in bringing these examples forward, I do so not to detract anything from the right of the civil magistrate or add anything to ecclesiastical ministry, but to refute their slander.

For when I show that, although the Lord has not always employed magistrates themselves, he has nevertheless in every age employed for the preservation of his church, as he knew expedient, that power of which the magistrate is the ordinary minister, it appears, unless I am mistaken, that the doctrine of those who invoke the example of Christ and the ancient church to teach that the church ought to be governed by the ministry of the Word alone is false.

To make this clearer, I say that at the very time when those who should have protected Christ’s kingdom openly and avowedly attacked it, the Lord provided for his church in such a way that, despite the futile resistance of the princes of the world, he willed a certain new species of external power and punishment to exist. This is what Paul means by the term “powers” and numbers among the gifts of the Spirit with which the Lord confirmed the church, as it were, at its birth. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 12:10.]*

Against their opinion it follows from this that we see God established this order in the church: in addition to the ministry of the Word, impious and seditious people should also be restrained by examples of external punishments. Those who forbid the magistrate—the ordinary minister of that power—to touch heretics, just as though they were sacred and inviolable by the law of nations, violate the order established by God and always preserved, although in different forms. They therefore, I say, should be severely chastised by the magistrate himself, so that the very order they attack may be confirmed by their just punishment.

To summarize all this briefly: although, as often and for as long as the Lord pleased, magistrates did not assist but attacked the church, their power nevertheless was never lacking to the church. Next, even if I conceded that it was lacking in the time of Christ and the apostles, who except a mad and insane person would derive from this or that period, as regards the church’s external polity, a perpetual rule for administering it? Indeed, what else is doing this than prescribing a fixed rule to Christ?

The apostles were fishermen, and Paul says that God’s kingdom does not consist in speech. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 4:20.]* What then? Shall we not acknowledge eloquence in Chrysostom and singular acuity and skill in the liberal arts in Augustine—to pass over almost countless others most fully equipped with human aids—as splendid instruments of God’s glory in those men?

The church is certainly perpetual and rests on God’s power alone, yet it is propagated by the outward ministry of human beings. According to his infinite wisdom, God manifests this power differently at apportioned moments and times. Thus now the church is raised up and triumphs; now it is cast down and oppressed so that it can almost appear to have perished. Now it wanders; now it remains for a time in a definite place. Sometimes it is cherished by godly princes, whether foreign or domestic; sometimes it is treated most harshly by them.

England today supplies us with the saddest example of this change. Recently it flourished most greatly under that most excellent king Edward; now it is suddenly afflicted and butchered so miserably, cruelly, and savagely that in the space of six months in that one island almost more examples of Scythian savagery and folly have been perpetrated even against the noblest and most innocent women than are read in the whole history of the Turks.

Thus it pleased the Lord to employ the apostles wholly naked and unarmed for that marvelous proclamation of the Gospel, and by them to defeat the most powerful kingdoms of the world. For in this way he wished to manifest to the world the power and excellence of this divine and heavenly doctrine. Afterward, in his own time, he raised up kings by whose protection he might defend his church—not because that spiritual power was insufficient to destroy counsels and strongholds, or because Christ and the apostles were not equipped with sufficiently suitable weapons, as these men slanderously and sophistically infer, but because, whenever the Lord pleased, he also wills his people to be preserved by the external defenses of human beings, so that they may breathe for a time and recognize in reality that the heart of kings is in God’s hand, and so be more greatly strengthened in subsequent afflictions. *[Marginal reference: 2 Corinthians 10:4.]*

Therefore, when the Lord offers these protections to us, we say that they should be sought and embraced as useful and, since God has so willed, plainly necessary to the church, just as eagerly as these men proudly despise and reject them. We embrace them not to compel people to faith—for no one can be compelled to faith—nor because we fear the light, for we do not teach in secret or know how to employ false names, but so that those obstinate despisers of God’s majesty, the ecclesiastical ministry, and even the princes themselves may be subjected to just penalties.

[PDF page 41 | logical scan 40 | printed page 124]

Although these things, unless I am mistaken, are sufficient to refute this argument, I choose nevertheless to append Augustine’s words concerning this same controversy. They are as follows:

“Those who do not wish just laws to be enacted against their impieties say that the apostles did not request such things from earthly kings. They do not consider that the time was different then and that all things suit their own times. For what emperor then believed in Christ, so as to serve piety by enacting laws against impiety? At that time the prophetic statement was still being fulfilled: ‘Why did the nations rage, and the peoples devise vain things?’ What is said a little afterward in the same psalm was not yet being done: ‘And now, kings, understand; be instructed, you who judge the earth. Serve the Lord in fear and exult before him with trembling.’

“How, then, do kings serve in fear except by forbidding and punishing with religious severity the things done against the Lord’s commands? A person serves in one way because he is a man and in another because he is a king. Because he is a man, he serves by living faithfully. Because he is a king, he serves by enacting with suitable force laws that command just things and forbid their opposites. In this, therefore, kings serve the Lord insofar as they are kings, because in serving him they do things which only kings do.” *[Marginal source: Augustine, Epistle 50 to Boniface.]*

So far Augustine. It is certainly astonishing that these men dare misuse his authority in order to deceive the simple and ignorant. From his own words, which agree perfectly with the Council of Carthage at which he was present, it is sufficiently clear that these men attempt nothing which was not long ago rejected by the judgment of the orthodox church. When we seek the lawful aid of the magistrate, established by God, against inflexible and incurable heretics, we do nothing which was not long ago confirmed by God’s Word and the authority of the holy fathers.

Fourth argument: Because the world cannot judge concerning heresy.

Come, let us hear another new argument. “The world,” says Eleutherius, “cannot judge concerning heresy, any more than flesh can judge concerning spirit or darkness concerning light. Therefore the world cannot render judgment about things which it not only does not know, but even mocks as foolishness and censures as falsehood.”

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

Let it be so, Eleutherius. Let the world not only fail to understand but even persecute the things of God. Does it follow that a Christian magistrate may not punish heretics convicted from God’s Word who nevertheless remain obstinate?

We already answered this argument above in its proper place. For we taught that God’s Spirit is by no means idle in the church, but leads the children of God, who are not of this world, into all truth. *[Marginal reference: John 16:13.]* Now, so that everyone may understand that this argument is also empty in this respect—that is, as regards the magistrate’s office—I answer that investigating heresies is one thing and investigating heretics another; investigating blasphemy is one thing and investigating a blasphemer another.

When there is doubt about heresy or blasphemy, the subject of inquiry is the matter itself, that is, some article of religion. Since religion is written in the records of both covenants and explaining them belongs to the ecclesiastical office, the church must, as place and time and the nature of the business require, duly and calmly decide the controversy from God’s Word and employ every effort so that the person in error may understand his error and repent.

The magistrate’s office, however, is to investigate the blasphemer and heretic himself and, according to the gravity of the crime, to avenge both the violation of divine majesty arising from depravity or willful ignorance and the contempt of ecclesiastical discipline. Thus we entrust judgment of heresy not to the world but to God’s Word, while we entrust the investigation of heretics to God’s minister.

I acknowledge that in this matter most kings and princes customarily sin gravely. Yet their example should not deter other godly and truly Christian princes from their duty. On the contrary, as the saying goes, “One must pay no heed to him, but go straight at him.”

For today, in cutting apart Christ’s members, the same judicial procedure is customarily employed which was observed in crucifying Christ the head. The Jews deliver Christ to Pilate, partly from envy and partly because they had zeal, but not according to knowledge. Pilate, thinking it no concern of his when they truthfully cried, “According to our law he ought to die because he made himself the Son of God,” later, when another charge of seeking kingship was brought, fears Caesar’s anger and adjudges Christ to the demand of those by whom he knew Christ had been delivered through envy.

[PDF page 42 | logical scan 41 | printed page 125]

So too today, if anyone has dared even mutter against the blasphemies of the Roman Antichrist, he is immediately arrested. Then some Dominican emissary of Antichrist appears—Horius, for example, or Rutherus, Diuolaeus, Picardus from the Sorbonne’s dregs, or Mallarius, for I am speaking of my own Frenchmen. Some of these men are so celebrated for their crimes that whoever calls them hypocrites does them a grave injustice; others are so crude and ignorant that they provoke even little women to laughter.

At first they are accustomed to make a light attempt to see whether he can be led away from his opinion by setting against him what they call the church’s ancient custom or a few stale arguments refuted a thousand times. If they obtain this and the miserable prisoner buys back his life with money, after publicly abjuring the heresy he is condemned to the galleys or sometimes even escapes. If not, he is nevertheless dragged to the fire, but his tongue is not cut out and he is not burned alive.

But if the blessed martyr of God remains in his opinion, then, since they have nothing to oppose to God’s Word, they immediately cry out that all these things were decided long ago, shout for their mother, the Faculty of the Sorbonne, and turn to abuse, wood, and flames.

Meanwhile, certain energetic nobles, coveting the possessions of the wretched, do not cease exhorting the prince to see that those seditious blasphemers are severely restrained. Immediately edicts appear, written not in ink but in blood, commanding Pilate to crucify Christ again or else not be Caesar’s friend. Cardinals, pseudo-bishops, pseudo-presbyters, canons, monks, and the other slaves of Antichrist also press the matter.

Although the judges unwillingly understand all these things and even behold them with their own eyes, nevertheless, either because they share in the spoil, because they wish to win the friendship of the king and nobles, or, if any are ignorant rather than wicked, because they rest in the theologians’ conscience, they deliver the innocent man to the executioners.

This is their tyranny. But in the judgments which our magistrate has exercised against some most obstinate and shameless Anabaptists, and besides them against one or two others, I ask these men to say what similar thing can be found. Who has been arrested because of simple error? Who was not first addressed gently and in a Christian manner? When has heresy been judged from human authority, traditions, or custom? Who has plotted against the life or goods—not only of innocent people, but even of the most guilty?

Finally, let them show even in the case of that most monstrous and abandoned Servetus what the ministers omitted in order to recall the man from error, or what the most illustrious Senate of Geneva neglected so that nothing would be done rashly or without sufficient religious care. For what these men customarily babble in corners in order to stir up hatred against the best and truly faithful servants of God is refuted by the testimony not only of the whole city of Geneva, but also of all the churches of Switzerland.

There is therefore no reason for them to cry that the world cannot judge concerning heresies, as though we attributed the entire investigation of these matters to the world rather than to God and his ministers and interpreters.

Fifth argument: Because most princes abuse their power.

“But,” say some otherwise good men wholly unlike these, “since no bird is rarer than a good prince, there is great danger that you will hand a sword to a madman by which good men will afterward be slaughtered, and so fall into the very dangers which you recounted a little before.”

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

This has some appearance of truth, especially among those who rightly shudder at the mention of that papal butchery. But I ask them to consider the whole matter more attentively with me. For if this reasoning holds, it is truly astonishing that God’s infinite wisdom, which established so sharp and severe a law against blasphemers and those who led people away from the true worship of God in any way, either failed to foresee this danger or despised it.

Next, if you admit this argument, what, finally, will be established in any part of a commonwealth? What is rarer in the world than faithful ministers of the Word? What is more frequent than false prophets? Therefore, let the care of interpreting religion be entrusted to no one. What corner of the earth is almost immune from tyranny? Therefore, let no one be given power over others. For whether you choose rule by one, by a few, or by the people, you cannot avoid the danger of tyranny.

What is more frequent than the corruption of judgments? Therefore, let no judges sit, especially in a capital case. Countless people use swords and blades to avenge private injuries while disturbing the commonwealth. Therefore, let the use of swords and blades be forbidden.

[PDF page 43 | logical scan 42 | printed page 126]

Cities are full of drunkards. Therefore, by renewing Emperor Claudius’s edict, let no vines be planted and let those already planted be uprooted. Who would tolerate this confusion of everything?

There is no reason to think this comparison unjust. For the world could lack princes, judges, swords, wine, and all other things with far less harm than it could lack the church’s ministry. Yet, insofar as lies with them, those who wish the magistrate to give no aid against its inflexible despisers abolish the church’s ministry.

Certainly, to summarize everything briefly, it would be preferable for every human being and even the entire universe to be destroyed utterly than for that—not to say to perish, for it cannot perish—but not to be maintained among human beings with the greatest zeal, for whose sake above all all things were created: namely, the manifestation of God’s glory through the ministry of the Gospel.

It follows from this that the daily cruelty of tyrants or the perverse zeal of certain people by no means prevents good and godly princes from kissing the Son—that is, from using with moderation the power granted them by God against those whom the devil sends to overturn religion itself, if possible, by unrestrained audacity and willful ignorance. *[Marginal reference: Psalm 2:12.]*

What if the Lord gives us princes who attack Christ’s kingdom through either open cruelty or gross ignorance? First of all, let the church resort to prayers and tears and reform its life, for these are believers’ weapons by which they conquer the world’s furies. Meanwhile, with the greatest prudence and moderation, yet steadfastly and courageously, the lesser magistrate must protect pure religion within his jurisdiction insofar as possible.

The city of Magdeburg situated on the Elbe supplied a distinguished example of this in our time. Yet Bellius was not ashamed to misuse its name in order to establish a doctrine which, if it had been received there, would have ensured that the most courageous citizens of that city never underwent every manifest danger for true religion.

Therefore, although most princes abuse their office, I affirm that whoever thinks the aid of a Christian magistrate, offered by God against the external violence either of unbelievers or heretics, should be rejected disarms God’s church of a most useful and, whenever it has so pleased the Lord, necessary protection.

Sixth argument: From the authority of the ancient church.

There remains the authority of the ancient church, which our Academics oppose to us in order to teach that the civil magistrate has no part in a cause of religion. Besides Christ’s doctrine and the examples of the ancient church, Eleutherius contends that the ancient doctors judged that heretics should be punished only by excommunication. He says that the laws of Theodosius, Valentinian, Marcian, and the others were written against all the Scriptures and ancient canons.

Yet in saying this, Eleutherius certainly excludes the magistrate from every restraint of heretics, since, by everyone’s confession, excommunication pertains to ecclesiastical jurisdiction alone. This, then, is Eleutherius’s position in summary and almost in so many words. I do not sufficiently know whether I should marvel more at his shamelessness or his ignorance.

*[Marginal heading: Refutation.]*

For even from the first inspection of the canons he adduces, it can be clearly established that this man is remarkably shameless, since he does not hesitate to fight with the very weapons by which he is most strongly attacked. Nor can I sufficiently grasp what kind of judgment Bellius possesses, who collected this medley.

For if it is true that by the custom of the ancient church heretics were cast out of the communion of the churches, can anyone doubt that Bellius is scarcely less insane than that Thracian Lycurgus whom the poets say cut his own legs with his double axe, when Bellius says that heretics should be left to their own liberty, accuses as cruel those who oppose the efforts of heretics, and proclaims that heretics are the class of people least to be feared? But this, plainly, is how these men are accustomed to act. Let us now come to the matter.

When they boast of Scripture on this point, they surpass Satan himself. For when he tempted the Lord, he indeed corrupted Scripture, yet he did not merely oppose its empty name. But although these men have the name of Scripture on their lips, they do not adduce even one passage from which it could be established that magistrates must be wholly removed from the administration of religion or that heretics must be restrained by excommunication alone. Therefore, since in this respect they have left me nothing to refute, there is no reason to labor in vain

[PDF page 44 | logical scan 43 | printed page 127]

in vain.

As for the Fathers, I could indeed employ the qualification which Augustine himself so often brings against the Pelagians: namely, that the Fathers’ authority is valid only insofar as they agree with God’s Word. But I am not compelled to seek this defense. For I have determined rather to defend the Fathers themselves against these men than to blame them as though they had departed from God’s Word. This will appear clearly from the canons these men adduce.

Thus Ambrose says in the canon *Qua dignior*, 24, question 1: “Any church which rejects the faith and does not retain the foundations of apostolic preaching must be deserted, lest it be able to sprinkle any stain of perfidy”; and the Apostle also plainly asserted this. Origen says in the canon *Quum aliquis*, 24, question 3: “When anyone departs from truth, from the fear of God, from faith, and from love, he departs from the church’s camp, even if he is not cast out by the bishop’s voice.”

What follows from this? Plainly, that it was not the ancient church’s custom to leave anyone to his own faith, or to refuse to explain a controversy—concerning the Trinity against the Arians and the other bands of heretics, concerning justification and free will against the Pelagians, or concerning God against the Manichaeans—as though it had been delivered too obscurely. Rather, it decided all these things from God’s Word and slew by anathema those who obstinately tore apart the church’s agreement. This, I say, can be gathered from these two passages.

Eleutherius, however, reasons that because they are excommunicated by the church, they therefore must only be excommunicated and consequently ought not to be touched by the magistrate. This is plainly the same manner of argument by which they conclude that heretics are not to be restrained by the magistrate because Paul, writing to Titus, did not command that they be killed but avoided, and Christ did not command that false prophets be killed but guarded against. Everyone sees, I think, how ineptly this is inferred, since those passages concern the church’s duty, not the civil magistrate’s office; and the examples of them all make sufficiently clear that the ancients judged in this way.

For Constantine drove Arius, condemned by the synod, into exile; Constantius did the same to Aëtius; and Theodosius, though otherwise more lenient toward heretics, did so to Apollinaris and Nestorius—to pass over the remaining histories, of which we shall speak in their proper place. It is well known not only what Valentinian and Marcian enacted against the followers of Manes and Eutyches, but also to what bitter penalties Constantine, Theodosius, and Justinian subjected heretics.

But it is worthwhile to hear Eleutherius’s response. “Those penalties,” he says, “exceed due measure and are contrary to all the Scriptures and the ancient Fathers, and even contrary to their own decrees.” We have already often answered concerning the Scriptures. But because he cites certain canons, we shall also say a few things about them.

There is the canon *His a quibus*, 23, question 8, in which, according to the judgment of the Council of Toledo, ecclesiastics are forbidden to conduct a judgment of blood—that is, a case in which someone’s life is at issue—or, as is explained afterward, to judge by their own sentence something that must be punished by death. This is assuredly right, since the jurisdictions must be distinct. But what has this to do with our subject?

There is another canon from Ambrose, 13, question 5, in which it is determined that whatever is added to the principal is regarded as usury, even if it is not money. I ask you, is Eleutherius not mad to apply this to the question proposed?

He also cites another canon, headed *Si Ecclesia*, 25, question 4, excerpted from Augustine’s fiftieth letter to Boniface. That excellent man wrote this letter precisely in order to prove against the Donatists the very thing which we have undertaken to prove against these Academics: namely, that heretics who despise ecclesiastical discipline must be restrained by the magistrate’s authority. I marvel what came into this man’s mind, that he should try to confirm his contrary opinion even from Augustine’s testimony.

“There,” he says, “it is plainly taught that the church is the one which suffers persecution, not the one which persecutes, as was the church of Christ and the apostles.” On the contrary, this very position is not defended but refuted in that very passage and in countless others, and throughout almost the whole of Cause 23. Augustine’s exact words are these: “If the true church is the one which suffers persecution and not the one which inflicts it, let it be asked of the Apostle which church Sarah signified when she persecuted the maidservant.” And a little afterward he says: “Next I ask: if good and holy men persecute no one but only suffer persecution, whose voice do they think it is in the Psalm where we read, ‘I will pursue my enemies and overtake them, and I will not turn back until they fail’? Therefore, if we wish to speak or acknowledge the truth, the persecution which the ungodly inflict upon Christ’s church is unjust;

[PDF page 45 | logical scan 44 | printed page 128]

but blessed is the persecution which it suffers for righteousness, while those who suffer persecution for unrighteousness are wretched. Accordingly, the former persecutes by loving, the latter by raging; the former in order to correct, the latter to overthrow; the former to call back from error, the latter to plunge into error.”

Thus far Augustine. I have copied his words so that everyone may understand with what good faith these men have treated this question, since they are not ashamed so shamelessly to distort the writings of an excellent man.

Eleutherius can also be plainly refuted when he says that Pelagius, bishop of Rome, who lived under Justinian or certainly Justin, was the first to enact that, if heretics refused to be led by arguments to the Christian faith, the worldly power should be summoned to compel them to it by force. For in the canons *Non vos* and *De Liguribus*, 23, question 5, Pelagius demands not that heretics be forced to faith, but that they be suppressed. Passing over the examples of Constantine, Theodosius, and Marcian, and omitting the decree of the Council of Chalcedon which Pelagius himself adduces, the African church had already enacted this under the consulships of Honorius XII and Theodosius VIII.
