This appears from decrees 94 and 95 of the Council of Carthage, which Augustine attended. Thus Eleutherius cannot deny that his ignorance is joined with the utmost shamelessness.

THE MAGISTRATE MAY NOT INFLICT CAPITAL PUNISHMENT UPON HERETICS.

Come, then, let us now consider the third part of the question proposed: namely, by what arguments people are accustomed to prove that heretics, even if they must be restrained by the civil magistrate, nevertheless must not be punished capitally. Here I see that my business is not only with these Academics of ours but also with certain excellent and very learned men. Because they desire that everyone be drawn to God and possess a very gentle and mild disposition, they concede that the more contumacious heretics must indeed be punished by the civil magistrate if ecclesiastical discipline has accomplished nothing; nevertheless, they think they must never be killed, lest no space for repentance remain.

I shall therefore try to answer their arguments separately. Yet I earnestly ask and implore them to be persuaded that, when we say that certain heretics may lawfully even be killed, nothing is further from our purpose than to incite Christian magistrates to slaughter and butchery. We do not judge that all must be removed indiscriminately, that anyone must be condemned without his case having been heard or by a judgment constituted on the authority of men and traditions, or that anything must be done rashly and immoderately, not even against those who have deserved the cruelest death. Far be this savagery from us.

We remember that we are human and, by God’s grace, sufficiently understand that no one can be compelled to faith and that Christians must be meek and merciful. But we ask that a definite limit to this mercy be supplied from God’s Word. For it is written: “He who justifies the wicked and he who condemns the righteous are both abominable to the Lord.” *[Marginal reference: Proverbs 17:15.]*

We ask that more account be taken of the sheep than of the wolves. We ask that those who abuse the church’s mildness and princes’ clemency to the ruin of others be severely punished. Finally, we ask that we recognize that Christian love does not rejoice in unrighteousness and that at least we be moved by the injuries done through crime and wickedness to God’s majesty. Whether these requests are just and fair, you will recognize, I hope, if you calmly hear me answer their arguments and then, in its proper place, adduce the reasons by which we think our opinion is confirmed.

Therefore, I ask them again to regard this part of our disputation not as a quarrel with adversaries of the truth, but as a friendly and fraternal conference. For in all other matters, but especially in the cause of religion, that old saying must prevail: “Good men ought to deal well with one another.”

First argument: From the parable of the tares.

Both many ancient and many recent writers customarily apply to the confirmation of this opinion about not killing heretics the following words taken from the parable of the tares: “The servants said to the master of the household, ‘Do you wish us, then, to go and gather the tares?’ But he said, ‘No, lest in gathering the tares you uproot the wheat together with them. Let both

[PDF page 46 | logical scan 45 | printed page 129]

grow together until the harvest.’”

Our new Academics interpret these words so as to use them to overthrow all discipline, both civil and ecclesiastical; we shall dispute against them separately. Certainly far more equitable are those who, first, do not think ecclesiastical restraint is forbidden by these words and, second, do not remove civil authority from punishing heretics either, but only define it by certain limits. Thus the magistrate may indeed punish those who contumaciously disturb the church’s peace and agreement after despising ecclesiastical discipline, yet may not kill them. “For,” they say, “even if the tares are separated, nevertheless they are allowed to grow as long as they live, and space for repentance is left.”

I ask these men to bear kindly with me as I dissent from them—not obstinately or dishonestly, as God loves me, but modestly out of zeal for the truth.

First, then, I dissent from them because I judge that the name “tares” means not only heretics—that is, those who obstinately and in deed dissent from the church in the doctrine of religion—but also all those who offend the church by the example of their life, and indeed unbelievers themselves if they persist in unbelief; in short, all those whom the Apostle says will not inherit God’s kingdom. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 6:9.]*

To confirm this interpretation, I employ not only the authority of Augustine and, among many recent writers, Luther in what are called his shorter Postils, but also several firm and, unless I am mistaken, solid reasons.

First of all, the field is understood to mean neither the church, which is the smallest part of the world—that is, the part of the world in which the Gospel is purely proclaimed—nor our mind, although Theophylact interprets it so, but this world in which both good and evil people live, as Christ himself teaches. The same analogy of interpretation must now be preserved in explaining the tares.

But if by the tares you understand heretics alone, the master of the household will have signified only one kind of evil people, one peculiar to the church, that is, to the smallest part of the world. Moreover, if the field is the world and this field has been entirely sown with only two kinds of seed—wheat sown by the master of the household and tares by the adversary—the logic of the opposition certainly demands that, just as all the sons of the kingdom, that is, whoever are members of the true church, are signified by wheat, so whoever are not of the church are called tares. Yet besides heretics there are very many other kinds of evil people reigning even within the church’s own innermost parts. Why, I ask, would the master of the household have passed them over?

Finally, does not Christ himself, the author of the parable and also its best interpreter, explicitly interpret the wheat as the righteous who will shine in the Father’s kingdom, and the tares as all stumbling blocks and those who practice unrighteousness—namely, all those who are cast into the furnace of fire? For he mentions only two kinds, and those opposed to each other.

Therefore, even if you understand this parable of the church alone, there is, I think, no reason, merely because the tares are said to have been sown amidst the wheat, for you to understand only heretics by the tares. Heretics alone are not the evil seed sown by the devil; heretics alone are not the church’s stumbling blocks; heretics alone do not practice unrighteousness; finally, heretics alone, joined with believers, do not constitute the whole body of the church in this world.

Once it is established that the tares mean all those who are not good seed, it follows that what is inferred about not killing heretics from the master’s words, “Let both grow together,” can also be applied to the other kinds of crimes. This is too absurd to be reasonably defended by anyone.

Furthermore, if by these words of the master of the household the civil magistrate too were forbidden to inflict capital punishment upon heretics, and if the added reason were that the wheat should not be uprooted with the tares—that is, that with evil people there should not also be torn out good people, either those who are already good or those who will sometime be good—I ask this: Since this same danger has always existed, why did the Lord formerly establish so severe a law against factious blasphemers and false

[PDF page 47 | logical scan 46 | printed page 130]

prophets that he devoted even entire cities, together with all their good people and indeed their herds, to a perpetual ban? What then? Was there not even in those times the same danger that very many would be killed who had either sinned through error or could have repented and rendered energetic service to the church? And yet the Lord says: “You shall not spare him, nor show indulgence, nor conceal him, but you shall utterly strike the inhabitants of that city with the sword, and whatever is in it, even its livestock; you shall burn the city with fire, so that it may be a perpetual heap and never be rebuilt.” *[Marginal reference: Deuteronomy 13.]*

Moreover, lest anyone be mistaken here, I am not asking whether that law still remains in force—for, as I hope, we shall discuss this elsewhere—but I ask how it can be probable that in the new covenant the Lord wished so great an account to be taken of this danger, which under Moses he had wholly disregarded, when under the old covenant there was no less danger than now that the wheat would be uprooted with the tares. For this concerns those who violate that first and chief commandment, “AND HIM ALONE SHALL YOU SERVE,” which is perpetual, even if the ceremonial form of worship has now been abrogated.

Nor should what some assert have force: that God was formerly severe under Moses but is merciful under Christ. This is indeed true if you contrast Law with Gospel, as Paul and others sometimes do, but it does not pertain to the question proposed. It is sufficiently agreed among all who are moderately learned that this cannot be understood as though God now approved violation of the Decalogue or were held by less zeal than before to vindicate his glory.

On the contrary, the more abundant grace has been brought to us by Christ’s advent, the more fitting it is that the Lord is more grievously offended by the sins of those who abuse so great a gift. If we were not utterly blind, the horrible darkness of Mohammedanism and papism in which the world is known to lie overwhelmed and buried would teach us more than sufficiently that this is so. For what disaster or servitude of the Israelite people can be compared with this blindness in which the Jews themselves now live together with Mohammedans and papists because Christ was despised?

Who thinks so coarsely and ignorantly about God as to represent him as formerly establishing, as though seized by some blind impulse of fury, so severe a law for uprooting the tares that it involved the innocent equally with the guilty, but now, as though his wrath had cooled, discovering some better method of uprooting the tares without danger to the wheat? For this, in my judgment, must certainly be said if we declare this parable of the tares to be a certain moderation of those ancient laws.

A third matter also deserves consideration: namely, the wording the master of the household used. He does not command his servants to allow the tares to grow together lest they uproot the wheat instead of the tares, but lest they uproot the wheat together with the tares. Thus it readily appears that this law was prescribed not for the sake of the tares themselves, but to take care for the wheat.

Therefore—with the goodwill of Augustine, Chrysostom, and certain others, let this be said—those who wish these words to pertain to people who can be made wheat from tares, that is, orthodox from heretics, and who therefore judge that the master wishes space for repentance to be left to them, seem to me to offer an interpretation more subtle than is proper.

It is established that the master calls only those things tares which were sown by the devil, that is, all the reprobate. Although for a time they counterfeit the appearance of wheat, as darnel does while it is still green, they nevertheless always are and have been tares. But if any heretics repent, they must have been elect from the beginning. Although for a time they appeared to be tares—namely, so long as through ignorance they resisted the truth and were caught in the devil’s snare—they were nevertheless never sown by the devil.

Therefore, they do not appear capable of being included among the tares about which the servants ask and the master of the household answers in this parable. If this is so, that transformation of tares into wheat plainly does not seem to be applied very suitably to this parable.

Secondly

[PDF page 48 | logical scan 47 | printed page 131]

when this interpretation of Chrysostom is advanced—“LET THEM GROW,” that is, “Indeed refute and anathematize the impious doctrines, but spare the people themselves”—when these words, I say, are advanced, who does not see that violence is done to the master’s words?

*[Marginal gloss: “Meanwhile LET THEM GROW—that is, the impious assemblies of the heretics. Stop their mouths and cut down their audacity in speaking, but do not put them to death or kill them.”]*

For this reason Chrysostom, and Theophylact following him as usual, do not think the master simply forbids the removal of the tares, but rather their removal by war. “For,” he says, “if you take up arms, it is necessary that, when you slaughter the heretics, many of the saints perish together with them.” *[Marginal reference: Chrysostom, Homily 46 on Matthew.]*

Jerome, however, in order to reconcile this prohibition of the master with Paul—who delivers the incestuous man to Satan and admonishes the Corinthians to expel the wicked man from among themselves—holds that only a precipitous judgment in doubtful matters is forbidden. Augustine, too, laboring to untie the same knot, judges that this prohibition concerns a particular kind of stumbling block, namely, whenever there is danger of schism.

“For,” he says, “when that fear is absent, but certain security remains altogether concerning the stability of the grain—that is, when each person’s crime is so known to everyone and appears execrable to everyone that it has either no defenders or none of such a kind as could occasion a schism—let the severity of discipline not sleep.” *[Marginal reference: Augustine, Against the Letter of Parmenian, book 3.]*

To what end do I say this? Namely, first, so that these interpretations may appear forced; and then so that everyone may understand that there is not so great an agreement of the ancient interpreters in explaining this parable as Bellius’s faction loudly declares.

Finally, if by this parable Christ wished to teach what the civil magistrate’s duty is in punishing heretics, it is certainly astonishing that, when he explained it at his disciples’ request, he passed over this entire part, and that the disciples, who had questioned him so eagerly, were satisfied with only half an interpretation. For if he had left this parable entirely untouched, as he did many others, there would certainly seem to be some room left for our conjectures. But since he himself explained it so diligently to his disciples, I confess I do not see how we can—not merely invent, but even seek—these interpretations so utterly different from Christ’s purpose without doing a conspicuous injury to Christ the interpreter and to his disciples.

Just as we have set forth the difficulties which necessarily follow if we measure civil and ecclesiastical authority over heretics from this one small part of the parable, so it is now worthwhile to hear the reasons by which this interpretation is customarily confirmed.

“The master of the household,” they say, “is Christ. His servants are those through whom he administers his kingdom in this world: namely, the civil magistrates and those placed over ecclesiastical discipline. In this parable the limits of their respective offices are prescribed to them. The ecclesiastics, who received the keys and not the sword from Christ, are to be content with their peculiar form of restraint; but the civil magistrate may restrain by other methods also the heretics, who are plainly the subject here—yet so that he does not kill them, lest he tear out the wheat together with the tares.”

First of all, I ask why by the name “servants” they understand men rather than angels. For angels too are sent forth in ministry for the sake of those who will be heirs of salvation. And since the subject here is God’s eternal counsel for cleansing the church, it is more probable that, as at the beginning of Job’s history, so here too the Lord is allegorically represented to us as if conversing with his angels about the church’s future condition in this world. *[Marginal reference: Hebrews 1:14.]*

Theophylact therefore interprets the servants as angels. The very question also seems to declare this, since it is sufficiently established, even by these men’s own confession, I think, that the complete cleansing of the church dispersed throughout the world is a work too great to be administered by human powers.

Next, if by the servants you understand faithful human beings, you will plainly separate the servants from the wheat. I do not think this possible, since they too were sown in the field by the master.

Finally, in explaining the parable Christ interprets the master, the field, both kinds of seed, the enemy, the harvest, and the reapers. If the servants differ from the reapers, how shall we say it could have happened that Christ, who so diligently pursued all the other details, passed this one over?

If, following Augustine, it is answered that the servants must be distinguished from the reapers because it is improbable that the angels are ignorant of anything the devils do, while here the servants do not know by whom the tares were sown; and, further, because the master himself distinguished them when, answering the servants, he did not say, “AT HARVEST TIME I SHALL SAY TO YOU,” but “AT HARVEST TIME I SHALL SAY TO THE REAPERS”: I reply that this is too subtle to be decided by a theologian and perhaps more curious than should be investigated.

As for the latter reason, I certainly concede that the servants are distinguished from the reapers, but as genus from species, or, so to speak, part from

[PDF page 49 | logical scan 48 | printed page 132]

whole. For the name “servants” is common, general, and perpetual to all ministering spirits, while the function of reapers is peculiar and temporary to those sent forth for that duty. From this it follows that, since by the name “servants” angels rather than human beings are meant, the foundation of this interpretation about heretics not being killed by the magistrate collapses.

It also seems necessary to ask how it can be proved that Christ dealt with civil jurisdiction here, since, so far as I know, Christ does not ordinarily give commandments concerning these matters. But even if I conceded that the magistrate is discussed here insofar as he too is a part of God’s kingdom, I think a probable reason can scarcely be advanced why this concerns his jurisdiction over heretics rather than over other criminals, since, as we showed before, all stumbling blocks are meant by the name “tares.”

For I do not judge true what one of these men wrote: namely, that when a thief or murderer is punished there is no danger that a good man will be killed, because no one ever defends murder, not even a murderer; but the case of religion is different, in which a good man can easily err, since it has often been delivered obscurely and through riddles.

This, I say, I do not think true. To say nothing of Scripture’s obscurity, which some vainly use as a pretext for their obstinacy, and to be silent about the sacrifices to Moloch and the frenzies of the Circumcellions, who can be ignorant that in our time the Libertines, Anabaptists, and Davidists have not only defended homicides as lawful by their spiritual revelations, but sometimes actually committed them? Indeed, those who wish distinct possessions of things to have been abolished also abolish thefts and adulteries, and some would rather die obstinately in those errors than acknowledge guilt.

Moreover, since in trials for these crimes innocent people are often accused and, convicted by false witnesses or driven by torture to confession, condemned—which cannot happen in a just and lawful trial for heresy, in which everyone is judged from his own mouth—who does not see that in those crimes too there is the greatest danger that the wheat will be uprooted with the tares, if one chooses to interpret these words in this way? Yet who but an impure Anabaptist would therefore contend that robbers or murderers must not be capitally punished?

Finally, if we carefully consider the definition of a heretic, we shall confess that it is scarcely, or not even scarcely, possible for the same person to be both a heretic and a good man. Not everyone who errs, or even obstinately errs in a cause of religion, is a heretic, as we showed before. Rather, it is only the person who, after being rightly and repeatedly admonished from God’s Word, willfully ignores the truth, does not build upon the foundation, and obstinately and factiously prefers his own judgment to the church’s authority.

That person alone is a heretic. Such people, I say, are those about whom we ask whether the civil magistrate may, according to the magnitude of their crime, restrain even with capital punishment as obstinate disturbers of public harmony and as those who sin wickedly and criminally against God—for willful ignorance is the highest degree of wickedness.

Therefore, since a bad conscience is always joined to heresy, although heretics contumacious to death deny this, where at last is that supreme danger that, when you kill a heretic, you uproot wheat? So much for this matter.

How, then, shall we explain this parable? Christ’s interpretation satisfies me. From it one can sufficiently understand that the parable was proposed so that from it we might understand in general what the condition of God’s kingdom would be from the propagation of the Gospel’s doctrine until the consummation of the world.

This is declared to us allegorically. At the beginning of Job’s history God is presented to us like a king with his nobles, conversing with his angels while Satan too is present, so that the incomprehensible providence of God governing the whole world through ministering spirits might be set before our eyes as if in a picture. In the same way, in this passage the Lord converses with the same angels, as the master of a household with his servants, about the future outcome of their labors.

In my judgment, those who dwell upon every individual word of a parable do not do rightly, since its end should instead be considered. Otherwise, to go no farther, if in another parable of the same thirteenth chapter, concerning three measures of flour, you curiously scrutinize who that woman is, what the flour is, and why three measures are mentioned, you must babble many ridiculous things, since this likeness teaches only this one point: God’s Word will permeate the entire world like a certain leaven.

I therefore think the individual parts of this parable too must be applied to its end and purpose. Because unforeseen evils strike more violently, the attentive master wished us to be warned about those things by which, the more godly anyone is, the more he was going to be offended.

Among the others, this stumbling block discussed here is perhaps the greatest. For all the holiest people are accustomed

[PDF page 50 | logical scan 49 | printed page 133]

to be deeply moved by the church’s continual calamities, and to human judgments it appears extremely unjust that the ungodly everywhere flourish and most obstinately attack the church at home and abroad.

Our master, by whom we were sown, consoles us and foretells that this condition of the church in this world will indeed be perpetual, and that tares will never be absent here. Therefore we should not hope for a complete cleansing of the church in this world; yet this world will not be perpetual, for God has appointed a time in which the cleansed wheat will be stored in the barn and the cut tares burned.

The fact that the church’s complete cleansing is deferred so long happens for our good, to such a degree that if any people should perhaps attempt this complete cleansing of the church contrary to God’s counsel, they would not only accomplish nothing but would also bring great harm upon the church.

Let us therefore know that the church is not rashly troubled, but is so because it is expedient, so that, resting in God’s counsels, each of us may patiently await the time of harvest for himself.

This, then, is the sum of this parable, as appears from Christ’s own interpretation. Yet who does not see that, although evil people will always be mixed with the good in this world, and this mixture must therefore be patiently endured until harvest time, it by no means follows that either the civil magistrate or those placed over ecclesiastical discipline must cease from duty?

What then? Because there will always be some robbers and murderers in this world, will the civil magistrate therefore punish neither robbers nor murderers capitally? This comparison is not unjust, since we have already shown that all the church’s stumbling blocks are included under the name “tares.”

Therefore, to say freely what I think, I judge that neither civil nor ecclesiastical jurisdiction is treated here. Both of these pertain to particular judgments of individual persons, that is, to the church’s particular cleansing. But this passage discusses that complete and universal cleansing of the entire church which the Lord defers to the last judgment, to be accomplished by the angels’ ministry.

Unless I am mistaken, this can be clear even from the servants’ question itself and the master’s answer. For the servants do not ask: “Do you wish us to gather this or that manifest tare?”—that is, “Do you wish us to cast this criminal, whether heretic, fornicator, thief, or one infected with some other crime, out from the midst of this church, lest a little leaven corrupt the whole lump?”

For if they had asked this, and you understand the servants as those placed over ecclesiastical discipline, the master would undoubtedly have answered what he afterward commanded and carried out through Paul.

Nor do they ask: “Do you wish us to remove from life this criminal and blasphemer, and indeed this factious disturber of your majesty and ecclesiastical authority?” For if by the servants you understand the civil magistrates, the master would certainly have answered precisely what he decreed through Moses against blasphemers and factious false prophets.

This, I say, is not what the servants ask. Rather: “Do you wish us to gather the tares?”—that is, “Do you wish us to cleanse from all these stumbling blocks that good seed with which you have sown the whole earth?” The master therefore commands them to await the harvest, that is, the consummation of the world. From this it sufficiently appears that they consulted the master concerning the complete cleansing of the whole church.

For otherwise, when the matter concerns particular judgments—that is, the uprooting of this or that tare—we must judge just as we do concerning the particular gathering of the wheat itself. If you consider individual persons and not the church’s whole body, the Lord does not await that universal harvest to gather his wheat, but daily reaps his own one by one when, having called them out of this world, he places them in his kingdom. So one must also judge concerning the particular and daily harvest of individual tares, when he casts criminals removed from this world either through magistrates or by some other means into outer darkness.

Therefore, to bring this discussion at last to an end and summarize everything briefly: this passage concerns the universal cleansing of the church from all criminals. When questioned about it, the master answered concerning the universal harvest also. Just as the universal harvest, that is, the supreme judgment, does not abolish particular harvests, that is, daily judgments among both the elect and the reprobate, so we must judge that the universal cleansing which will occur only at the supreme judgment through the angels’ ministry by no means hinders the particular cleansings of individual churches rightly performed by either civil or ecclesiastical authority. The Lord has appointed both forms of authority in his church for these daily judgments; and since they are distinct, we shall act wrongly if we confuse them.

Second argument: Because the magistrate may not kill the soul.

I now approach another argument wholly joined to the preceding one. For

[PDF page 51 | logical scan 50 | printed page 134]

some think: “Since heretics have no part in God’s kingdom, it cannot be conceded to the magistrate that he should kill them without our in some fashion making him a minister of damnation. But to kill the soul belongs to the Lord alone. Therefore, the magistrate must not kill a heretic, lest he usurp God’s office.”

I answer briefly that, if this reasoning had no force formerly under Moses, there is no reason for us to think it has force now either, since Christ detracted nothing from the magistrate’s lawful power. It is established that the Lord enacted that the blasphemer and false prophet who leads others away from God’s worship should, without any mercy, be removed from life by capital punishment through the magistrate, with no condition of repentance added. *[Marginal reference: Leviticus 24; Deuteronomy 13.]*

Further, it is clear that what is objected concerning heretics can also apply to other criminals. Murderers, poisoners, sodomites, and, finally, other criminal persons can be condemned and led to death who do not repent of their past life, but who even delight in remembering their crimes and are affected by absolutely no sense of divine judgment or present death.

We have certainly seen some who, even in the executioners’ hands, though condemned with the greatest justice, nevertheless tore the magistrate apart with abuse; others who said they died willingly because they had avenged themselves upon an enemy; others who could not be induced to forgive those by whom they had been accused; and some even who cursed God himself.

What shall I say of wars, which all the godly nevertheless agree can sometimes lawfully be declared and waged by the magistrate’s authority? What then? In all those who must be killed, will the magistrate have to await repentance, lest he seem to kill their souls too and invade God’s possession? Or will he not rather, as God’s minister for wrath, punish the bodies of the wicked, defend his citizens by arms against others’ injuries, and entrust the rest to the Lord?

Moses commands the Levites to seize their swords and slaughter everyone they encounter without any distinction at all. They obey, and this deed of theirs is approved by God through Moses’s voice. *[Marginal reference: Exodus 32.]* I ask, since the matter here concerned idolatry and unbelief—“spiritual” sins, as some call them—should Moses have awaited repentance lest he become a killer of souls? On the contrary, if any had seriously repented, they should rather have been spared, and the sword drawn against the contumacious alone.

Phinehas too, although acting outside ordinary procedure, thrust down to hell two people pierced through in the very act of adultery. *[Marginal reference: Numbers 25.]* Shall he therefore be said to have killed souls, though for this very reason he is so greatly commended?

Two robbers were crucified with Christ, each covered in crimes. One is received into Paradise; the other, as is indeed probable, is rejected. Was the magistrate therefore just in punishing the former but cruel in punishing the latter, and was he not rather God’s minister for wrath in both cases?

This is altogether how the matter stands. The magistrate was appointed by God so that we might lead a tranquil life in all godliness and dignity. *[Marginal reference: 1 Timothy 2.]* Therefore, whoever introduces any disorder either into human affairs or into religion—which heretics do above all others—the magistrate must punish him, according to the magnitude of his crime, by the prescription of the laws.

Nevertheless, I confess, Christian love obliges him, since he prosecutes the person’s crime rather than the person himself, to bring it about insofar as possible that the wretch who must undergo punishment acknowledges his sin and asks God for pardon.

But whether he has acknowledged his guilt and thus sought pardon, or remained obstinate to the end, just as the magistrate must do nothing rashly and inconsiderately, so his duty altogether demands that he not spare the guilty person, but instead carry out the office entrusted to him by the Lord.

He cannot therefore be said to have killed that man’s soul, since he did not kill him so that he would be damned by God, but to provide for others’ tranquility and, by making an example of one, to keep the others in duty, at least through fear of punishment.

And since he omitted nothing that could prevent that unhappy man from dying in his sin, he certainly left the judgment of the soul intact to God, and the man must accuse his own extreme obstinacy rather than the magistrate.

Although this is sufficiently clear in itself, I think, in every case, it can be confirmed especially in the crime of heresy, concerning which we particularly inquire, by the authority of Paul himself, who calls heretics “self-condemned” (*αὐτοκατακρίτους*). According to Paul, therefore, as regards that eternal punishment which is decreed by God’s sentence alone, the heretic is himself the cause of his own destruction. As regards bodily punishment, he too has brought it upon himself by his crimes, while the magistrate, in inflicting it, is God’s minister for wrath. Certainly, the heretics themselves can much more justly be called killers of souls than those by whom they are killed.

These, unless I am mistaken, are the reasons by which certain good and learned men have been led to judge that heretics should be punished short of death. I thought that I should answer them from no contentiousness at all, but from zeal for the truth alone.

If there are any who also bring forward the argument that no one can be compelled to faith, I hope they have been answered in the second part of the preceding dis-

[PDF page 52 | logical scan 51 | printed page 135]

putation.

As for some who set Christian clemency before us—saying that God does not burn wandering sheep but seeks and heals them; that he desires us not to kill heretics but to avoid them; that swords are to be forged into plowshares and spears into sickles; and that our most gentle Savior Christ commands many similar things—we approve all these things and pursue them according to our strength.

For, as we have often testified already and testify again, we judge that neither unbelievers, nor those who simply err, nor every kind of error should be punished, but only manifest ungodliness duly convicted from God’s Word in factious persons, and that this should be done with moderation—yet with capital punishment when the magnitude of the blasphemy or danger has demanded it. We also judge that this belongs not to those placed over ecclesiastical discipline but to the civil magistrate. Everyone therefore sees that we are as far as possible from the tyranny of which these men accuse us.

Many indeed loudly declare that they fear we are offering the sword to furious men, and they judge this question insufficiently necessary in this disastrous age, in which the power of certain people seems rather to need curbing. But although I have actually experienced the cruelty of some people on account of God’s name—why should I not glory in the Lord?—I profess that I cannot agree with them.

For even if everyone abuses his power, it is nevertheless not for us to conceal the truth and remove the boundaries established by God. Nothing must be done among us in the church, especially in this so licentious an age, with greater zeal and effort than that, after the sincere doctrine of the Gospel has been explained, both civil magistrates and those placed over ecclesiastical discipline should know the boundaries of their jurisdiction and power well and accurately.

I freely testify before God and his angels that all the corruptions which we are compelled to endure everywhere in God’s church today with the greatest grief have sprung from this as from a fountain: princes, partly through ignorance of their duty, partly because they themselves were exceedingly vicious, and partly through sluggishness—which those who flatter princes falsely call clemency—neither joined themselves courageously with the church’s true ministers nor, when the most cunning foxes crept into God’s sheepfold and little feared the church’s keys, displayed their drawn swords with sufficient severity.

Thus it happened that, once they had degenerated into wolves and cast the pastors out of the church, they securely occupied their seats. Then, by God’s just judgment, they subjected the princes themselves to their rule, until Satan, sitting in God’s own temple, made the greatest monarchs of the world ministers of his cruelty.

For when the order established by God is neglected, the greatest disturbance of all things must follow. This, I say, is the true origin of the churches’ devastation, not the one Montfort teaches.

For he loudly declares that the pope’s tyranny—though these men are accustomed everywhere studiously to suppress his name—arose from his having taken the power of judging away from men. This is inept, unless I am mistaken, since he must already have been a tyrant in order to prohibit it; nor was the power of judging ever so free and promiscuous in God’s church that individuals could think and teach anything whatever, as Montfort wishes to be lawful.

Third argument: From the parable of the tares, according to the Academics’ opinion.

But come, let us return to our Academics. They too are accustomed to adduce certain arguments, which, as I hope, we shall readily refute.

Let a marvelous enthymeme first come forward from someone who calls himself Cleberg, several of whose arguments we have already dispatched. He too fights with the parable of the tares and, as it seems to him, fights very keenly, with a double-edged weapon.

“If heretics,” he says, “are the tares, they must not be killed, but left until the appointed time. If heretics are not the tares, but murderers and other criminals are, criminals must be left until the appointed time and not killed—which is false, for if criminals were not killed, the world could not stand. But either criminals or heretics must necessarily be left until the harvest. It follows that whoever wishes heretics to be killed before the harvest does not wish criminals to be killed; and whoever wishes criminals to be killed does not wish heretics to be killed, unless you wish to reject Christ’s command about leaving the tares.”

I concede this man even more than he asks. For he contends that either heretics or other criminals are called tares. I affirm that both heretics and other criminals—that is, all stumbling blocks and all who practice unrighteousness—are included under the name “tares.” He himself cannot reprehend this without reprehending Christ himself.

What then? Plainly, if we follow this hypothesis—that is, if these words, “LET THE TARES GROW UNTIL THE HARVEST,” were spoken to the civil magistrate and forbid him to kill—it will follow that no guilty people are to be killed before the consummation of the age and no scandal removed before the day of supreme judgment. This neither

[PDF page 53 | logical scan 52 | printed page 136]

is so, nor can it be.

It remains, therefore, that this dilemma is inept, since it assumes a false hypothesis and indeed posits the very thing in question; finally, it does not comprehend all the possibilities. For, as we showed before, these words pertain neither to the civil magistrate nor to ecclesiastical discipline. They do not signify that no guilty people are to be removed from the midst by the magistrate’s daily judgments or restrained by ecclesiastical discipline, but rather that stumbling blocks will never be absent from the church until the consummation of the age.

Accordingly, by God’s decree the angels are forbidden to undertake before that time the church’s complete cleansing, which Paul calls the redemption of the sons and which Peter testifies the angels most eagerly await. *[Marginal references: Romans 8; 1 Peter 1.]*

“But,” you will say, “these words are customarily explained otherwise, especially by the ancients.” They certainly are, and by some recent writers too. Yet among the ancients Theophylact agrees with me, interpreting the servants as angels; and the others, let me say it with their goodwill, disagree with themselves. Among recent writers, all the most learned follow Christ’s interpretation with me. These men cannot reprehend this without themselves incurring the gravest reprehension.

Moreover, which of the Fathers will they produce as a patron for their judgment that heretics not only must not be killed but must not even be judged? They go so far as not to be ashamed to affirm that no class of people is less to be feared—so that, plainly, a citizen may think and teach anything whatever.

But this is an old device of the devil. Augustine testifies that even in his own age some interpreted this parable in that way, and he refutes their error thus:

“Those who perceived that this mixture of good and evil in the church was demonstrated or predicted, and learned the patience commanded—which makes us so steadfast that, even if we see tares in the church, neither our faith nor our love is hindered, nor do we ourselves withdraw from the church because we discern tares in it—think that a most perverse discipline should be established, one granting security to the rulers, so that it should belong to them only to say what must be guarded against or done, but not to care that each person does whatever he pleases.

“In answering them, I say first that no one should so receive those testimonies of Scripture which indicate the present mixture of good and evil in the church or foretell its future mixture that, deceived by his own opinion and not instructed by those writings, he thinks the severity of discipline must be wholly relaxed and abandoned without love. For because Moses, God’s servant, endured with the greatest patience that mixture of the first people, it does not follow that he did not punish many with the sword. And Phinehas the priest pierced with an avenging sword the adulterers found together.” *[Marginal reference: Augustine, On Faith and Works, chapter 4.]*

Thus far Augustine. I do not deny that he abhorred the killing of the Donatists; yet these men vainly boast his authority to establish such license.

Fourth argument: Because the greatest part of humankind would have to be killed.

It is time to approach another argument of theirs. “If blasphemers must be killed,” says Montfort, Bellius’s colleague, “are not those especially to be killed who profess God with their mouth but deny him by their deeds, since they cause God’s name to be blasphemed? If those who sacrifice to strange gods must be killed, those must especially be killed who sacrifice to their god Maonim, that is, violence and cruelty. Likewise the greedy, whose greed is idolatry, and gluttons, whose God is their belly. Simoniacs,” says Eleutherius, “who are badly called heretics, canon *Eos qui*, 1, question 1. But, I ask, what would happen to so great a flock of heretics? From where would sufficient wood be supplied to the executioner to burn them all?”

“Indeed,” Montfort objects, “since there are innumerable sects, none of which agrees with another in interpreting the sacred Scriptures, it would be necessary to kill them all except one, and that the smallest, since Christ’s flock is little. Who does not see how absurd and impossible this is?”

Thus these men speak—as though we breathed nothing but slaughter and threats against everyone who dissents from us, or rather from the truth, or as though we were so unskilled that we did not discern manifest paralogisms.

I answer briefly that the kinds both of blasphemy and of idolatry must be distinguished. In a general sense, a blasphemer can be called one who injures another’s reputation, as the derivation of the word itself declares and as it is also used by Paul in 1 Corinthians 4:12. You Academics do this when you most calumniously accuse so many distinguished commonwealths and so many eminent servants of Christ of being cruel and savage. This crime must be judged from civil laws.

But in a narrower sense, theologians call a blasphemer one who either robs God of his proper honor or ascribes to him something unworthy of and foreign to his nature—in short, one who injures God’s majesty and treats it with contempt.

The kinds of this blasphemy must further be distinguished. Sometimes it is joined with heresy, as recently in Servetus and also in those Academics who deny that knowledge of the Trinity makes a person better, who diminish God’s grace by establishing free will, and, finally, who complain that God’s Word is

[PDF page 54 | logical scan 53 | printed page 137]

obscure and enigmatic. There is no doubt that they are worthy of punishments so much graver as God’s majesty, obstinately and wickedly violated, must be vindicated more severely than the dignity of every king and prince.

But sometimes this blasphemy proceeds from ignorance. Thus formerly by Paul, and today, as I think, by many Jews, Turks, and papists, Christ is treated with the most unworthy contempt through ignorance rather than wickedness. This sometimes also happens to those otherwise imbued with the Christian religion who nevertheless display the greatest ignorance concerning the chief heads of Christian doctrine.

I do not excuse their blasphemies. There is no doubt that they merit God’s most grievous wrath, since this very ignorance in which they live is the penalty of sin. *[Marginal references: Romans 14; 1 Timothy 1.]* Nevertheless, prayer should be made for them and the weak patiently instructed, rather than punishment exacted from them by the magistrate, unless their ignorance is more obstinately willful.

For then they must be regarded not as weak but as wicked, and the Christian prince must restrain these rebellious disturbers of the church according to the magnitude of their crime.

Finally, certain blasphemies arise from anger and an unconsidered impulse of the mind. They certainly must be abhorred, yet no certain rule can be delivered concerning them. All the circumstances, which are manifold, must be prudently considered, so that neither is God’s majesty violated with impunity, nor does a stumbling block to the church remain unpunished; yet neither should the remedy be of such a kind that it exacerbates rather than heals the disease. Finally, the punishment must be seasoned with the highest balance of severity and love.

Let these men therefore say what in this distinction can justly be reprehended, and let them cease calumniating us as though, by defending the law delivered by the Lord against blasphemers, we had conspired to destroy almost all mortals.

As for idolatry, a distinction was even more necessary. To omit other meanings of “idol” which do not pertain to the subject, we sometimes call material representations of corporeal things “idols.” Those who attribute divinity in any degree to these representations, or who worship God in them in any way, are therefore called idolaters, that is, servants of idols.

Scripture most gravely reprehends this idolatry on every page. From this it must be inferred that there is scarcely anything by which God is more offended or which he punishes more bitterly.

Nevertheless, he by no means established a law by which all idolaters of every nation should be punished. He established it only against those who attempted to introduce idolatry into his Israelite people, to whom alone he was prescribing laws, plainly reserving the others to be punished by his own judgment.

Thus, if anyone now were to strive to recall people in God’s church to pagan or papal idolatry, we say the Christian magistrate’s duty demands that he punish such a criminal idolater. Augustine testifies that this was also enacted against pagans. *[Marginal reference: Augustine, Epistle 50, chapter 2.]*

Nevertheless, we tolerate papists and kindly receive those who come to our churches. We do not do so from fear of them, as you pretend—for why would we fear to kill them, if killing were lawful, when by God’s grace we do not fear to be killed daily by them for the sake of piety?—but because God will judge those who are outside. It is one thing to defect from religion and solicit others to defect, but another not to hold the way of truth upon which you have never entered. By what right could a magistrate invade the subjects of another?

Although it is his duty courageously and resolutely to protect and retain Christ’s religion within his jurisdiction, even with the sword when no other course is lawful, no one can or ought to propagate it by arms.

But another kind of idolatry very often occurs. It is indeed most pernicious, but it must be judged differently from the preceding kind. For Scripture sometimes calls an idol whatever reigns in someone’s mind in place of God and holds supreme authority, to which a person, passing over the true God, gives himself in servitude.

This idol is either wholly imaginary, because it exists only in imagination, or it indeed exists in the nature of things but is nevertheless not of such a kind as we have persuaded ourselves it is. Both kinds of idol seem to require illustration by examples.

The Mohammedans dream of a certain eminent prophet and interpreter of God named Mohammed, and from his authority have established a religion for themselves which consists entirely of dreams and empty imaginings—namely, of things that nowhere exist and never will occur.

We also refer to this class the papists’ purgatory and very many empty opinions. They therefore have as many idols as the dreams they have invented for themselves.

Others form within their mind from created things images of gods whom they serve. In this manner the glutton is said to regard his belly as god, the greedy person riches, the ambitious person honors, and the lustful person his mistress. Thus the Cyclops says in Euripides:

[PDF page 55 | logical scan 54 | printed page 138]

“Wealth, little man, is god to the wise;
all else is boasting and fine-sounding speech.”

And a little afterward:

“I sacrifice to no one but myself, and not to gods,
and to this belly, greatest of divinities.”

For this reason Paul too calls greed a kind of idolatry; and speaking of those who set their mind on earthly things, he says, “Their God is their belly.” *[Marginal references: Ephesians 5:3; Colossians 3:5; Philippians 3:19.]*

The former of these two kinds is therefore joined with the idolatry of images, or at least with certain external ceremonies, of which it is plainly the parent and mother. For the mind must conceive an idol before the hand gives birth to it.

Consequently, the same judgment must be made concerning this kind as concerning the first: unbelievers must be distinguished from defectors, the unlearned from the wicked, the simple from the factious, and foreigners from members of the household.

But the other kind of the latter idolatry, from which scarcely anyone is entirely free, must be judged differently. It lies wholly in the mind’s innermost chambers, which God alone can search, and arises from vices and depraved desires. These so reign in the mind that, if we do not simply regard the things we desire as gods, we nevertheless justly seem to regard them as gods, since, with reason oppressed by desires, we knowingly and deliberately prefer them to the one true God.

We showed in the second part of the preceding disputation that the magistrate punishes not vices but crimes. For so long as vices do not break out into crime, they are either left to God’s judgment when they are altogether hidden or insufficiently manifest; or, if in some manner they disclose themselves, yet without injury to the commonwealth or a neighbor concerning which laws have been established, they are restrained by God’s Word, prayers, exhortations, rebukes, and, finally, ecclesiastical discipline.

But if the root produces full fruit—for example, if a glutton devours his own or another’s goods with harm to someone or a corrupting example, a greedy person diverts another’s goods by evil arts, or a lustful person violates someone’s chastity—then at last the magistrate will punish him according to the crime, not as an idolater, for such people are called idolaters figuratively; nor as a glutton, greedy person, or lustful person, for these are names not of crimes but of vices; but as a thief, robber, or adulterer. So much concerning blasphemers and idolaters.

As for those called simoniacs, I answer briefly that they are not the question of this passage, since they must be numbered among sacrilegious people rather than among heretics. I am nevertheless not ignorant that the ancients list Simon Magus as a leader of the heretics.

But this does not refer to that crime which the Roman pontiffs—successors, plainly, of that Simon, not of Simon Peter—openly practice. It refers instead to certain other doctrines about which, among others, Epiphanius and Theodoret wrote copiously.

There remains what these men say: “If heretics were killed, it would happen that the lives of few people were spared, since Christ’s flock is little and even within it there are innumerable sects.”

This is said against us so calumniously that I am ashamed to answer. For—as I am compelled to repeat in almost countless places—we distinguish from heretics both unbelievers and those who sin by simple error and those whose life alone is reprehended. Next, we confess that among heretics themselves there are great differences of errors and manifold circumstances. We admonish that all these must be prudently and diligently considered, so that neither are the church’s plagues nourished by excessive indulgence nor is the church scattered by untimely severity.

Furthermore, the comparison of monks with Anabaptists—to say freely what I think—seems to me extremely unjust. It is one thing to abolish distinct ownership of property as though it conflicts with Christian love, but another to share all one’s resources with certain people in a monastery for the sake of a vow. It is one thing to establish the common use of women, but another to vow celibacy. It is one thing to cast the civil magistrate out of the church and condemn oaths and all forensic disputes as impious, but another to contend that by privilege one is subject to only a single abbot. It is one thing to say that one no longer sins and to strike out remission of sins, in which our happiness consists, as even David testifies, but another to profess a certain more perfect life. *[Marginal reference: Psalm 32:1.]*

I do not say these things to favor monasticism, which I abominate as a swamp of superstitions, but to teach that it cannot rightly be inferred that, if it is lawful to punish Anabaptists capitally, monks must by the same reasoning be subjected to the same penalties.

For there seems to be no less a difference between the latter and the former than between open ungodliness and gross superstition.

Fifth argument: Because Paul does not command that a heretic be killed, but avoided; and he wishes to become anathema for the Jews.

These men object that Paul admonishes Titus not to kill a heretical person,

[PDF page 56 | logical scan 55 | printed page 139]

but to avoid him. When that eminent servant of God Bullinger—whom, as I ought, I name for honor’s sake—rightly answered this most futile argument in the eighth sermon of his Second Decade, saying that Paul was writing there to an apostle, but that if he had written to Sergius Paulus or some governor he would undoubtedly also have prescribed his duty to him, there suddenly arose a certain masked Montfort. He objected against that excellent and most learned man that Paul could not have commanded that those be killed for whom he writes that he wishes to become anathema. *[Marginal reference: Romans 9.]*

This is certainly ridiculous. For what the Apostle says of the Jews in general, he himself applies even to each of the most abandoned heretics.

This would be just as if he accused Matthew of falsehood for writing that Christ was delivered to Pilate through envy, since the Apostle testifies that the Israelites have a zeal for God, but not according to knowledge. *[Marginal references: Matthew 27:18; Romans 10:2.]*

The same Apostle of ours also wishes that certain people from that very nation for which he wishes to become anathema would cut themselves off—those by whom the Galatians are troubled, namely, those whom elsewhere he calls enemies of Christ’s cross and whose end he knew to be destruction. *[Marginal references: Galatians 5:12; Philippians 3:18.]*

Further, is it not utterly absurd to be unable to distinguish hatred of a crime from hatred of the criminal person? For I ask, when Moses requested of the Lord that he be blotted from the book unless he forgave the Israelite people’s sin, did he not sufficiently declare that, when a little earlier he had caused three thousand people indiscriminately to be killed, he exacted those bitter but nevertheless just penalties from no hatred of them, but rather because he was kindled by abhorrence of idolatry and zeal for God?

This, then, is how the matter stands. A faithful minister of God can and should piously demand the civil magistrate’s aid against contumacious heretics whom ecclesiastical discipline has done nothing to restrain, and yet he may grieve deeply over their condition and commend their salvation to the Lord with prayers and tears.

The magistrate too may deliver the same heretics into the executioners’ hands while himself groaning and earnestly invoking the Lord, that, if possible, he may grant the grace of repentance to those about to die.

Otherwise, whenever private hatred of the person enters into either a civil or an ecclesiastical judgment, it immediately ceases to be God’s judgment. It is rather the most savage butchery, in which profane people abuse God’s power to avenge a private injury, whereas crimes, not people, must be hated, and criminals must be punished for their crimes, not because of some private desire.

Sixth argument: Because Paul forbids anyone to judge before the time.

The Academics also customarily adduce a certain passage of Paul in which the Apostle forbids anyone to judge before the time. *[Marginal reference: 1 Corinthians 4:5.]* As though it treated public judgments, whether civil or ecclesiastical, and not rather those made by private persons, as appears from what precedes and follows.

For what he had said before, “HE WHO JUDGES ME IS THE LORD,” and what follows a little afterward, “AND THEN EACH ONE’S PRAISE WILL BE FROM GOD,” sufficiently declare—as the most learned interpreters of that epistle also testify—that in that passage only those judgments are reprehended among the Corinthians in which, as though they were contest judges, they judged the excellence of the ministers, crowning some but dismissing others with disgrace.

For otherwise the spirits must be tested not only publicly but also privately, and a tree must be known by its fruits, provided that we approach judgment neither impelled by rashness, zeal to detract, or some other vicious desire, but guided by true love.

Seventh argument: Because fear of death makes hypocrites.

Montfort adds this reason also to the preceding ones: capital penalties for heretics open the way to hypocrisy. Yet everyone sees that the same can be affirmed of other crimes.

Nor is this end properly set before the civil magistrate, that he should rule the mind, but rather that he should preserve external concord and tranquility both in religion and in civil dealings.

Since human dispositions vary, and most people whom love of virtue does not move must be terrified by fear of punishment, the magistrate, bearing the sword, does both: partly to protect those who do well, and partly to be a terror to evildoers.

Because he cannot perceive the mind, although he labors insofar as possible to have his citizens as well disposed as possible, he necessarily judges only external deeds.

Therefore, whether someone abstains from troubling religion or another part of the commonwealth through hypocrisy or from the heart, the magistrate has nothing in him that he can punish.

But even if very many are hypocrites, he must not abstain from public judgments. For, as Augustine says, “Although fear of penalties does not yet possess the delight of a good conscience, it at least restrains evil desire within the barriers of thought.” *[Marginal reference: Augustine, Against the Letters of Petilian, book 2, chapter 83.]*

Though medicine does not benefit everyone, no one unless utterly insane would therefore

[PDF page 57 | logical scan 56 | printed page 140]

judge that it must be despised.

Eighth argument: From the example of certain people for whom this ended very badly.

Finally, in order that it may become still clearer by what spirit these men are driven, it is worthwhile to examine a certain argument of theirs in which Cleberg openly attacks faithful and truly godly churches.

Thus, after accusing as rabid and savage those excellent and courageous princes who suppressed the unheard-of frenzies of the Münster Anabaptists, Cleberg finally bursts forth into these words:

“God seems to have shown,” he says, “how greatly those things pleased him when he brought it about that the very author of that opinion—that the Anabaptists should be capitally punished—a learned man celebrated throughout the world, not long after other unarmed people had been killed, himself fell in battle with many others. Many godly people believe that this happened to him because of that deed.”

Montfort too, insulting our English brethren with a kind of canine rage, says: “The outcome showed what sort the Evangelicals were who had admitted the Gospel under compulsion. Those who escape from there with great difficulty know this, and they proclaim that nothing is more foreign to the Gospel. Nor is it strange if like is returned for like, so that those who used violence should suffer violence.”

O audacious and shameless crime! Cleberg, whoever you are, are you really so unrestrainedly shameless that even after his death you would attack a distinguished servant of Christ in our time with that insolent madness? Yet plainly this was lacking to complete his praises: that he should be reprehended by you and those like you.

As for that most calamitous defeat, neither party, I think, is accustomed to remember it. I shall therefore not do what you cunningly seek and mention the causes of those calamities to the offense of the parties. I say only that you are criminal when—even granting you that this man was the author of the opinion that Anabaptists should be capitally punished—you reprehend so great a man for most excellent counsel which was plainly necessary in those times.

For why should I not call that foremost counsel which so many godly princes, so many distinguished cities, and so many faithful churches were not ashamed to embrace with one consent? Yet you were found to accuse as savage and cruel what everyone except one or two approved.

That counsel, I say, if it had not prevailed, would mean that the churches were now held oppressed by the most abandoned Anabaptists, no senators would sit safely in the cities, and chastity and modesty would be exiled from every house and family.

But come, from what do you finally infer that this counsel displeased God? “Because,” you say, “its author shortly afterward died in battle.” What? Was that war undertaken for this reason? Was the fight against the Anabaptists waged under his auspices?

Then, I ask, what an excellent demonstration is this? “The man who shortly before had judged that those unarmed people should be killed shortly afterward fell in battle; therefore it is probable that he paid the penalties for cruelty.” But what if those unarmed people were more justly killed than any robbers or murderers?

Is death while fighting for one’s country really so unworthy a death for a good man that it seems connected with God’s wrath? “But many godly people believe that this happened to him because of that deed.” If you speak the truth, it is certainly astonishing that you alone were found after so many years to reveal this secret. For you are not ignorant that others adduced far different causes.

In the killing of this most innocent man it happened as in very many other cases, so that each person judged it according to his own will. Yet it is certainly most unjust to pass judgment upon anyone, even a living person, much less a dead one, from the outcome of those things which the Lord wished to be common to good and evil people.

But concerning the church of England, recently most flourishing but now, since it so seemed good to God, most afflicted, tell me, Montfort: if you possessed even the smallest particle of Christianity, would you speak as you do?

Very many are exiled from England for religion’s sake, not only the most courageous people from the common folk, but even the noblest princes. Very many most courageously underwent death in their homeland. Very many venerable heroes in prisons, formidable even to their adversaries, await the martyr’s palm with the most eager minds.

All the godly, publicly and privately, lament this scattering of the English church. Publicly and privately, by prayers and tears and every kind of duty, they all declare their love toward their excellent and dearest brethren.

But you, who have undertaken the patronage of clemency, were found alone so hard, savage, and inhuman that you calumniate as justly tormented those whom all others most rightly pity; you insult them as though they were being harassed by God’s just judgment for the cruelty which they exercised against others, when all others understand that God is exercising and testing them; and, finally, you suddenly mock amidst their calamities those whose grace and favor you lately sought through published prefaces.

But what cruelty, what violence, can you reproach them with? Is it that they spared those most cruel beasts whom, like creatures enclosed in a cage, they held so long in their power during the reign of that most holy prince Edward, by whom, now released, cru-

[PDF page 58 | logical scan 57 | printed page 141]

elly devastate the church? Or is it that they strove to draw by God’s Word rather than restrain by the sword the adversaries of religion whom they could have killed with only a nod? Are you not ashamed to insult as cruel people in whom you have nothing to reprehend except perhaps excessive mildness?

O mild and gentle Academics! O distinguished champions of Christian gentleness!

Indeed, Lord and heavenly Father, who do not wish the afflicted to be afflicted, especially those in whom tyrants persecute your only-begotten Son: Lord, who for this reason avenged yourself upon Doeg the Edomite by your just judgment, and who did not think the whole Edomite nation should be spared because they had insulted your afflicted people, for your name’s sake avenge with equal punishments these men who commit equal things.

Inspire Christian princes with courage and steadfastness to cut off all these disturbers of your church. Protect your church especially against the insults of domestic enemies. Finally, grant your exiles some corner until you come to judgment to redeem your sons, in which they may celebrate your immortal name with praises while the world rages and snarls in vain.

But where has these men’s wickedness carried me? I therefore return to the matter.

Ninth and final argument: From the authority of the ancient church.

There remains, unless I am mistaken, one argument which many customarily boast: namely, the example of the Fathers, or the authority of the ancient church.

First of all, I answer with what, I hope, all fair judges will grant me: some distinction of times and Fathers must be established. As regards the times, God does not please always to administer his church in one way, but sometimes sustains it by external protections and sometimes protects it by his Word alone when it is attacked alike by all the world’s powers. We therefore should not marvel that, at a time when the Caesars, masters of affairs, openly attacked Christ and there was no magistrate to secure the church’s external peace, heresy was not restrained by capital punishment and the apostles and those ancient bishops did not flee to the magistrate’s protection.

For by the same reasoning it is clear how, from the apostolic synod Luke describes until the time of Constantine the Great, amid such great tumults and dissensions of the churches, we read of no assembly being held in which anything was decreed against heresies by a common and universal council.

Consequently, those who infer from the example of those times, in which the church is known to have flourished, that heretics must not receive capital punishment from the Christian magistrate must necessarily also reprehend very many other things without whose protection the church stood for so many years, yet which cannot without contempt of God be rejected when he offers them to us in this time.

A little afterward, when it seemed good to the Lord to join some Caesars and kings to his church as protectors and Arius immediately broke forth, I indeed confess that Constantine spared his life and that later princes followed this example. But what if in this respect Constantine, although nothing occurred without God’s decree, was nevertheless not free of fault? He certainly should have vindicated Christ’s majesty, so wickedly and obstinately violated, more severely than his own.

Furthermore, not only the restoration of Arius himself but also the exile of Athanasius the Great declares how perversely that good prince sometimes pursued peace. Moreover, since Caesar’s protection in the church was still new, what wonder is it if at the beginning his milder course pleased both Caesar and the bishops? For Christian love and even human prudence command that we begin with the milder remedies.

But why is it necessary to pursue these points? Although Constantine spared Arius’s life, he nevertheless established a capital edict against those who concealed Arius’s books, to say nothing of those who followed his party and tore apart the church’s concord. No bishop reprehended Caesar for this as cruel, either by voice or writing. *[Marginal reference: Eusebius, Life of Constantine, book 3, chapter 65.]*

Justinian also established the same penalty against those who failed to burn the writings of Severus of Antioch.

Why, then, do these men oppose to us the perpetual agreement of the ancient church? Did those princes remove from themselves the care of religion? Did they think they had nothing to fear from heretics? Did they hesitate to establish capital penalties against factious people and those who resisted the church’s doctrine? By those penalties they abundantly testified—although in fact they did not execute them, whether from softness of mind or some other cause—what they would do if their adversaries’ importunity could not be restrained by another method.

What shall I say of Augustine? I profess that I regard him as one who, in explaining the chief heads of Christian religion, surpassed by a long interval, at least in my judgment, all the ancient writers, both Greek and Latin.

[PDF page 59 | logical scan 58 | printed page 142]

But if one chooses to follow his example without distinction, since he did not judge that the death penalty should be imposed upon the Donatists—who were not only highly seditious heretics but also most cruel murderers—what remains except that all criminals be spared, provided they join the crime of heresy to their other crimes?

What, then? In my judgment, when Augustine interceded for the Donatists’ lives, he was not considering what princes may lawfully do against heretics or what the Donatists deserved, but was contending that Caesar’s governors should do what he knew to be expedient in this cause.

For the matter concerned not one or two people, but this evil had already spread so widely throughout each city of Africa that almost more favored the heretics than the Catholics; and he saw that a financial fine had been sufficiently effective to recall most to the way. Therefore, in this cause he thought they should be dealt with by threats rather than capital punishments.

Perhaps no one after the passage of so many ages can adequately judge how rightly he formed this opinion. We have often testified that moderate severity pleases us greatly, yet so that softness is avoided.

But how rightly, I ask, can you establish a universal doctrine from this counsel of Augustine in one cause? What Augustine judged in general can be sufficiently understood from these words. Writing against Parmenian’s epistle, he says:

“From God’s hidden authority, through the manifest authority of men, many things by which they are restrained have been enacted, not only for heretics somehow whitened by the Christian name but even for pagans themselves. For they too are certainly ungodly through false religion. Their images have been commanded by recent laws to be overthrown and shattered, and sacrifices forbidden under terror of capital punishment.” *[Marginal reference: Augustine, Against the Letter of Parmenian, book 1, chapter 7.]*

Thus Augustine. Certainly, I think, he would by no means have opposed this edict to his adversary as right and praiseworthy if he had believed that it was written against God’s Word.

Moreover, when the same man intercedes for mitigation of the Donatists’ punishment, does he not everywhere confess that they deserved the utmost and praise the emperor’s gentleness because they did not act against those factious and insane people according to the utmost rigor of the law?

From this it follows that what I said a little before is true: Augustine seems not to have disputed what could lawfully be done in general, but to have contended with the greatest zeal that what he recognized for certain reasons to be expedient in this one controversy with the Donatists should be done.

But if the authority of the Fathers has so much force here that this controversy seems capable of being decided by it, come: why do we not number Moses among the Fathers, who, although otherwise the meekest of all men, so severely punished idolatry? Why do we not count Asa among the Fathers, who established a capital penalty against those who did not invoke the God of Israel? Why do we not regard Josiah as a Father, who slaughtered all the priests of the high places? *[Marginal references: Exodus 32; 2 Chronicles 15; 2 Kings 23.]*

For these are witnesses greater than every exception, and concerning these things they are praised by God’s clear voice.

Since matters stand thus, in my judgment we may affirm that the opinion that heretics must not be killed cannot be sufficiently confirmed by those passages of Scripture, by arguments fabricated in the human brain, or by the example of the Fathers.

Confirmation of the proposed question.

Now that the greatest part of the work is completed—that is, after the arguments ordinarily adduced on the contrary side have been refuted in order, insofar as we could gather them from Bellius’s medley—it remains for me to recount my reasons too, in the same order in which I thought theirs should be refuted.

I call them “mine,” although for the greatest part I collected them from the writings of other most learned men, whose authority in God’s church certainly has such weight that Bellius could not despise it without crime and wickedness.

I mean Luther, Bucer, Capito, and Regius, whom the Lord called to himself within the last few years; and, among those who by God’s grace still survive and labor vigorously in the Lord’s work, all the most learned, whom I did not wish to name individually here, lest I seem to wish to overwhelm my adversaries by the authority alone of such great men.

The extent of Bellius’s good faith and integrity in stitching together this medley appears even from his not being ashamed to name Luther and Calvin among the others. Bellius himself well knew those two eminent servants of God to be wholly foreign to the ungodliness he defends. Why should I not call that unbridled license to think and teach anything whatever “ungodliness”?

Montfort, plainly to prepare a road for his future defense, says that some people who at first, when poor, detested persecutors, afterward, when they had acquired strength, abandoned Christ’s weapons and took up the Pharisees’ weapons, without which they cannot protect their power. Yet this is plainly such a statement as cannot be made against those from whose books I excerpted most of these things without open wickedness and most manifest calumny.

But let us come to the matter. I have determined to be brief: partly because the matter itself is by no means obscure in itself, and partly because, after removing the points customarily opposed by our adversaries, a level

[PDF page 60 | logical scan 59 | printed page 143]

and unobstructed road now seems to have been made for us.

That heretics must be punished.

First, it must be remembered that a definite class of people is treated here. We have already distinguished them from unbelievers such as Jews and Turks; from those whose life or morals alone are reprehended, such as thieves and murderers; and, finally, from those who wander from the truth through mere simplicity and unwilled ignorance, without zeal for faction.

We showed that only those are called heretics who, though they claim the name of the faithful and have been lawfully convicted from God’s Word, nevertheless follow their own judgment and so obstinately and stubbornly defend against the church certain false doctrines in religion that they do not hesitate to tear apart its peace and agreement by their factions.

There is no reason to confirm by many arguments that these must be punished, since, so far as I know, no one besides these Academics has yet been found who simply asked whether they should undergo any penalty. Indeed, I believe no paradox so absurd can be found in the whole doctrine of the Stoics.

These monstrous minds are the greatest plagues in God’s church and true instruments of the devil for overthrowing the church.

For although the greatest part of humankind lives insufficiently honorably, or the church is most violently attacked by external enemies, nevertheless, so long as doctrine remains sound, it appears to people like the Cynosure, whose radiance shines before all the godly so that they can hold their course amid tempests.

But when doctrine is corrupted, and corrupted so that the devil lies hidden beneath God’s appearance, what finally remains except for very many to embrace the devil in place of God, very many to cast aside all religion after abandoning hope of knowing the truth, and, finally, a horrible disturbance to arise in God’s church?

For this evil is by far the gravest, when Satan in disguise rages within the church’s own innermost parts. It therefore needs a more prompt and also a sharper remedy. So far are we from having no duty to restrain this devouring gangrene, or even, if necessary to preserve the rest of the body, to cut it away with a cautery.

We also possess manifest testimonies of God’s Word by which we are confirmed in this opinion.
