To be silent about the laws prescribed against blasphemers and false prophets and to pass over the examples of Moses, Asa, and Josiah: when God’s Word commands us to regard as a heathen and a publican the one by whom we are privately injured, if he hears neither us nor the church, the same must certainly be done much more to one who tears down doctrine itself. *[Marginal reference: Matthew 18:17.]*

The Apostle employed this against the heretics Philetus and Hymenaeus when he delivered them to Satan. For one who is cast out of God’s church, in which God properly reigns, is delivered to the adversary’s power, and he does not recover the right of the city he lost until he has returned by right of return.

From all these things joined together it follows that those who think heretics must not be punished are attempting to introduce into God’s church the most pestilential opinion of all, diametrically opposed to the doctrine first revealed by God the Father, then restored by Christ, and finally employed with perpetual agreement by the whole orthodox church. In my judgment, they act more absurdly than if they denied that sacrilegious people or parricides must be punished, since heretics are infinitely worse than them all.

Therefore, I shall not use more words to prove this part of the question, which I trust all judges who are not wholly unjust will grant me.

That heretics may lawfully also be punished by the civil magistrate.

The remaining two parts of this question seem to require confirmation by more arguments. We shall therefore show first that heretics may also be punished by the civil magistrate, and then that they sometimes may and must even be punished by death.

Let this be the first argument. Since God created all things for himself, the chief end of human society is that God should be honored by human beings. The civil magistrate has been appointed guardian and governor of this society. Therefore, in administering this society he must have supreme regard for that chief end. Although, insofar as possible, he must provide that no discord intervene in citizens’ mutual dealings, nevertheless the final and chief end of human society is not that people should live peacefully among themselves, but rather that in living peacefully they should worship God. It is therefore the magistrate’s duty, even at the cost of external peace if he cannot otherwise do so, to redeem the true worship of God within his jurisdiction. So far is he from being able or obliged to abstain from the care of religion.

But he cannot preserve religion unless by the right of the sword he restrains its obstinate and factious despisers.

It remains, then, that those who remove the magistrate from religion either do not understand the true end of human society or conceal what they understand well.

That what I have said is true—namely, that the civil magistrate is appointed guardian and governor of human society as regards its external disci-

[PDF page 61 | logical scan 60 | printed page 144]

pline—and this especially in the cause of religion about which we inquire—I prove as follows.

Someone must certainly be placed over it to guard its external discipline. But there are altogether two kinds of government: one is the civil magistracy, the other the ecclesiastical ministry. Therefore, unless you attribute to the former this power by which the church’s external discipline is guarded, you must transfer it to the latter—unless, intolerably, you prefer to introduce anarchy into the church.

But you cannot attribute this right to ecclesiastical ministers without mixing their keys with the magistrates’ sword. It follows, then, that it pertains to the civil magistrate.

I show this confusion of different jurisdictions as follows. Suppose, for example, that some Anabaptist is reported. The presbytery will assemble; when summoned, he will answer that he will have no dealings with sinners.

What will the church do? If that unhappy man cannot be corrected by another method, it will plainly deliver him to Satan from God’s Word so that he may learn not to blaspheme. He, for his part, will willingly and voluntarily separate himself from the church. Other fanatics will follow him, and thus a defection will occur.

Then some disciple of Servetus, Schwenckfeld, or Osiander will come forward. When summoned he will appear, but in order to judge the church. Cast out of the church, he too will find disciples. Thus another faction will arise.

Finally, some Academic will appear—an upright and modest man, to be sure. When summoned, he will preface his remarks by saying that, eager to learn, he is accustomed to read everything and hear everything. If you wish to teach him, he will ask that no violence be done to his conscience.

At last, if you persist and press his manifest shamelessness in corrupting Scripture’s testimonies, he will escape by saying, contrary to the decree of the ancient Academics, who said that they knew this one thing, that they knew nothing, that everyone except him alone knows nothing; and yet he will testify that he condemns no one.

In short, however much you attempt, you will accomplish no more than if you labored to be insane with reason. Cast out of the church, he will gradually gather his own separate assembly and, after acquiring some strength, open a school of madness.

What will the church do here? “Let it cry to the Lord,” you will say, “and he will hear.” It will certainly cry and, though Satan struggles in vain, will be preserved. Yet a hungry person too will cry, but he will not wait to be fed by an angel after Elijah’s example; rather, he will receive food that is given or procured by honorable arts as if from God’s own hand. *[Marginal reference: 1 Kings 17.]*

Suppose, then, that there is a Christian magistrate in the church. I ask which of these two we must judge: that the man who would not tolerate dissension among his citizens in profane matters must remain at rest when the greatest matter and chief end of human society is at issue; or, rather, that those to whom no right of the sword was otherwise conceded may usurp this restraint?

Who does not see that if the magistrate winks at this, the greatest danger threatens the church in that region; but if the ministers invade his possession, as the Roman Antichrist did, then, after the powers distinguished by God have been confused, the greatest disturbance of all things will arise?

For what if the pastors themselves, as happens daily, degenerate into wolves? “Let them be restrained by others in an assembled synod,” you will say. Certainly rightly. For this reason it was often decreed by the ancient canons that synods should be convened several times each year throughout the individual dioceses; and among ministers otherwise endowed with equal power, certain ranks were established for the sake of order.

But what if the inferiors, both summoned and condemned, do not obey? What if a universal synod must be assembled? By whose authority will it finally be summoned? By that of a universal bishop, which Pope Gregory himself confesses to be the true name of Antichrist?

Therefore, whichever way you turn, unless you attribute to the civil magistrate the right to restrain obstinate and factious despisers of the church’s external discipline according to the gravity of their crime, infinite and incredible disorder must follow, unless the Lord brings some extraordinary remedy. For human beings are so born that they must also be restrained by human authority.

“But,” you will say, “what if the civil magistrate abuses his power? Will you not fall into the same or rather a much greater danger?” I certainly confess and acknowledge it. The church cannot be so constituted in this world that it is exposed to no dangers.

But whenever the church has ungodly princes, whether because our sins deserve it or through some hidden judgment of God so that those who are approved may become manifest, it is ours patiently to bear our lot even in the most unjust matters; but in impious matters, to obey God rather than men, yet so that we excite no sedition.

For whenever the Lord deprives his church of ordinary protections, he is accustomed to preserve it by extraordinary judgments, as happened in the times of Elijah, Jehoiada, the apostles, and finally our own.

Meanwhile, it is not lawful for us, from fear of tyrannical cruelty, to despoil the civil magistrate of the chief part of his jurisdiction and, passing over an ordinary remedy, demand another extraordinary one from God. For that fixed word of the Apostle stands:

[PDF page 62 | logical scan 61 | printed page 145]

“Every soul must be subject to the higher powers.” *[Marginal reference: Romans 13.]*

Second argument: From the authority of God’s Word.

The second argument is of this kind. It is certain that this is required of everyone: that they should serve God’s glory. For God’s glory is not only the chief and final end of the whole of human society, but also of its individual parts, and indeed of individual offices; every action both of mind and body must be referred to it. *[Marginal reference: Colossians 3:17.]*

As the servants’ duties are distinct in a very large household, so there are different offices in this world. It is clear that God’s glory cannot be advanced by individuals in one and the same way, but according to the office they have received from the Lord, so that those whose office is public should publicly serve God’s glory, and those whose office is private should do so privately.

It follows that, when the Lord commanded the king to read the volume of the Law received from the Levites so that he might learn to observe what was written in it, this had to be understood as commanding him not to order his life from God’s Word like some private person, but to administer his office according to the Law’s prescription as a king—that is, as Augustine rightly explains, not only by living faithfully, for thus he serves God insofar as he is a man, but also insofar as he is a king, by enacting with appropriate rigor laws which command just things and prohibit their contraries. *[Marginal references: Deuteronomy 17:18; Augustine, Against the Letters of Petilian, book 2, chapter 92.]*

But perhaps these things do not suffice for the obstinate minds of certain people. They will say that these matters properly pertain to the kings of the Israelites and that even from them the king’s office does not yet seem to extend to the affairs of religion.

I answer that among the Israelites the civil power was bounded by neither wider nor narrower limits than among other nations.

Furthermore, no better interpreters of that precept should be sought than the kings themselves who were comprehended under this edict. Let us therefore consider the life and deeds of those, I mean, whose principal care was not only to know the Lord’s commandments but actually to perform them.

Let David first come forward, whom the Lord found to be after his own heart and whom he also proposed to Solomon as a singular exemplar. *[Marginal reference: 1 Samuel 15.]*

How, then, did he keep the Lord’s commandments? Only as some private person? Or by administering civil affairs alone, so that he did not touch religion’s external discipline?

He rejoiced in the Lord’s strength; he trusted in the Lord and therefore was not moved; he also shepherded his people with integrity of heart and guided them with skillful hands; indeed, as a prophet he proclaimed forever and praised the God of Jacob. *[Marginal references: Psalms 21; 26; 78; 146.]*

But did he abstain from religion? “I will break,” he says, “all the horns of the ungodly, and the horns of the righteous will be exalted.” And elsewhere, describing the prince’s office, he says, “Each morning I will destroy all the ungodly in the land; I will cut off from the Lord’s city all those who practice unrighteousness.” *[Marginal references: Psalms 75:10; 101:8.]*

“But perhaps these things pertain only to criminals.” As though anything could have been said more generally, and as though it did not comprehend all against whom laws prescribed by the Lord exist.

If Bellius’s faction had therefore addressed David thus—“What do you say, eminent servant of God? Will you not also cut off those who, though good men in other matters, nevertheless dissent from you in establishing God’s worship? Certainly, no class of people is less to be feared by you; no one must be compelled to faith; you must allow the tares to grow lest you uproot the wheat; and finally, that cognition pertains to the supreme pontiff”—if Bellius, I say, or Montfort, or Cleberg, or someone from that dregs of humanity had addressed David with these words, what do we think he would have done? Would he not have supplied his vote for punishing them?

For he knew that the king’s chief virtues were to love justice and hate ungodliness. *[Marginal reference: Psalm 45:7.]* He knew that one does not hate ungodliness seriously enough if, according to the ability received from God, he does not also hinder it in others.

Finally, he knew that the Lord demands of kings and princes that they not touch the Lord’s anointed ones and prophets, but that they seem to afflict them whenever they do not prevent their affliction according to the power they received from God. *[Marginal reference: Psalm 105:15.]*

Therefore, it cannot be doubted that David actually performed what he would not have promised to the Lord if the care of the church’s external discipline did not pertain to the prince.

Otherwise, I ask, by what right was David so diligent in bringing up the ark and distributing its entire worship among the Levites? There is no doubt that whoever has the right to establish these things possesses the power to guard them. *[Marginal reference: 2 Samuel 6.]*

What of Solomon? Does he not execute his father’s commands, to such a degree that he even deprives the high priest himself of the priesthood’s dignity? *[Marginal reference: 1 Kings 2.]* But as soon as he departs from his father’s footsteps, is he not deserted by God and the kingdom of Israel for that reason divided into two parts?

This was indeed the beginning of every evil: Solomon despised the church’s ancient discipline and wished to fabricate a new one. So far are princes from having to be removed from its government.

Let us come to Asa, who is numbered first after David and Solomon among the good princes. He did not indeed wish to burn incense, as Uzziah later did, nor did he seize the functions of the ecclesiastical min-

[PDF page 63 | logical scan 62 | printed page 146]

istry to himself, but he removed the foreign altars.

Although Montfort will be compelled to confess that this pertains to religion, he will nevertheless perhaps say that the case of heretics is different, since they do not teach that foreign gods must be worshiped. We shall discuss this later in its place.

What more, then, did Asa do? He demolished the high places. Yet at some high places sacrifice was offered to the true God of Israel. Why, then, did he demolish them? Because heretics sacrificed there, who of course defended themselves splendidly by the ancient custom of the Fathers, as can be established from Christ’s conversation with the Samaritan woman, and boasted of the true worship of God. *[Marginal reference: John 4.]*

Did Asa therefore leave them to their own judgment? Did he refer them to the priests, whom he well knew they stubbornly despised? By no means. Since he possessed God’s manifest Word concerning sacrifices to be made only in the place the Lord had chosen, and since beneath the appearance of piety these men had declared open war upon God’s church, he overthrew the high places no less than the idolaters’ images and groves.

Then, not content to have cut off heresies and manifest idolatries, he assembled the people, restored God’s worship, and renewed the covenant with solemn ceremonies, adding this formula of oath: whoever did not invoke the God of Israel would be killed, with no regard for age or sex.

But these deeds are as praiseworthy if we believe the Holy Spirit’s testimony as they are execrable if we listen to Bellius.

For the Holy Spirit says: “Asa did what was good and right before the Lord.” And a little afterward, when the oath was formulated in the terms I have given, he says: “They had sworn and sought the Lord with their whole heart, and he was found by them, and the Lord gave them peace on every side.” *[Marginal reference: 2 Chronicles 14–15.]*

But what does Bellius say in his fine preface? “Who would not think Christ to be some Moloch or some god of that kind if he wished living human beings to be sacrificed and burned to him?”

If Bellius’s opinion is true, what prevents us from regarding Elijah as a murderer and sacrilegious person who thought God, like Moloch, delighted in human slaughter? And you too, Jehu, who did and judged the same? And as for you, most holy King Josiah, there is no reason for us not to accuse you of savagery and cruelty, since you raged even against the bones of the dead, slaughtering the priests of the high places upon the altars and even burning human bones. *[Marginal references: 1 Kings 18; 2 Kings 10; 2 Kings 23.]*

But far from us be those blasphemies and profane thoughts. We praise their deed with the Holy Spirit. We praise Jehoshaphat too, offspring worthy of a holy father, because, after removing the high places and cutting down the groves, he appointed teachers by whom the people in each city might be instructed in the Lord’s Law, and he most wisely distributed ecclesiastical judgments. *[Marginal reference: 2 Chronicles 17.]*

We also approve the people’s killing of Mattan the priest, and magnificently extol, as is fitting, Hezekiah’s singular diligence in restoring God’s worship. *[Marginal references: 2 Kings 11; 2 Kings 18.]*

From all these examples of the best kings it is sufficiently clear, unless I am mistaken, that the Lord’s command that kings receive a special copy of the Law from the Levites, and what David later demands more generally of every king and prince—namely, that they serve the Lord and kiss the Son—must be understood as commanding princes by these words to use their power especially for the end for which they received it from God: that is, to labor to preserve and enlarge religion against every domestic or external enemy, whatever dangers Satan finally displays like specters. So far is merely civil peace from being proposed to the Christian magistrate as his final and chief end, since, as Isaiah says, they must be the church’s nourishers and foster parents. *[Marginal references: Deuteronomy 17:18; Psalm 2; Isaiah 49.]*

Furthermore, since everyone is required to confess God with the mouth—that is, to declare by every external action that they assent to true doctrine, but abhor and, according to their strength, hinder contrary doctrine—it follows that princes are bound to this confession not as private persons, but as princes.

Therefore, the magistrate who, by the ministry received from God, works to preserve God’s church must truly be said to confess God with his mouth. But if he acts otherwise, how will he finally escape that horrible sentence of Christ? “Whoever denies me before human beings,” Christ says, “I also will deny before my heavenly Father.” *[Marginal reference: Matthew 10:33.]*

Whoever can advance God’s glory but rests securely as though the matter had nothing to do with him certainly denies God much more shamelessly than one who through fear of death renounces religion by pretense.

Consequently, how will the prince who, relying on the protection of his power, can restrain a wanton and obstinate heretic but does not do so escape the crime of having betrayed the commonwealth to the devil? Or what reason will he adduce for not being a participant in the church’s stumbling blocks?

Thus we see that it has often happened that, when princes were inactive, the Lord raised up by his Spirit certain eminent heroes who extraordinarily discharged the office of princes in attacking heretics and restoring true religion, as we read in the histories of Phinehas, Jehoiada, Elijah, and Mattathias. Although their deeds must not rashly be drawn into an example, nevertheless,

[PDF page 64 | logical scan 63 | printed page 147]

since they are commended by God’s open testimony, they are most worthy to be proposed for imitation to those in whom the Lord wished that power to be ordinary and perpetual which in them was extraordinary and temporary.

Finally, since very many capital laws were prescribed against manifest and convicted despisers of the first Table, while the ecclesiastical ministry has no power of the sword, it remains either that the Lord established those laws in vain—which cannot be said without mocking him—or that he entrusted their execution to the civil power.

That this is so is clear not only from the examples of the holy kings we mentioned a little earlier, but also from the first tractate of the book which the Hebrews call *Massecheth Sanhedrin*, as that man Wolfgang Capito of blessed memory taught.

For it is written there that the great Senate of seventy judges, in which the king himself presided with great ceremony, took cognizance of the crime of defection from God. We perhaps have an example of this judgment, or at least one not very dissimilar, in Jeremiah. *[Marginal reference: Jeremiah 26.]*

The civil magistrate was therefore also employed as guardian of the first Table as regards external discipline. Consequently, let our adversaries either teach that Christ’s advent detracted something from civil jurisdiction, or confess that the princes’ office is now the same as it formerly was.

I am not now disputing whether Christian princes are bound by those Israelite laws, but rather asking whether Christ changed or removed the boundaries of civil power.

For, unless I am greatly mistaken, either it must be proved by firm arguments that Christ did this, which I think impossible; or it must be confessed that, just as formerly by Moses’s political law, so today at least by civil laws the duty of preserving Christian religion as regards external discipline is imposed upon the civil magistrate.

Ecclesiastical history can show that, without any controversy among the orthodox, this was both believed and practiced no less in our Christian church than in the ancient Israelite church whenever it had Christian princes.

Let Eusebius, Theodoret, and Nicephorus be read. Let the letters called synodical be read. Let the acts of the councils be read, especially that Council of Carthage in which the ambitious fraud of the Roman Antichrist was exposed. Let everything Augustine argued against the Donatists and Circumcellions be read, and we shall find all the universal synods summoned, held, and confirmed by the authority of Christian Caesars; heretics condemned, expelled, and sometimes restored by the Caesars’ sentence after the bishops’ anathema; laws concerning bishops and clerics delivered by them; edicts concerning the catholic faith prescribed against heretics; and, finally, everything done that pertains to preserving the church’s external discipline. This occurred not only with all orthodox bishops approving, but even with them demanding it with one consent by voice and writing.

Finally, those turbulent tempests occurred in which the Roman Empire was tossed. The Roman bishops, fishing in troubled water, as the saying goes, gradually established their tyranny and progressed so far as to subject the emperors and kings themselves to their rule, as John had long before foretold would happen. *[Marginal reference: Revelation 17.]*

This was certainly a horrible judgment of God. But in my judgment, what these Academics attempt to introduce into God’s church under the appearance of moderation and clemency is something worse than papal tyranny itself: so much worse as tyranny is a lesser evil than anarchy and as it is better to have even the cruelest tyrant than no prince at all—or at least, as the man said, to have one under whom everything is lawful for everyone.

Let this therefore be the conclusion of this argument: those who exclude the Christian magistrate from the care of religion, and especially from restraining heretics, despise God’s manifest Word and spurn the authority of every age, and thus contemplate the church’s utmost disorder.

Third argument: From the perpetual custom of external nations.

Moreover, even all those peoples before whom the torch of God’s Word did not shine saw what great disadvantages arise from this opinion formerly of the Donatists and now of Bellius’s faction. Thus every Christian prince must be subjected to great infamy if they seem to have understood the parts of their office less rightly than those people.

Certainly, none of the ancient princes or legislators lived in such great darkness that, although they were without God, as Paul speaks, they nevertheless judged that the prince has no role in establishing religion. This can be understood from the example of Numa alone. *[Marginal reference: Ephesians 2:12.]*

Just as they must be greatly reprehended because they wished to make the laws of religion themselves—which we showed belongs properly to God alone—so they certainly merit great praise because they did not think the care of religion foreign to them.

Among those examples of external princes, certain ones comprehended in the sacred writings are found worthy of particular praise and atten-

[PDF page 65 | logical scan 64 | printed page 148]

tion: for example, the most holy edicts of Nebuchadnezzar, Darius, Cyrus, Darius Hystaspes, and Artaxerxes, plainly recorded in the sacred writings by the Holy Spirit so that Christian princes might be stimulated to do their duty at least by the example of external princes. *[Marginal references: Daniel 3 and 6; Ezra 1, 6, and 7.]*

For, I ask, how will a Christian prince escape God’s judgment if he winks at the impious violation of God’s majesty which otherwise profane princes vindicated with such zeal? By what right will the Christian magistrate nurture blasphemers and factious people whom a Babylonian king punished with the bitterest penalties?

Finally, with what face will a Christian prince conduct trials for injured majesty if he allows pure religion to be contaminated, God’s Word treated with contempt, and ministers of the Word harassed by the most abandoned scoundrels under the pretense of clemency and modesty?

For these are their arts: namely, as that man said about Philip seeking the empire of Greece, that once the shepherds have been intercepted they may plunder the Lord’s flock with impunity. Yet the faithful Lord will surely guard his church and preserve his faithful shepherds.

Final argument: From the agreement of the church’s recent doctors.

But let me omit the whole of ecclesiastical history, say nothing of the examples of the ancient kings, princes, and bishops, and pass over the titles concerning the catholic faith and concerning bishops and clerics, the Novels, the canons of the councils, and especially canons 94, 68, and 95 of the Council of Carthage, and, finally, Augustine’s many books written against the Donatists concerning this controversy. Come, let us see the opinion of those through whom it seemed good to the Lord in our time to kindle again the light of his Gospel which had almost been extinguished among human beings.

I have not determined to mention everyone, for that would be almost infinite labor, but shall recount only the principal writers known to me. Among these are even some whose name and authority these men were not ashamed to abuse in order to dazzle the simple person’s eyes with the empty radiance of their names.

Thus Luther, explaining the parable of the tares in the so-called Shorter Postils for the fifth Sunday after Epiphany, says:

“The magistrate guards not only the second, but above all the first Table. He avenges idolatries, blasphemies, curses, and perjuries. He restrains heretics brought before him as those who contemptuously affront the true deity, and others who teach those blasphemies. He undertakes to punish as malefactors those who are more stubborn and more contumacious in weaving their error, to the most certain ruin of many.

“Nor, when he does this, is he understood to uproot the tares, because in discharging his office he does not act in that kingdom in which uprooting the tares is forbidden.”

And a little afterward he says: “Therefore, when the magistrate and the officers of this kind of bodily life meet scandals and disturbances of tranquility with severe punishment, they do not uproot the tares contrary to Christ’s command. They perform their part so that the true wheat in the church is not wholly oppressed by the tares and God’s kingdom does not entirely disappear from the earth.”

What shall I say of Melanchthon, that most learned man, who, besides affirming the same as Luther in countless passages, also wrote a special book *On the Prince’s Office* to refute the contrary opinion?

I hear that Urbanus Rhegius, that most diligent man, did the same in the name of the church of Lüneburg, although his little book was not at hand when I wrote these things.

But the churches of Saxony testified in these words that they hold this opinion generally, in the confession which they presented not long ago to that conventicle of Trent:

“God wishes the highest and immutable laws which prohibit the worship of idols, blasphemies, and perjuries first to be proposed to the magistrate by the voice.”

And a little afterward: “The magistrate is guardian of the Law, namely, of the first and second Table as regards discipline.”

Likewise elsewhere: “Although many unlearned people neglect God’s glory, nevertheless this ought to be the chief care: to hear and embrace the true doctrine concerning God’s Son and nurture the churches, as the Psalm says, ‘And now, O kings, understand.’ Likewise, ‘Open your gates, O princes,’ that is, open your realms to the Gospel; offer hospitality to God’s Son.

“And Isaiah 49: ‘Kings will be your nourishers and queens’—that is, let commonwealths be nurses of the church; let them offer hospitality to the church and godly studies. Let kings and princes themselves also be members of the church and understand doctrine rightly. Let them not assist those who establish false doctrine and exercise unjust savagery. Let them see that judgments in the church are rightly conducted, as Constantine, Theodosius, Arcadius, Marcian, Charlemagne, and many godly kings took care that the church’s judgments were rightly conducted.”

Brentius wrote the following in his little book *On Administering the Commonwealth Well*:

“Before all else it will be the duty of a godly and resolute prince not to allow his people to be defrauded of this most efficacious food of God’s Word and antidote of faith for afflicted consciences. By no means should he allow through sluggish labor, but command with the greatest diligence, that this very Word be proclaimed to the people purely and sincerely, without any poison of human traditions.

“For

[PDF page 66 | logical scan 65 | printed page 149]

since it is the Word of peace and righteousness, and the prince’s reciprocal and chief duty is to plant and advance the commonwealth’s peace, tranquility, concord, and justice, how, I ask, can he better and more happily obtain his desire than if he allows the Crucified One’s pure doctrines and the Word of peace to be spread purely and freely?

“Since all power also belongs to God, it will accordingly be fitting for every power to advance this Word of its Lord with every sinew. Although the far more excellent and preeminent kind of righteousness and peace is proposed in the Gospel, it nevertheless brings with it into the midst also that external peace and justice.

“For how many are there who truly possess peace with God through Christ and yet fail to preserve peace with their neighbor? Whatever controversies, tragedies, and lawsuits exist, it is fitting to believe that they arise from no other source than that peace of conscience with God has not yet been acquired. As soon as you have attained it, it will easily bring that civil peace together with itself, as a fruit of that divine peace.

“Consequently, a prudent magistrate must apply himself especially to this: that this Word of peace and not sedition be proclaimed with the greatest possible diligence.”

Bucer set forth his opinion in these words in his expositions of Matthew 22:

“From the position long held, and held now, among all who think rightly, that no one must be compelled to faith or religion, some make the magistrate’s office entirely foreign to matters of faith and religion. They permit him to establish, amend, or even punish nothing concerning them, assigning him wholly to secular affairs alone.

“These people seem to think insufficiently piously about this sublime office of power. Princes and magistrates are called gods in Scripture. Therefore, they too ought to perform as certain gods among human beings, removing evils and advancing goods in such a way that they take care first to advance and protect the chief goods and with particular zeal avert the more harmful evils.

“Since religion not only excels all other goods, but without it nothing is good and salutary to the human race, it will certainly follow that the magistrate must first be devoted to the care of vindicating and preserving religion. Nothing more pestilential could happen to a people than that this be violated among them; nothing more salutary than that it persist safe and sound.

“Hence in the Law magistrates were most solemnly commanded that, besides counselors, Levites, and priests, they themselves should diligently read God’s Law: plainly so that, just as God must be loved before all things, they should first labor that his worship be conducted rightly.

“Hence the Law made capital not only adulteries, homicides, and more atrocious crimes of that kind, but also if anyone should lead others away from God’s worship by teaching, establish for himself a rite, idols, or other superstitions, consult diviners, speak abuse against God, and similar things which properly pertain to the care of religion.

“But God does not change. Therefore, it is fitting that the magistrate also punish these same things today. True religion was no less precious among the ancients than today, and then as now it was the work of the Holy Spirit.

“Nevertheless, since both doctrine and ceremonies are external, the public power was placed by God over human affairs for this purpose: that these things should be in the best condition, that is, according to his will, and thus before all else his worship should flourish. Since nothing more harmful can occur than impious doctrine and superstitious ceremonies, even among us it will be the magistrate’s duty to take care that no one sins openly in these things. Hidden matters must be left to God’s judgment.”

The same writer says a little afterward:

“Where ungodly magistrates reign, God must be prayed to convert them to himself or exchange them for godly ones. Meanwhile, let each person retain what he has received from the Lord until he grants otherwise. Whatever those magistrates command to the contrary, one must say with Peter, ‘It is better to obey God than human beings.’

“But it is by no means fitting to go so far, because many abuse their power, especially in sacred matters, as therefore to make the care of religion foreign to the magistrate’s office, which not only divine law but also the laws of all wise people have enacted must be the first care of anyone who exercises power.”

Capito too wrote an entire little book concerning the magistrate’s right over religion against a certain jurist who was certainly of the Academic faction. I have excerpted a few passages from it so that one may behold in a few words the opinion of that excellent and most learned man.

“The Lord,” he says, “so greatly wished his Word to be the magistrate’s care that, to vindicate those offenses committed against the true worship of God, he armed the foremost power of the world with laws and oracles.

“But our counselor, as a marvelous advocate is accustomed to do, uses external tranquility as a pretext, seeking nothing but the tranquility of these affairs. He punishes thieves and robbers because they harm the public. But one who is contemptuous of the divine majesty, so that by his doctrine and example he turns others away both from divine worship and from passing their life blamelessly among human beings, will through him remain unpunished because such people do not disturb this flesh.”

These words express the man’s opinion. What, then, will the godly prince do here? He will certainly compel the people, according to his strength, to hear God’s Word and to express it in life and morals, by severe authority, that is, lawful punishment.

“He will consider himself God’s minister no less than the ancient kings. From this peace of conscience will be raised up for himself and the people, which Christ alone grants and which surpasses every understanding. For then, with God’s approval, each party will seem to have satisfied the duties of its office toward the other.”

Thus far Capito.

Finally, to show also the agreement of our churches—that is, the Swiss churches—it is unnecessary for me to recount at greater length here what that most faithful pastor of the church of Zurich, Bullinger, thinks about this whole controversy, since he himself wrote about the matter so co-

[PDF page 67 | logical scan 66 | printed page 150]

piously and clearly in sermons 7 and 8 of the Second Decade. You, Montfort, have properly instituted a refutation against him, which we shall discuss a little later.

Musculus, that distinguished interpreter of the Scriptures, openly declares how greatly he dissents from you Academics in very many other places, but especially in his commentary on Psalm 2.

For in explaining the passage “SERVE THE LORD WITH FEAR,” he writes:

“Let those also note this passage who deny that royal power and the so-called secular magistrate have anything to do in the cause of religion. The Holy Spirit admonishes the kings and judges of the earth to serve the Lord. He means the service owed to God’s Son.

“Let them answer here in what respect princes must serve Christ if there is absolutely nothing in religion that can or should be administered through them.

“It is established that Christ’s kingdom in believers’ hearts is spiritual. That is true. Yet as regards the mind’s internal motions, not even the work of the one who preaches God’s Word can accomplish anything unless the Holy Spirit is added.

“But who of sound mind will meanwhile deny that God is served by preaching the Word? Therefore, when princes take care by their power that the doctrine of the Word is retained in the church, idolatry and false worship are removed, the church’s ministers are suitably supported, and adversaries suppressed; then forbid God’s name to be blasphemed, and take care that those who live piously act securely, while the ungodly and restless are punished and restrained—in what respect do they not serve Christ?”

In my judgment it is by no means necessary to repeat in this place the opinion of the Genevan church. It is sufficiently declared partly by that most just punishment exacted from Servetus, and partly by that golden book in which the whole argument was comprehended briefly yet so skillfully by Calvin, that theologian full of judgment and faith, that I marvel that after the completion of his little book anyone could still be found to raise a controversy concerning this question.

Come then, Bellius, where finally is the agreement—not to say of the ancients, but of those very men in whom you so greatly pride yourself? Where are those vindications according to liberty? Where is your incomprehensibility in the chief heads of religion? Where is that good conscience of heretics joined with ignorance?

But concerning these matters, I think, more than enough.

That heretics must sometimes also be restrained by capital punishment.

It remains for us to confirm the final part of the proposed question: that heretics must sometimes also be restrained by capital punishment.

Here, however, I foresee that my business will be not only with our Academics but also with otherwise godly and learned men; and the former’s singular shamelessness must no less be sharply beaten back, in my judgment, than the latter must be disputed with calmly and amicably. I therefore ask the latter, if they find anything said by us rather harshly, to receive it as said not against themselves but against the former’s open wickedness.

For the unbridled audacity of a certain Basilius Montfort, whoever he may be who hides beneath this name, compels me in some manner to turn aside from my path.

Since he wrote a refutation of those things which, as he speaks, are ordinarily said in favor of persecution—but in fact in favor of the just and lawful punishment of the most abandoned people—I am plainly compelled so to arrange this part of our confirmation that it comprises an antithesis.

I desire this to be done without any offense to those from whom I profess that I dissent neither concerning punishment nor the judge who imposes it, but only concerning the mode of punishment, and in such a way that, if they adduce better reasons from God’s Word, I shall willingly yield to their opinion.

First, I ask them diligently to consider the definition of a heretic which we proposed and, unless I am mistaken, confirmed sufficiently suitably. That is, let them remember that we call neither unbelievers, hypocrites, those properly called schismatics, nor those who simply err “heretics,” but only those who, although they use supreme zeal for piety as a pretext, nevertheless, when duly admonished by the church’s sound discourses, not only fail to acquiesce but also tear apart the church’s peace and agreement by a false doctrine proposed or seized.

Next, I ask and implore them—as we have often said already and shall repeat here for a third time—to be persuaded that our doctrine neither incites princes to exercise cruelty nor confuses civil power with the ecclesiastical function.

Finally, let them remember that we distinguish heresies themselves in such a way that we confess there is a great difference of persons and errors.

It seemed good to preface these things also for this reason, so that everyone may recognize the extent of Montfort’s shamelessness. For although he selected from everyone Henry Bullinger, pastor of the church of Zurich, as the one man for him to attack—a man of proven integrity and so foreign to every suspicion of cruelty that nothing could be more so—he nevertheless did not fear to begin that refutation of his with the most unjust and open calumnies.

For when Bullinger began the cause we are disputing in the eighth sermon of his Second Decade with these words, “It is still disputed today whether it is lawful for the magistrate to punish those subjected to his power on account of religion either despised or treated with contempt,” Montfort replied:

“There are some,” he says, “who wish every heretic—that is, everyone who dissents from them—to be killed, of whatever race or nation, if only this can be done. Others think foreigners should not be invaded, just as fighters had not been commanded to invade foreign nations, but to punish people of their own body. Such is the wolf’s caution: he does not attack the lion; the sheep is safer.”

Thus Montfort. Let him say

[PDF page 68 | logical scan 67 | printed page 151]

if he can, to whom finally these things apply. To the man whose arguments he refuted individually a little later? Yet that man expressly testifies that he is asking about the magistrate’s authority over his own subjects, and calls “heretics” not those who think differently from himself, but those who despise religion or treat it with contempt.

Do they then apply to anyone else among the Saxon or Swiss churches? If he can, let him name even one who judged that every heretic should be killed or was so pleased with himself that he regarded everyone who thought differently as a heretic.

If he can name no one of this kind, how should one regard a man who calumniates the most innocent people against the testimony of his conscience?

Will he plead that these things were written against papists, whom he nevertheless fears so greatly that in his entire little book he did not dare to name them? Yet the arguments he refutes afterward are not the papists’ but are copied word for word from Bullinger.

Further, not even the papists would say this, for they are not so foolish that they fail to distinguish unbelievers subject to a foreign prince from heretics, unless perhaps war is in progress.

What then? Plainly, these things were written against the men by whose diligence and attentiveness he saw his evil counsels obstructed. Immediately, full of fury, he spewed out against them whatever came into his mouth in order to indulge his mind.

As regards foreigners, whom did he finally think he could persuade that we abstain from them as wolves abstain from the lion, because it is safer to attack a sheep?

As though Anabaptists or Servetus—for he can object only these to us—should be numbered among the sheep and not rather among those savage and monstrous wolves who do not spare the flock; or as though any suspicion of such fear could fall upon us, who do not cease daily to provoke the papists by speech and writings and who indeed fear nothing from being killed by those same men for Christ’s religion.

But let us also hear with what purpose he undertook to write this refutation.

“I refuted in this writing, as I could, their arguments with a good and religious zeal, so that, if possible, they might open their eyes; so that, if perhaps they err unknowingly, they might cease to err. For it is better to recover one’s senses late than never. Then, so that others might not be deceived by their authority.”

Plainly, Montfort approached this refutation with a good and religious zeal! Why, then, did he begin it with manifest calumnies? Why did he suppress his name? Why did he not rather confer amicably and as a Christian with those whose singular piety, learning, and modesty he had long ago experienced in reality?

“I would indeed,” he says, “willingly have heard them myself and conferred with them amicably and as a Christian. But since they dispute with iron, flames, and waters, we, armed with no iron—for Christ forbade us iron weapons in this war—will conduct the affair from afar by the Word, like Jotham the son of Jerubbaal.”

But where did he detect any trace of this inhumanity or cruelty? With whom was there disputation by iron, flames, or waters? “With Anabaptists,” he will say, “and with Servetus.” Yet the whole world refutes that lie.

This man must plainly be either an Anabaptist or a Servetian. He so wretchedly fears not us but our princes that he did not even dare publish his true name. Otherwise, what occasion for just fear could he have had?

But even if he is conscious of those heresies in himself, if he is guided by zeal for truth, what danger threatened him from the ministers of our churches? Are they accustomed to contend with anyone by any weapons other than God’s Word? Whom did they report to the magistrates who conferred amicably and as a Christian with them, even if he lived in some most pestilential error? When did they exhort princes to any cruelty? When did they ascend the judges’ bench to condemn some Anabaptist or Servetus?

Those claims, then, are empty, futile, and false, prepared partly to conceal ambition and partly to ensnare the simple.

But I shall come to the matter.

First argument: From the gravity of the heretic’s crime.

We shall make three classes of arguments. First, we shall reason from the quality and nature of the crime about which we inquire.

For it is agreed among everyone, unless I am mistaken, that when the mode of punishment is at issue, only that punishment seems equitable which, insofar as possible, corresponds to the magnitude of the crime.

Otherwise, I also confess that, since crimes are punished for the sake of example, judges must take great account of humanity and diligently consider what benefits the commonwealth. Hence it occurs that one and the same crime is restrained more mildly in one place and more bitterly in another, and indeed in the same region sometimes by a milder and sometimes by a severer penalty.

But certain crimes are of such a kind that, although they are not punished by the same kind of punishment among everyone, nevertheless by a certain common right they are regarded as capital among all nations that are not wholly savage. Such are parricide, voluntary homicide, sacrilege, blasphemy, ungodliness or the public violation of the received religion, and whatever other things are of that kind.

I think matters are sufficiently established concerning parricide, other voluntary homicides, and sacrilege. But I marvel that some doubt concerning blasphemy and ungodliness. No one can deny that the magnitude of a crime must be estimated from the quality of the one said to be offended.

For this reason parricide too is distinguished from simple homicide. If this is true,

[PDF page 69 | logical scan 68 | printed page 152]

it also follows, unless I am mistaken, that blasphemy and ungodliness, by which God’s majesty is properly injured, are so much greater crimes as God’s glory excels human dignity.

Yet such is the blindness of the human race that it is especially blind in the chief matter, that is, in God’s worship. Therefore, punishment among human beings was established not against every blasphemer and ungodly person, but only against those who sinned knowingly and deliberately.

Moreover, because it is extremely difficult to judge the mind, a certain constant rule had to be established from which it could be understood who seemed to have sinned knowingly and deliberately.

Thus, by a certain law of nations, the rule is this: since it is probable that everyone holds the religion in which they were born and educated, whoever does or says something by which that religion is torn down seems to have committed it knowingly and deliberately.

Therefore, when Meletus accused Socrates of impiety, he framed the charge in these words: “Socrates does wrong by not acknowledging the gods whom the city acknowledges, and by introducing other new divinities.” *[Marginal note: The charge against Socrates.]*

But since human beings must err in countless ways when establishing religion, as regards external and profane nations this rule was certainly most unjust.

Yet in Christian churches, in which the kindled lamp of God’s Word dispels all darkness, nothing can be done more rightly than by this one norm and rule.

I call those “Christian churches” in which those certain and peculiar marks appear: namely, God’s clear voice sounding forth, obscured by no human traditions, and the pure and lawful administration of the sacraments, by which doctrine itself is sealed in the minds of the godly.

Therefore, in these churches in which the sheep hear the shepherd’s voice, it is most equitable that this rule should prevail: whoever teaches a different doctrine in them, especially after the church’s repeated admonition, should be regarded as sinning against religion knowingly and deliberately.

For if anyone is wholly outside the church, plainly a foreigner and unbeliever, although before God his blasphemy and ungodliness are not excused, he must nevertheless be left to God, who will judge those outside or in his time bestow upon them the light of his Spirit.

But what if someone from the church’s flock lives in error? Since he suffers from a human sickness, he must plainly be treated with milder remedies so that he may recover.

Thus, besides believers’ private admonitions and mutual conversations concerning piety, the church’s judgments too were established against those who need somewhat sharper remedies.

But if heresy is added to blasphemy and ungodliness—that is, contumacious contempt of God’s Word and ecclesiastical discipline—and a certain insane fury for corrupting others too has invaded, what greater or more criminal offense could finally exist among human beings? Indeed, if the mode of punishment must be established according to the crime’s magnitude, no equal punishment seems capable of being found for this crime.

Further, if you consider the harm to human beings in reckoning this crime, I seem able also to affirm that no class of people more destructive and pestilential can be found.

One who kills another person, violates another’s wife, intercepts someone’s property, or finally commits some crime of that kind injures the commonwealth in such a way that some estimate of the damage can nevertheless be made. But one who opens a way in some assembly for corrupting the true worship of God certainly kindles a fire which will perhaps scarcely be extinguished at last by the eternal destruction of countless people.

For the corruption of true religion must necessarily be followed by the deity’s most grievous wrath.

Therefore, whether the magistrate has undertaken to vindicate God’s glory or preserve human society, he must nowhere punish any people more severely than blasphemous and ungodly heretics.

At Athens, therefore, the trial for impiety was capital even for the accuser if he did not prosecute the defendant to conviction. We read that an example of this judgment was made in the case of three supreme philosophers: Socrates, of whom we spoke a little before; Theodorus, who was surnamed “the atheist,” as Diogenes testifies on the authority of Amphicrates; and Protagoras of Abdera. Cicero reports that, after Protagoras wrote at the beginning of a certain book, “Concerning the gods, I cannot say either that they exist or that they do not,” by command of the Athenians he was expelled from the city and territory, and his books burned in the assembly—the punishment of so great a crime plainly displayed rather than paid. *[Marginal references: Pollux, book 8; Diogenes Laertius, book 2; Cicero, On the Nature of the Gods, book 1.]*

Thus, to say what the matter is, one is moved to marvel at the judgment of those who wish the penalty in a Christian commonwealth for prostituting true religion and trampling ecclesiastical discipline to be milder than formerly among profane nations for violating superstition.

Can any judge be so slothful, so forgetful of his office, that, while he punishes murderers, thieves, forgers, and those guilty of injured princely majesty with capital punishment, he seeks praise for clemency by preserving those whom he has recognized in reality to have defected from God and to solicit others to dare the same, after corrupting God’s Word, despising the church’s authority, and obstinately neglecting the princes’ edicts under a pretense of simplicity and modesty?

Montfort’s objection is ridiculous: that by this same reasoning unbelievers must be punished,

[PDF page 70 | logical scan 69 | printed page 153]

since unbelief is the greatest vice; likewise those who hate their brother, since they are murderers; and those who desire another’s wife, since they are adulterers.

For, as we said before, heresy must not be numbered among those vices which, because they lie hidden within, must be left to God’s judgment, but rather among those crimes by which external concord and tranquility are violated both in civil affairs, because of God’s wrath, and in religion.

We proved sufficiently suitably in its place, unless I am mistaken, that the magistrate ordinarily punishes not vices but crimes.

Second argument: From the authority of God’s Word.

But why, I ask, prove by human conjectures with what penalty this crime must be vindicated, when plain and open testimonies of God are read in his Word?

For he says: “Whoever sacrifices to other gods besides God alone shall die.” *[Marginal reference: Exodus 22:20.]*

Elsewhere he writes a law sharper and severer than any read in God’s whole covenant:

“If a prophet arises in your midst, or one who says that he has seen a dream, and foretells a sign and wonder, and what he spoke occurs, and he says to you, ‘Let us go after other gods whom you have not known, and serve them,’ such a prophet or interpreter of dreams must be killed, because he has spoken defection from the Lord your God, in order to drive you from the way which the Lord prescribed to you that you might walk in it. Therefore, you shall exterminate the evil from your midst.

“If your brother, your mother’s son, or your son, or your daughter, or the wife in your bosom, or your neighbor whom you love as your own life secretly solicits you, saying, ‘Let us go and serve foreign gods whom neither you nor your fathers knew,’ do not acquiesce in him or hear him. Let your eye not spare him; do not indulge or conceal him. Rather, you shall utterly kill him, and let your hand be first upon him that such a person may be killed, then the hand of the whole people. Overwhelm him with stones until he dies, for he attempted to drive you from the Lord your God who brought you out of Egypt, from the house of bondage. Let all the Israelites hear and fear, so that they no longer devise an abominable thing in your midst.

“If in one of your cities which the Lord your God gives you to inhabit you hear this report, ‘Men, sons of Belial, have gone out from your midst and solicited the citizens of their city in these words: “Let us go and serve other gods whom we have not known,”’ you shall inquire, investigate, and question diligently. If that report is true and certain, that an abomination of this kind has been done in your midst, you shall utterly strike the inhabitants of that city with the sword, and devastate it with the sword, and whatever is in it, even its livestock.

“You shall gather all its spoils into the middle of its marketplace, and burn the city with fire, and all its spoils entirely to the Lord your God, so that it may be a perpetual heap and never be rebuilt.

“Nothing from that thing under the ban shall cling to your hand, so that the Lord may turn from the fury of his wrath, be moved toward you with intimate affection, have mercy upon you, and multiply you, just as he swore to your fathers.” *[Marginal reference: Deuteronomy 13.]*

Again in the same book:

“When there is found in your midst, within one of your gates which the Lord your God gives you, a man or woman who does what is evil in the eyes of the Lord your God so as to transgress his covenant, and goes and worships other gods and adores them—such as the sun and the moon, or any host of heaven concerning which I did not command—and it is reported to you and you hear it, inquire diligently. If it is true and certain that this abomination was done in Israel, you shall bring such a man or such a woman who perpetrated this evil deed to your gates; you shall bring out, I say, the man or woman, and overwhelm them with stones, and let that person die.” *[Marginal reference: Deuteronomy 17.]*

Against the blasphemer, however, this law is prescribed:

“Whoever curses his God shall bear his sin; and whoever blasphemes the Lord’s name shall be punished by death. The whole assembly shall utterly overwhelm him with stones, whether he is native or resident. Whoever blasphemes the Lord’s name shall be killed.” *[Marginal reference: Leviticus 24.]*

One who violated the Sabbath was punished by this law: “The one who profanes the Sabbath shall be punished by death. Whoever performs work on it, that soul shall be cut off from his people.” *[Marginal reference: Exodus 31.]*

Since these laws were established by God, the Best and Greatest, Montfort must confess even unwillingly that no more equitable or better laws concerning these matters can be established.

Nevertheless, it is worthwhile to recognize how we finally reason from these laws. In order that this may be explained more suitably, we shall repeat the whole matter from a somewhat higher point, since this necessity is imposed upon us.

For we acknowledge that these are political laws prescribed for the Jewish nation alone. Nor are we so ignorant that we wish to recall the Mosaic polity, as though it were not lawful for each magistrate to enact particular laws concerning civil affairs within his jurisdiction.

But since the Law given through Moses has three parts—one called the Moral Law or Decalogue, another the Ceremonial, and a third the Judicial—each of these parts needs particular consideration.

For the Decalogue, or the law they call Moral,

[PDF page 71 | logical scan 70 | printed page 154]

because it is an accurate description of natural law and the testimony of conscience agreeing with God’s perpetual will, we must acknowledge to possess such authority that it must be the certain rule of justice and injustice for every nation and age, and cannot perish before nature itself is extinguished.

Ceremonies must be judged differently. They were accommodated to one nation alone for certain uses and a certain time.

At their appointed time the shadows of ceremonies were driven away by Christ’s rising like the sun. Yet the worship of God prescribed by the Decalogue remained, plainly spiritual worship, such as God always required. Indeed, even in the time when ceremonies flourished, he often testified through the prophets that he abominated them, because in that external worship the people did not bring what God chiefly required and what the ceremonies themselves foreshadowed: that is, a contrite heart, faith, hope, and love, in which true worship of God consists.

But, again, the Judicial Law, about which we now inquire, must be treated differently from the Decalogue or the ceremonies.

Ignorance or concealment of this distinction gave these men occasion to reject the Judicial Law in the same manner as the Ceremonial. We shall readily show, I hope, that in this they sin most gravely.

I therefore say that the Judicial Law differs from the Decalogue because the Decalogue comprehends in few words all justice and equity in every kind of duty, while the Judicial Law individually explains only that part of justice and equity which concerns those matters for which judgments have been established.

Moreover, since the Decalogue is the most complete description of natural law and is expressly confirmed in the Gospel, it binds everyone without exception in perpetuity. But the judicial laws were accommodated to the circumstances of one commonwealth and, since they were never written for us, cannot even be said to have been abrogated.

Finally, the Decalogue imposes laws upon conscience; but the judicial laws, although the magistrate must be obeyed not only because of wrath but also because of conscience, regard conscience not simply but in a certain respect. *[Marginal reference: Romans 13:5.]*

Again, the Judicial differs from the Ceremonial first because the latter delivers the external worship of religion, while the former concerns partly the external preservation of divine worship, as the laws we described a little earlier show, and partly the civil affairs of this life.

Second—and I fear this has not been sufficiently weighed by some—the ceremonies properly belonged not to one nation, for other nations could and indeed should have joined themselves to the Israelites, but to one time. In contrast, the judicial laws belonged to one nation, but for the greatest part not to one time.

This can readily be recognized as follows, unless I am mistaken. Whoever embraced true religion, even if he was not a Jew and did not live in Judea, was nevertheless bound to Moses’s ceremonies, such as circumcision, observing the Sabbaths, selecting foods, and other things of that kind, so long as the church was under the Law’s tutelage.

But when that time elapsed, both Jews and the other peoples of whom the church consists were freed through Christ from the yoke of those ceremonies, to such a degree that, contrary to the papists’ opinion, human beings may neither recall them nor substitute others in their place.

For if any ceremonies are established by the church’s consent for the sake of order, they must be distinguished from those which bound conscience and in which for a time God’s worship consisted.

In contrast, only Israelites—that is, those who lived in Judea—were bound by the judicial laws, because they were accommodated to this one commonwealth. If that commonwealth still existed today, apart from certain laws which pertained to punishing violations of ceremonies—which we have said are now abolished—it would still use the same laws, since the Gospel otherwise changed or detracted nothing from them.

“What, then,” someone will say, “since we are treating here the mode of punishment by which heretics are to be punished by the civil magistrate, why do you propose Moses’s political laws to us?”

I answer that, although we are not bound by the formulas of the Mosaic polity, nevertheless, because those judicial laws prescribe the equity of judgments, which is a part of the Decalogue, although we are not bound by them insofar as they were prescribed by Moses to one people, we are nevertheless bound to observe them insofar as they comprehend that general equity which must prevail everywhere.

In this respect, they seem to have been established not by Moses for the Israelite people alone, but by nature for the universal human race.

To make this more easily understood, we shall offer an example. The Lord commands that a deposit be returned and a thief punished. Both are political laws and so far bind the Israelites alone.

Nevertheless, because each follows natural equity and explains that perpetual precept of the Decalogue, “YOU SHALL NOT STEAL,” everyone is so far bound to perform them both.

A thief is sometimes condemned to restore the theft, sometimes double, sometimes fourfold or even fivefold, or is delivered over for the damage. This penalty is merely political and binds the one Israelite nation, to which alone it was accommodated.

Therefore, for certain good reasons the magistrate may enact a sharper penalty against thieves in his own jurisdiction. For that law of Moses, insofar as it prescribes the mode of punishment, was never established for other nations and is not now properly abrogated.

It must be known, however, that although those particular formulas of the laws never pertained to us, nevertheless, because their author is God himself, the most just

[PDF page 72 | logical scan 71 | printed page 155]

and equitable Legislator, magistrates act most rightly when, whenever no particular circumstance of time, place, or persons prevents it, they regard the most perfect exemplar of the Mosaic laws in making their own laws.

Thus Moses’s yoke is not imposed upon us, yet judgments are exercised with the highest moderation.

Certainly, if anyone compares some political laws of the Greeks and most of the Romans with the Mosaic laws, he will find such a great likeness among them even in establishing punishments that it sufficiently appears to everyone that they were accommodated to the same end of natural equity.

Therefore, finally to relate all these things to our purpose, when we propose to Christian princes those political laws of Moses prescribed against despisers of true religion, we do not do so in order that Moses’s polity be thrust upon Christians, but that, insofar as possible, they should follow its equity as the best exemplar when establishing judgments concerning the same religion.

There are therefore certain peculiarities in these laws which do not pertain to us. First, because they were prescribed to Moses, not our magistrate, and to the Israelites, not us.

Second, because they expressly concern bodily sacrifices, the Sabbath, and other ceremonies which Christ abolished when he came.

Third, because they were made against prophets and interpreters of dreams, which gifts do not now exist in God’s church.

Finally, some particular regard for this nation seems to have been employed in establishing the very kind of punishment.

But when these particulars are removed, I gather two things from these laws which I certainly do not think can be refuted by suitable reasons.

First, since defection from the religion established by God’s Word, and solicitation to defection which Paul calls “different teaching” (*ἑτεροδιδασκαλίαν*), are punished in these laws by God through the civil magistrate, I confirm from this Law that the same crimes must now too be punished by the same magistrate within his jurisdiction.

For so far this Law regards the Decalogue and rests upon that perpetual and universal equity of nature.

Therefore, whoever calls cruel those who conduct these judgments; whoever removes the magistrate as profane from these judgments; whoever so confidently asserts that no class of people is less to be feared by the commonwealth than heretics—I dare say that they sin against God and the testimony of their conscience, unless perhaps they are either wholly stupefied or among those whose conscience has been, as it were, cut away by a cautery.

Second, since no suitable reason can be adduced why God’s majesty and the church’s authority should possess less weight among us than formerly among the Jews—indeed, Christians, if they despise true religion or protect it with less zeal than the Jews formerly did, can be excused so much less as the Lord revealed himself more clearly through his Son than through the prophets—I do not hesitate to affirm that only those princes perform their duty who, if not in the very kind of punishment, at least in establishing its mode, propose those laws of God to themselves for imitation. By God’s example they too should enact some capital penalty against factious apostates according to the atrocity of the blasphemy and crime.

For God’s majesty should possess so great a value among everyone throughout the everlasting ages that whoever holds it in derision, because he holds the very author of life in derision, is most worthy to have life taken away by force.

These things I say; these things I proclaim loudly, relying upon God’s truth and the testimony of conscience. Let these men on the contrary loudly call us, as much as they please, savage, cruel, monstrous, and bloodthirsty, until they are hoarse.

Truth will nevertheless conquer and show that only those are worthy of these names who, through perverse or pretended zeal for clemency, allow wolves to be fattened upon the life of the sheep rather than perform their duty in vindicating God’s majesty.

And these things could perhaps have sufficed. Yet lest anything seem to have been passed over by me, I have determined to refute the remaining objections individually.

Thus Montfort answers first of all: “If they understand the bodily sacrifice designated in the law of Exodus, they must confess that no one can be punished from this Law. For no one sacrifices bodily to a foreign god, not even the Turks—not to Jupiter, Mercury, the Sun or Moon, but to the God of Moses.

“But if they understand spiritual sacrifice, as Peter and Paul speak of a rational sacrifice, I desire two things: first to be killed must be those who sacrifice to their god Maonim, that is, violence and cruelty, and the greedy and gluttonous, whose idolatry is the true idolatry of the New Testament, of which that described in the Old was the shadow and figure. From this it follows that the punishment in the Old was also a figure not of temporal but eternal punishment.”

Thus he writes in nearly so many words.

Although I seem long ago to have cut off every handle for all these calumnies when I showed with what great ungodliness these men confuse the true God of Moses with the idols of Turks and Jews, I shall nevertheless answer again briefly so that this may become more and more evident.

Certainly none of us is so ignorant as to recall those ancient sacrifices into use. But in this Montfort betrays his manifest stupidity: clinging to the word “sacrificing” itself, he does not consider that the rationale for establishing this law extends more widely than that it can or should be restricted to this one kind.

For it com-

[PDF page 73 | logical scan 72 | printed page 156]

prehends rather public contempt of God’s worship, and that whole worship is understood by the word “sacrifice.” Thus Elijah, when signifying that Israel had violated God’s whole covenant, says, “They have overthrown your altars and killed your prophets”; and Isaiah, when predicting that Egypt would embrace true religion, says, “Egypt will swear by the name of the living God.” *[Marginal references: 1 Kings 19; Isaiah 19.]*

Nor does the jurists’ rule have force here which teaches that laws are not to be extended in what they call odious matters. There is no room for conjecture where we have an express law.

These men should have compared this law with Deuteronomy 13, so that they might possess it as a sort of commentary. From that passage they would have recognized that capital punishment is inflicted upon those who, after they themselves defect from the Lord, solicit others to defect.

There are various public testimonies of this defection. Although the laws mention only the principal ones and those which ordinarily occur for the most part, the rationale of the law itself was nevertheless framed in a universal formula. Thus, in general, it must have force against those who defect from true religion and solicit others to defect, whom we now call by the general name “heretics,” not false prophets, interpreters of dreams, or sacrificers to foreign gods, because those external ceremonies and those gifts of prophecy have ceased.

But although these have ceased, neither defection nor its penalty has ceased.

Elsewhere we have proved, sufficiently I hope, how absurd is their inference that, if spiritual idolatry is signified here, cruel, greedy, and gluttonous people must also be punished by the civil magistrate, so that it needs no longer refutation.

There remains that marvelous allegory of Old Testament idolatry and the penalty established against it, concerning which I thought I should argue somewhat more copiously.

Therefore, if these men are believed, the idolatry of the Old Testament, that is, bodily sacrifice, was a figure of the idolatry of the New Testament, that is, spiritual sacrifice. Consequently, its penalty, namely stoning, was not a figure of some temporal penalty which we should now employ, but of a spiritual penalty—that is, the eternal penalty to which we must plainly leave heretics—or of the penalty inflicted by a sword which is not bodily but spiritual and double-edged, that is, by the living Word of God.

Montfort repeats this invention in many places, but particularly when he employs this objection against the law written against those who violated the Sabbath. There he writes: “A transfer has been made from bodily sin to spiritual sin, from which it follows that the penalty too must be spiritual, not bodily.”

Likewise, where he answers the argument taken from the Levites, through whom Moses punished idolaters capitally, he says: “This has already been sufficiently answered. A bodily penalty followed the bodily calf; a spiritual penalty follows the spiritual.”

But how can I doubt that in these things I recognize that fanatical spirit which once through Origen so wretchedly troubled God’s church?

For this was formerly a conspicuous cunning of Satan, which nevertheless none of the ancients, let me say it with their goodwill, sufficiently noticed: since he could not cast Scripture wholly out of the church, he would nevertheless render the whole of it useless and mythological through empty allegories, so that there is not even one letter in the Scriptures which was not contaminated by such allegories. The Libertines and Anabaptists still enter the same path today.

But I would like these men to show us from what passage of Scripture they finally learned this invention concerning the shadows and figures of the judicial laws.

I acknowledge these things in the ceremonies and indeed in certain histories too, on Scripture’s authority. But I do not remember ever reading anything concerning the judicial laws or bodily idolatry foreshadowing spiritual idolatry. *[Marginal reference: Epistle to the Hebrews.]*

I indeed confess that the punishment exacted from some criminal declares the disposition of the judge toward that crime, and that external idolatry is a testimony of inward idolatry.

But even if I concede this, what has it to do with our subject? Further, I do not see why they attribute bodily idolatry to the Old Testament and spiritual idolatry to the New, since both flourished formerly and flourish even more now than we would wish.

To be silent about the papists’ images and that edible god, I say that as many external or inward kinds of worshiping God as ever existed or will exist, so many idols were fabricated by human beings and so many kinds of both forms of idolatry. Jerome presses this on almost every page in his commentaries on the prophets.

As for what they say concerning bodily punishment foreshadowing spiritual punishment, it should at least certainly have been said equivocally.

For if they call the penalty inflicted upon the body “bodily,” then the one by which the spirit is afflicted must be called “spiritual.”

But punishment is not ordinarily inflicted upon the spirit by human beings, but by him alone who can destroy both soul and body in Gehenna. For that power of binding which he conceded to the church is not so much a judgment as the declaration of that judgment by which whoever is bound by the church’s just judgment had already bound himself. And since it is employed for the destruction of the flesh so that the spirit may be saved on the Lord’s day, I do not see how it can properly either be called a spiritual penalty or be opposed to a bod-

[PDF page 74 | logical scan 73 | printed page 157]

ily penalty.

But perhaps there is another rationale for this obscure allegory, namely, if you say that a spiritual penalty is employed when by the spiritual sword—that is, preaching the Word—we slay our enemies, that is, our sins, just as the Apostle admonishes us to consecrate ourselves to our God as rational worship. *[Marginal references: Romans 12:1; Ephesians 6:17.]*

But even if I concede that God’s Word is called the Spirit’s sword and that the Apostle commands us to mortify our earthly members, where did they read that the bodily punishments inflicted upon criminals’ bodies under the Old Testament foreshadowed that spiritual sacrifice by which we mortify our flesh either in ourselves or in others? And that they foreshadowed it so that what is said concerning ceremonies must also be said concerning civil laws: that they vanished when the light came, that is, when Christ’s Gospel arose?

As though under the Old Testament that mortification were only foreshadowed and not rather actually employed always by every believer.

But if they nevertheless needed some figure, why do these men take it away from us? For they so evade those ancient laws by fictitious allegories as therefore to contend that heresy must be punished differently among Christians than formerly among the Jews.

Further, if that penalty of false prophets was merely temporary, why is the law said to have been delivered for an end which must remain in force perpetually? For thus it is written at the law’s end: “THAT ALL THE ISRAELITES MAY HEAR AND FEAR, AND HENCEFORTH NO LONGER DEVISE SO ABOMINABLE A THING.”
