What do you say to this, Montfort? For it is established that God’s people still need these examples. Is it not sacrilege, then, to remove these things established by God?

Therefore, in whatever fashion they employ these fictitious allegories, they cannot but seem most false and empty, even if they were confirmed by the authority of all the ancients.

I remember Augustine touching somewhere in one word upon that allegory of the visible and invisible sword. But it is also established that, with the goodwill of so great a man let this be said, he indulged his age somewhat too much in pursuing allegories. *[Marginal reference: Augustine, On Faith and Works.]*

I therefore affirm again that this allegorical invention is empty and futile.

But come, let us proceed to other refutations which they oppose to Deuteronomy 13.

Montfort denies that this law has force because there is now no false prophet according to Moses’s definition, that is, one who predicts something future and teaches the worship of foreign gods.

I answer first of all, as I did a little earlier, that the legislator’s intention must be understood from the reason for delivering the law, that is, from these words: “BECAUSE HE SPOKE DEFECTION FROM THE LORD YOUR GOD.”

Next, I say that what he denies is true: those who wrongly interpret the sacred writings lead human beings away from the true worship of God and thus exhort them to worship other gods.

For every doctrine which speaks concerning God’s worship must, if it is not from God, proceed from the devil. Therefore, whoever receives it receives the devil, and whoever advises that it be received leads away from God.

Paul even calls the prohibition of foods and marriage doctrines of demons; we need seek no better authority. *[Marginal references: 1 Timothy 4:1; 1 Corinthians 10:20.]*

What they adduce concerning those who build hay or stubble does not pertain to the matter. That passage treats those who build only empty things, yet build them upon the foundation. This cannot properly be said of heretics, who indeed posit Christ in word but remove him in reality.

Such are those who proclaim Christ to be God’s Son and the world’s lord and judge, and display that white garment of theirs, but meanwhile deny that knowledge of Christ’s state and office, the Trinity, justification, free will, and other things of that kind pertains to religion; and, by publishing books after violating the churches’ agreement, solicit others to defect.

Against them, then, and those like them who tear down the church’s foundations either by manifest wickedness or willful ignorance, this passage of Isaiah rather applies: “Their strength will be like stubble, and the maker like a spark, and both will burn.” *[Marginal reference: Isaiah 1:31.]*

The other passage of Paul which they adduce to teach that those who wrongly interpret the sacred writings nevertheless lead no one away from God and are not comprehended by that law of Deuteronomy betrays their ignorance even more.

“Paul testifies,” they say, “that Christ died even for him who thinks it unlawful to eat. For God pardons ignorance.”

Who among us denies that God pardons ignorance whenever it pleases him? As though we treated persons here, rather than the matters themselves.

Further, what ignorance is this, not to distinguish indifferent matters from those which are impious in themselves, and the weak from heretics?

The whole course of the disputation shows clearly that there Paul argues concerning indifferent things and those who are weak in faith.

For otherwise, when the question concerns things which cannot be committed without violating God’s Law, he delivers a wholly different rule, commanding us to test all things and hold what is good, and indeed to abstain from every appearance of evil. *[Marginal reference: 1 Thessalonians 5:21–22.]*

Again, just as Paul testifies that he became all things to everyone, plainly speaking of the weak, so it also appears how little he indulged the obstinate and stubborn.

[PDF page 75 | logical scan 74 | printed page 158]

For this reason he refused to circumcise Titus and admonished certain people to beware of the dogs and the mutilation. *[Marginal references: Galatians 2; Philippians 3:2.]*

Since these men mix all these things together, I do not marvel that they gather such absurdities from this passage as to apply what was said about indifferent matters to the blasphemous and ungodly doctrine of heretics, whom it is plainly unlawful to judge, much less punish; and to twist to heretics what concerns the weak—not those teaching a different doctrine, but those still clinging to the elements of the Mosaic Law.

There remains Montfort’s double objection against the law written against blasphemers, for we refuted the others elsewhere.

“If this law had force,” he says, “the whole world would have to be removed, for it blasphemes God. In words it confesses him; in deeds and heart it denies him. Nor is this hidden but most manifest.

“Next, as was demonstrated above, it is not yet perfectly known who are truly heretics.”

But let Montfort hear what I answer to these things.

Whoever blasphemes God through wickedness must be severely restrained by the magistrate. I confess that most are negligent here, but what their duty is neither can nor should be inferred from their negligence.

I acknowledge that blasphemies are much more frequent among us than is fitting, and I detest with tears this perversity of the world. I nevertheless deny that the whole world is manifestly guilty of this crime, as he wishes. If he can, let him show these things to be false.

As for what he says he has demonstrated—“It is not yet perfectly known who are truly heretics”—although I long and extensively sought this demonstration throughout that whole medley, I found it nowhere. Rather, I found many blasphemies most shamelessly and criminally poured out against God’s Word in place of it: as though it had plainly been written so obscurely and enigmatically that a new sentence of the judge must be awaited, and meanwhile, while the lawsuit between Christ and the devil remains pending, possession must be granted according to liberty.

These blasphemies, I say, and very many others I confess I found in that writing. I certainly do not see how they can be called “obscure proofs” (*ἀφανεῖς ἔλεγχοι*), unless perhaps because they demonstrate manifest ungodliness.

Third argument: From examples of both Testaments and of the ancient church.

In the third place we shall set examples taken from both Testaments and from the storehouse of ecclesiastical history.

We shall first speak concerning the examples of the Old Testament, not all, but the principal ones. They are as follows.

Moses, the meekest of men, of whom very few punishments in civil affairs are read, produced the severest example in the crime of violated religion, when at his command three thousand people were killed by the Levites because of the idolatry of the calf, without any distinction of age, sex, blood, or relationship. *[Marginal reference: Exodus 32.]*

Asa, that most holy prince, restored the Lord’s covenant with the people, adding this formula: “WHOEVER DOES NOT SEEK THE LORD GOD OF ISRAEL SHALL BE KILLED, WHETHER SMALL OR GREAT, MAN OR WOMAN.” *[Marginal reference: 2 Chronicles 15.]*

Jehu, chosen by the Lord, wholly cut off Ahab’s entire family because of idolatry and slaughtered the priests of Baal to the last man. *[Marginal reference: 2 Kings 10.]*

That godly king Josiah slaughtered the priests of the high places upon the altars and burned human bones upon them. *[Marginal reference: 2 Kings 23.]*

Elijah, when the magistrate was inactive, exercising extraordinary power, killed Baal’s priests at the brook Kishon. *[Marginal reference: 1 Kings 18.]*

Jehoiada, raised up by God, killed the ungodly Athaliah, extraordinarily discharging the right of the magistrate. *[Marginal reference: 2 Kings 11.]*

But before I come to examples of the New Testament, it is worthwhile to recognize what can be gathered from these histories, so that, if possible, we may take away from our adversaries every opportunity for caviling.

I choose to reason as follows. What was done by these most eminent heroes from a prince’s authority and command and is praised by the express testimony of the Holy Spirit is of such a kind that it cannot be blamed without blasphemy.

It remains, then, that our adversaries either concede to us that Christian magistrates may imitate their deed, or at least prove one of these two things: either that the condition of heretics differs from those treated in these examples, or that through the Gospel something was detracted from civil power as regards the affairs of religion.

Montfort certainly saw this when he answered these examples. Thus he labors to prove both, but uselessly, in the manner of those who speak for falsehood against truth.

He therefore says that these examples apply to idolaters and the priests of Baal because they professed a foreign God, but by no means to heretics, since no heretic professes a foreign God.

Thus he speaks, following the footsteps of Bellius, who does not know how to distinguish God from an idol. We have spoken abundantly concerning this exceedingly crude and pernicious error.

Next

[PDF page 76 | logical scan 75 | printed page 159]

these men measure God and his worship by each person’s opinion. But I affirm that, as soon as anyone contemplates God outside the Word, an idol is conceived in the mind; and that whoever adds anything to God’s worship is truly an idolater.

I also assert that whoever in perverse order does not subject himself to God’s Word but attempts, as is peculiar to heretics, to subject God’s Word to himself, is also guilty of the crime of idolatry. Such people are worse than unbelievers themselves because they use the name of piety and truth as a pretext for their lies.

But I think it is by no means necessary to repeat these things at greater length. It suffices for us in this place to have shown that whatever was repeatedly done by godly and holy kings against that cruder and external idolatry applies to heresy as its parent and mother.

There therefore remains only the adversaries’ other refuge: namely, to teach that through Gospel doctrine something was detracted from civil power; for they concede that the care of vindicating religion formerly pertained to princes, but that this faculty has now been taken from them.

Montfort proves this by this one reason: under Moses the bodily sword foreshadowed the spiritual sword to be employed under the Gospel, which spiritual sword was entrusted not to the civil magistrate but to the church.

As though under Moses the Lord did not also brandish the spiritual sword. These men assume what no sane person will concede to them: that the Judicial Law was no less full of figures and shadows than the Ceremonial, and that it vanished at Christ’s advent as at the coming of light.

For this reason they also loudly declare that, when we cite those laws and histories of the Old Covenant, we recall again the Law’s bondage. They twist against us whatever the Apostle rightly wrote against those who overthrew the Gospel’s grace through confidence in ceremonies or other works.

But we explained at greater length a little earlier how false and empty these calumnies are.

It remains, then, that our conclusion taken from the example of the ancient kings has force, since like things are compared with like.

I nevertheless confess that the deeds of Elijah and Jehoiada seem to need a certain particular consideration. But if we weigh the whole matter more accurately, we shall discover that all these examples have the same rationale.

For in this place Elijah must not be considered as a prophet or Jehoiada as a priest, but each rather as exercising the royal power attributed by the Lord extraordinarily for accomplishing these singular deeds.

Therefore, no one should cavil against us that, by the same reasoning by which we drew an argument from the example of kings to our princes, the same argument can also be applied from the deeds of Elijah and Jehoiada to those who succeeded the prophets and priests.

For if we did this, we would confuse the different and distinct natures of the two jurisdictions, which never came into our mind.

But it is also worthwhile to recognize certain objections of Montfort: one against Moses’s deed in punishing the idolatry of the golden calf, and two against Elijah’s heroic deed in killing Baal’s priests.

The adversary therefore asks why we do not imitate at least those things in Moses which are better and approach more closely to Christ’s clemency. Such was the deed of Moses himself when he appeased God, angry with the Israelites, although they wished to stone Moses himself, and preferred to be blotted from the book of life rather than that they should perish.

“But now,” he says, “to select from his law all the harshest things and seek blood by every art—this is most foreign to Christ’s kindness.”

Thus Montfort opposes Moses, whom Scripture praises to us so greatly for meekness, to the most patient and merciful Christ as savage and precipitate in this particular deed. When he does this, he certainly reprehends the Holy Spirit.

For the conclusion of the history about which we inquire is prescribed in these words: “Thus the Lord struck the people because they had made the calf.” *[Marginal reference: Exodus 32:35.]*

From these words we understand that whatever Moses did here he did by the breath and impulse of God’s Spirit.

Thus, when the same man exhorted the Levites to that bloody slaughter, as Montfort calls it, but in fact to execute a most just and—if the magnitude of the sin is diligently considered—most moderate command, he said: “Consecrate your hands today to the Lord, each even against his son and his brother, that a favorable blessing may be given to you today.” *[Marginal reference: Exodus 32:29.]*

But that eloquent interpreter of the Bible has most audaciously corrupted this passage too. He interprets those words of Moses as though the Levites, after perpetrating the killing, were commanded to expiate their hands contaminated with their brothers’ blood, whereas, even on the authority of the most learned Aben Ezra, they should rather be translated by the pluperfect, so that the property of the imperative mood is preserved.

Nor do I think an example can be adduced in which the word “FILL,” which Moses uses there, is employed for “EXPIATE.”

Certainly, so far was this deed of the Levites from needing expiation that Moses, on the contrary, dignifies it by no other name than that of a most pleasing sacrifice. Indeed—as a most learned interpreter noted upon that passage—because of this one deed Jacob’s curse upon Levi was thus turned into blessing, as appears from Moses’s blessing. *[Marginal reference: Deuteronomy 33.]*

[PDF page 77 | logical scan 76 | printed page 160]

Whom, then, shall we believe? This man who calls the deed most harsh and wishes a better one to be selected by us, or rather the Holy Spirit, who calls this killing a sacrifice, blesses the Levites because of it, and finally proclaims God himself the author of the deed?

“But Christ,” he says, “did not do this.” On the contrary, could what was done by the Father’s authority have displeased the Son, who daily and justly inflicts similar penalties upon ungrateful human beings?

I nevertheless confess that when he lived here with us on earth, nothing of this kind was done by him. Yet I deny that he abstained from this example as though it were either ungodly or cruel—for it is neither ungodliness nor cruelty for the magistrate to kill the most guilty people—but because this was not properly the office for which he was destined by the Father, nor had the time appointed by God for those judgments arrived.

For Christ first had to be heard before contempt of him was vindicated.

Let Montfort now go and select for us some better and more excellent deed in Moses’s life for the civil magistrate to imitate.

What he mentions about Moses asking the Lord for the Israelites’ salvation neither pertains to the matter at issue nor argues against us. For Moses was also the people’s intercessor before God.

Furthermore, Christian love commands that we be most solicitous for one another’s salvation. So far are we from judging that this conflicts with the princes’ office that, on the contrary, we judge that the whole commonwealth must be commended by them to the Lord as by certain common fathers of everyone, and especially those very people for vindicating whose crimes they received the sword from God.

For a prince must bring to judgment not some depraved desire, but the fear of God and love of the commonwealth.

Moses supplies us a distinguished example of this, embracing this people so that he does not wink at its crimes, and again punishing it so that he is prepared even at the loss of his own salvation to reconcile them to the Lord.

Montfort, therefore, is blasphemous because he partly obscurely accuses and partly praises so sparingly a deed which the Holy Spirit so greatly commends as even to dignify it with the highest reward; inept because he recalls us from the magistrate’s office, which we treat, to the ecclesiastical ministry; and unlearned because he thinks love conflicts with moderate severity.

Let us now see what he thinks concerning that sacrifice of Elijah.

“You act maliciously,” he says, speaking of us, “by not also mentioning Elijah’s deed when he called down fire from heaven to consume the soldiers. They certainly would not have omitted it unless they saw that the apostles were reprehended by Christ when they wished to imitate that deed of Elijah.”

Fair words, Montfort! That example of Elijah which you mention was not passed over maliciously by us, as you think, but because, unlike you, we do not wish rashly to babble anything whatever.

For this example pertains to tyrants and tyrants’ ministers, whom the Lord will not allow to depart unpunished.

Although we could in some degree have applied it to heretics, who themselves plunder God’s church, nevertheless, because, as you yourself confess somewhere, in this deed somewhat more was lawful for Elijah than for a magistrate, we did not wish to use it, lest you be able to plead that something was said by us insufficiently suitably or sought too anxiously.

But when the apostles later wished to imitate this example and were reprehended by Christ, this by no means occurs as though Elijah were accused of cruelty and Christ wished his disciples to be foreign to it, but because their misguided zeal certainly deserved the greatest reprehension. This was neither their office, unless perhaps they were sent extraordinarily to accomplish it by God’s special command, nor was the deed of the Samaritans similar to that which Elijah avenged.

For the ungodly apostate Ahaziah wished to kill Elijah under the pretext of piety; here the Samaritans deny hospitality to Christ as he passes through. There the prophet’s business was with apostates; here with those who had never known the Gospel. There Elijah by God’s command calls fire down from heaven to assert God’s glory; here the apostles are stimulated by a human passion to avenge an injury.

Account must also be taken of the time. Elijah was sent to assert God’s worship and avenge Israel’s treachery. Christ and the apostles were sent to proclaim the doctrine of the Gospel, which appeared new and hard to most who were accustomed to the Law’s ceremonies.

But it is one thing to promulgate the Gospel, and another to assert the authority of the promulgated Gospel against its treacherous despisers.

Christ therefore deservedly recalls the disciples to the consideration of their office, yet does not reprehend Elijah as cruel, as you think.

Your other objection, however, is this:

“Elijah obtained fire from heaven to burn the sacrifice even after it had been sprinkled three times with water, which the Baalites could not do. Having obtained this victory, he therefore triumphed over them, that is, killed them. But these men wish to triumph without victory. For what fire have they ever obtained from

[PDF page 78 | logical scan 77 | printed page 161]

heaven? The sacrifice acceptable to God is a contrite spirit; the living offering is a people serving God in spirit and truth. If they possess such a people, and have called down from heaven the fire of love and the Spirit to kindle this sacrifice, there is certainly reason for them deservedly to triumph over Baal’s priests and slay them with the sword not bodily but spiritual and double-edged, that is, with God’s living Word.

“But if they possess a rebellious and contumacious people who spend whole nights in revelry, as they themselves are accustomed loudly to testify from the pulpit, and although they have cried until evening have still been unable to obtain any fire of love without everything growing worse, to such a degree that the pastors themselves are often driven into exile by their sheep, let them cease wishing to imitate Elijah’s triumph when they cannot imitate his victory.”

These are your words, Montfort, clinging to your allegories. I have copied this whole passage from your refutation so that their supreme facility and levity might be better understood.

But we, as the matter itself shows, seek neither our own triumphs nor the things that are ours, but God’s glory, and we invoke God as witness of this matter.

But what at last must be thought of you, who are not ashamed to burden God’s faithful servants with so many curses? They certainly daily lament with very many tears the supreme contumacy of human beings which you mockingly reproach them with.

Yet what can you finally object here which has not happened to all faithful servants of God from the beginning of the world? To be silent about the most ancient patriarchs, how much did the prophets advance by their cries? What did Christ himself and the disciples who followed his footsteps receive besides lashes, exiles, and crosses?

For the world loves only what is its own. Therefore, you should not object against the ministers of our churches—most learned and diligent men—that their labor and diligence produce small fruits.

This should rather be attributed to those who strain every sinew to overthrow what God’s faithful servants constructed by the greatest labors.

Yet I hope that this obstinacy will neither retard their course nor break the steadfastness of excellent and most courageous princes, so that the former labor more vigorously than ever to disclose evil counsels and the latter to hinder them.

There remain the edicts of Nebuchadnezzar, Darius, and Artaxerxes, whose formulas the Holy Spirit wished to remain extant, so that Christian princes might be ashamed to yield first place to profane kings in vindicating religion.

They are as follows. “This decree was made by me,” Nebuchadnezzar says, “that whatever people, nation, or language speaks blasphemy against the God of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego shall be cut into pieces and his house reduced to a dunghill, because there is no other God who can save in this way.” *[Marginal reference: Daniel 3.]*

Darius says: “Such a decree was established by me that, if any mortal alters this edict”—namely, concerning restoring the temple—“a beam shall be taken from his house, upon which he shall be raised and hanged, and his house for this reason reduced to a dunghill.” *[Marginal reference: Ezra 6.]*

Then Artaxerxes, addressing Ezra, says: “Whoever refuses to perform the Law of your God and the king’s law, let judgment be made concerning him immediately, whether to death, eradication, forfeiture of property, or imprisonment.” *[Marginal reference: Ezra 7.]*

You cannot deny, Montfort, that these edicts are so written that by the Holy Spirit’s voice they can appear to anyone magnificently praised. But here that acute allegory of the Judicial Law which you customarily oppose like a shield deserts you.

I have not yet been able to divine what objection you will finally adduce.

Meanwhile, while you prepare yourself, it will not weary us to exhort every Christian magistrate with the greatest effort we can, not to cruelty—far be that from us—but prudently and moderately, yet courageously and severely, to preserve true religion by all those protections which they received from God.

Examples of the New Testament.

Come now, let us proceed to the examples of the New Testament, which are altogether two, in Acts chapters 5 and 13, and which must be applied to our proposed question not without judgment.

Namely, Ananias and Sapphira killed by Peter’s word, and Elymas the magician blinded by Paul’s word.

As regards Peter’s deed, I indeed confess that they were killed by Peter not because of heresy, but because of hypocrisy, sacrilege, and a shameless lie.

I also confess that what Peter did here can be drawn as an example neither by ministers of the Word and those placed over ecclesiastical discipline—for Peter possessed an extraordinary power—nor even by the civil magistrate himself, insofar as Peter killed by a word alone, which the magistrate cannot perform even if he wishes;

[PDF page 79 | logical scan 78 | printed page 162]

and punished a crime otherwise hidden from human beings, whereas the magistrate can judge only manifest things by which it is established that civil society was injured.

This example therefore pertains to our purpose only so far as we gather from it that it ought not to seem strange for the sword to be drawn by God’s ordinary minister against convicted and factious despisers of religion, when Peter punished contempt of religion, though still hidden and latent, with capital punishment.

That this argument is firm is clear from the very comparison of persons and crimes.

In both cases the subject is despisers of God’s Word who nevertheless wish to seem most holy. The crimes are generically the same: contempt of religion and the church, sacrilege, hypocrisy, and supreme shamelessness in lying.

They differ only in that there the sin is in one deed, here in doctrine; there the crime is hidden, but here manifest.

From this it follows that the crime of heresy must be punished so much more bitterly as it is more dangerous to err in doctrine than to sin in performing some one deed, and as a manifest crime in which you yourself glory is more pernicious than a hidden vice which you so conceal as to contain it wholly within.

Luke openly declares, unless I am mistaken, that Elymas was blinded by Paul because of his perversion of true religion.

For he writes that Elymas labored to turn the proconsul Sergius away from the faith, and that Paul rebuked him with these words: “O full of every deceit and every wickedness, son of the devil, enemy of all righteousness, will you not cease perverting the straight ways of the Lord?”

It is certainly the purpose of heretics, through deceit and wickedness, to turn others from the faith and pervert the Lord’s ways, that is, the doctrine expressed in his Word.

Furthermore, although this example does not contain a capital penalty, it nevertheless shows what the Academics so stubbornly deny: that there is room even for bodily punishment in the cause of religion.

As we have often repeated, we by no means advise that the severest remedies be approached immediately, but only when the gravity of the crime demands it or the salvation of others cannot be more suitably provided for than by cutting away a putrid member with iron.

Moreover, if anyone objects that these examples are few, let him consider that every event which occurred at that time was not written down. It is probable that the gift of powers was not conceded to the church in vain, but was frequently employed in its times and circumstances.

Next, since, as I said before, neither God’s majesty is now diminished nor anything detracted from civil power, it is clear that the examples of both Testaments must be joined.

Finally, the circumstances of those times were peculiar. It seemed good to the Lord not only to send the apostles unarmed and destitute of every earthly power, but also to have the whole world hostile, so that it might be clear to everyone that the Gospel’s victory was entirely heavenly.

And certainly, as someone rightly wrote, Christ arms ministers of the Gospel generally more with patience than with the protection of any sword.

But although this is so, whenever the Lord fortifies the church even with the aid of civil power, that aid neither can nor should be despised without conspicuous injury to him.

From all these things what we undertook to prove seems to follow: namely, that this is perhaps the chief duty of the civil magistrate, to punish in heretics contempt and disturbance of true religion, sometimes with bodily and indeed even capital punishment.

But it is time for us to hear Montfort’s objections.

First, he asks why we do not rather adduce passages from the New Testament: “I send you like sheep amidst wolves”; or “Blessed are you when human beings persecute you”; or “The world will rejoice and you will weep.”

What then? Do we deny that these are true? Do we not rather experience daily that it is so? Do these conflict with the doctrine we deliver? Do we attribute civil power to ecclesiastical ministers?

When a minister of the Word demands from God’s minister the aid of civil power against the invincible rage of heretics, does he cease to be a sheep amidst wolves?

Does the magistrate put on the wolf’s nature when he draws the sword within his jurisdiction against obstinate and factious despisers of religion?

Because the magistrate, whenever it is expedient, leads heretics to just punishment, does the world therefore cease exulting and God’s afflicted church cease weeping?

But come, let us approach his remaining defenses.

“Peter,” he says, “killed Ananias and Sapphira because of hypocrisy and falsehood. But these men, in contrast, kill only those who refuse to lie.”

Could Montfort really have forgotten himself so far as to say that truth is punished by capital punishment by those through whom Truth itself loudly declares that it has been asserted in our times?

Yet he certainly adduces a suitable reason why he says this. For he writes of us: “If any agree with their religion in words and external deed, they are not killed,

[PDF page 80 | logical scan 79 | printed page 163]

even if they are alien in mind. From this it appears that falsehood has a greater place among them than truth, since if anyone says what he thinks, he is killed.”

Although these things are foreign to our purpose—for I am not asking here what is ordinarily done, but what ought to be done—I shall nevertheless refute them because of their singular and unheard-of malice.

For what would a man not dare to say who does not hesitate to write these things?

Let him show, then, whom we have finally endangered because he said what he thought.

Our princes punished some Anabaptists not because they said what they thought, nor so that they would be compelled by force to embrace true religion, but because they broke the bond of all human society, were treacherous apostates, most manifestly corrupted God’s Word, held the church’s authority in derision, and factiously tore apart the citizens’ agreement.

Servetus too, that most hideous monster, paid the penalties not because he said what he thought, but because he thought, spoke, and wrote so impiously concerning the entire Trinity, Christ’s human nature, infant baptism, faith, righteousness, and finally the whole of religion, and for thirty full years held God’s Word, the church, and the magistrate in derision so shamefully and criminally that this sun never beheld anything more impure and detestable.

As he invidiously and maliciously calls it “our religion,” let him know it is not peculiar to us but common to the whole Christian church.

We have learned to compel no one to its profession. Pastors labor so that everyone may understand this to be true religion, yet so that they never flee from conversation with anyone and administer absolutely nothing by their private authority.

Magistrates, by office, take care that God’s Word is taught purely and sincerely; according to their strength they remove public stumbling blocks; they remedy occasions of disturbances and offenses; they neither can nor wish to judge hidden things.

What in these things can justly be reprehended? But let us hear the remaining calumnies too.

“If they say,” he continues, “that they cannot judge the mind but judge a person from his words, let them cease citing Peter, who, taught by no one but the Holy Spirit, judged the mind of Ananias, something they cannot do, since they are not endowed with that Spirit.”

We cut off the handle for this calumny when we applied this example to the proposed question. It appears that Bullinger or anyone else thought no differently.

For who is ignorant that the magistrate cannot imitate Peter’s deed insofar as Peter, taught by the breath of the Holy Spirit alone, judged Ananias’s mind?

But although the magistrate ordinarily obtains knowledge of a heretic’s crime in another manner, does it follow, because the cognition or proof of the crime is different, that what was extraordinarily lawful for Peter in punishing the same crime is therefore unlawful for the ordinary power?

“But,” he says, “this did not happen to Ananias because of heresy, but because he wished to divide with the Lord when he should have given either nothing or the whole. Peter declares this when he says, ‘Did not the field remain entirely yours, and when sold was it not in your power?’ As though he said, ‘No one compelled you to sell or bring it, and we do not compel those who bring nothing.’ But these men wish to punish even those who have not enrolled in their religion.

“Why do they not say the same as Peter? ‘We compel no one to sell his goods and offer them to Christ, that is, we compel no one to renounce himself. But if anyone renounces himself voluntarily, he must renounce his whole self and offer the Lord a complete and unspotted sacrifice. For the Lord wishes to possess the whole person, as a husband possesses his wife.

“If he does not serve God with his whole heart but performs less than he can, he truly imitates Ananias.’”

But because many falsehoods are said here, we shall explain them individually.

They say this did not happen to Ananias because of heresy. I confess it. What follows? That this example is ineptly adduced by us. I utterly deny that.

For I say that, if it was extraordinarily lawful to punish so severely hidden contempt of religion, hidden sacrilege, and a hidden lie, much more must the magistrate by his right restrain those same crimes when they are no longer hidden but manifest, and further joined with blasphemy, the ruin of others, and the most dangerous disturbance of civil society, and finally confirmed by stubborn obstinacy.

This, I say, I affirm. Let these men therefore invent some new reasons, if they please, by which this argument can be refuted. For that one is too weak for them to rely upon.

Next, their statement that Ananias is reprehended by Peter because he wished to divide with the Lord when he ought to have given either nothing or the whole sufficiently proves that they read the passage only while yawning.

For they pass over what is chief: Ananias’s pretense, since he wished to appear to have contributed the whole price.

Otherwise, if he had openly professed that he consecrated only part of his property to the Lord, he would not have been so reprehended by Peter, since it was no less free to him to reserve part of the price for himself than not to sell the field at all.

[PDF page 81 | logical scan 80 | printed page 164]

But since at first he had vowed the whole price to the Lord—for why otherwise did he sell the whole estate?—and afterward reserved part of it for himself, he nevertheless wished to appear to have contributed everything.

Thus he was not only unbelieving, deceitful, and ambitious, but also sacrilegious and a despiser of the Spirit by whom the church is governed, whom he plainly thought unable to understand any of these things.

Since all these things never came into their mind, I do not at all marvel that they could invent that absurd allegory.

For from this they gather that God is not content with some part of us but demands the whole person.

Although this is true, it nevertheless needed to be interpreted suitably. For as long as we live here, remnants of sin will always be in us which compel us to cry with the Apostle: “I see another law in my members rebelling against the law of my mind,” and “Wretched man that I am, who will free me from this body subject to death?” *[Marginal reference: Romans 7.]*

Further, all these things cannot be inferred from what we showed they said falsely: namely, that Ananias ought to give either nothing or everything to God.

But if this doctrine has any place in this example, Montfort ought rather to have regarded Ananias’s hypocrisy and unbelief. He dealt with God only under good security, as they say, while the Lord our God demands full faith and its open profession.

But to what end are these things? They certainly do not pertain to the subject, yet I wished to explain them briefly in order to disclose these men’s ignorance in explaining the Scriptures.

For I neither wish nor am permitted to pursue the other absurdities of this allegory. I say only that, when they write that one who does not serve God with his whole heart truly imitates Ananias, they deservedly seem capable of betraying their own cause.

For certainly this can be affirmed more truly of no one than of heretics. Thus I marvel why Montfort reprehends those who judge that they must be punished by the magistrate no less severely than Ananias formerly was punished by Peter, or rather by God himself.

“But,” you say, “to attribute to the sword what Peter did by a word is utterly absurd.”

You indeed say this, Montfort, but adduce no reason for such great absurdity. I do not marvel, since no suitable reason can be found.

Bullinger—whose piety and learning you should have revered—certainly answered rightly. He foresaw that at first glance this example could seem to some insufficiently suited to our question, since not a civil magistrate but an apostle strikes, and not with a sword but with a word. He therefore briefly answered these two heads.

First, he shows that God does here through an apostle the very thing he ordinarily does through a magistrate. That is—to explain the most learned man’s opinion at greater length—Peter is proposed here to us not simply as an apostle, which was his ordinary office, but as extraordinarily exercising the magistrate’s power to punish a sacrilegious despiser of God and the church.

Next, as regards the other head, he demonstrates that killing is always killing, and it makes little difference whether someone is killed by a sword, cups, or words. That is, it is of little importance that Ananias is said here to have been killed by a different instrument, namely a word, rather than by the sword, that is, those instruments executioners ordinarily use at the magistrate’s command.

For although the instrument is different, the punishment is nevertheless generically the same, namely bodily and capital.

It follows that this example agrees with the proposed question: whether in a cause of religion the civil magistrate may employ capital punishment.

These things he answered truly and acutely.

But Montfort, since he understands none of them, recounts marvelous mysteries to us. For he shows that to kill by a word is different from killing by a sword because the former is Christ’s and the church’s weapon, whose arms are not carnal, but the latter is carnal and suits the carnal magistrate.

But to what end is this? We certainly do not confuse the jurisdictions. Rather, he himself most greatly confuses what he wishes to appear to distinguish.

For he thinks that in this deed of Peter there appears the use of that spiritual and double-edged sword which was conceded to the church. In this he errs greatly.

Ananias’s life is taken here, which is peculiar to civil jurisdiction, whatever instrument accomplishes it.

For that demolition of fortifications does not occur by taking away life, whether by a word or another instrument, but, as the Apostle himself declares, by leading captive every thought to obey Christ, plainly by that sword of the Word which reaches to the division of soul and spirit, joints and marrow, but so that it may discern thoughts and intentions, not take away life. *[Marginal references: 2 Corinthians 10:4–5; Hebrews 4:12.]*

I nevertheless confess that some power of vengeance, which elsewhere, as I said before, the Apostle comprehends by the name “powers,” was at that time conceded to certain persons.

But this gift was neither ordinary nor perpetual. Otherwise, if you wish what was extraordinarily lawful for one or another to be lawful to ecclesiastical ministers as their own right, you will confuse civil jurisdiction with ecclesiastical power.

[PDF page 82 | logical scan 81 | printed page 165]

A little afterward the same man adds these things:

“If the magistrate kills by a sword as Peter did by a word, why can he not raise the dead by the same sword, as Peter could by a word? A robber too can kill by a sword, but cannot by a word. Show that you can do more than robbers or than the scribes and Pharisees who killed Jesus with the sword.”

I confess that, as regards the instrument, the magistrate cannot imitate Peter’s deed. But what Peter could extraordinarily do by a word, magistrates can do by the sword, that is, those instruments which they received from the Lord for this use: that human beings under their rule may lead a calm and quiet life in all godliness and dignity.

The reason they cannot raise anyone by a sword is that killing and raising are different offices. The former is the perpetual office of civil power; the latter was given to the church for a time.

Here, then, Montfort is merely inept; but he is plainly wicked in daring to compare the power ordained by God—God’s minister, I say, who bears the sword—with robbers. *[Marginal reference: Romans 13:4.]*

But I come to another objection.

“But was Ananias,” he says, “a criminal? If you were a magistrate, would you punish Ananias as a criminal? Under what law? What did he commit? He lied to the Holy Spirit. And how few are there today who do not lie to the Holy Spirit?”

And a little afterward:

“We see that the office of pastor is far different from the magistrate’s. As someone sufficiently well known to you declares in these words: ‘The church assumes nothing for itself that is proper to the magistrate, nor can the magistrate accomplish what is performed by the church.’

“If you confess these words to be true, the magistrate could not have killed Ananias, and furthermore cannot kill any of the heretics or those who must be punished by the Word. Otherwise the magistrate deprives the apostles of their weapon.” *[The 1582 marginal note cites Calvin’s 1544 anti-Libertine work; the exact chapter numeral is unclear in the scan.]*

You recognize your own words, Montfort. I answer them as follows.

Even if I concede that Ananias was not among those whom the magistrate could lawfully punish, the argument taken from his example nevertheless has force, because not only like things are compared with like, but also lesser with greater.

I therefore reason thus: if the apostles were extraordinarily permitted to punish by capital punishment contempt of religion hidden from human beings and still lurking in the mind, hidden blasphemy, and hidden sacrilege, at that time when the church was attacked by every magistrate, first of all—to say this in passing—it appears that room for capital punishment remains in the cause of religion, and that the uprooting of the tares does not hold in every case.

Next, it is certainly much more lawful for the ordinary power which has given its name to Christ to draw against a heretic—that is, an ungodly, blasphemous, obstinate, factious apostate—the sword which it received from God to restrain evildoers.

Thus, I say, I reason, Montfort. You, if you can, show the flaw in these arguments.

Moreover, what that man also well known to you wrote—the church assumes nothing proper to the magistrate and the magistrate cannot accomplish what is performed by the church—was most truly said.

But what you gather from it is so far from agreeing with it that, on the contrary, it is destroyed by it.

To make this better understood, I shall set out your whole argument:

What the church performs, the magistrate cannot accomplish.
The church performs the killing of Ananias and the blinding of Elymas.
Therefore, the magistrate cannot accomplish the killing of Ananias or the blinding of Elymas, and so forth.

Do you not see that you assume a false premise here? No one will concede it to you: namely, that these examples of Peter and Paul pertain to ecclesiastical jurisdiction.

For here each exercises extraordinary power, just as Elijah and Jehoiada did in the examples mentioned a little earlier.

Otherwise, the faculty of killing, which is peculiar to the civil magistrate, would be shared with the ecclesiastical function, so that the two jurisdictions differed not in reality but only in instrument: plainly because the civil power would kill with a sword and other instruments of that kind, but the ecclesiastical by a word alone.

Far be it from us to introduce this confusion into the church.

The argument should instead have run thus:

The church does not assume what is proper to the magistrate.
To take life is proper to the magistrate.
Therefore, the church does not take life.

The major premise can be proved from the magistrate’s office and very many testimonies of Scripture.

Therefore, this reasoning of yours also collapses:

If the magistrate can kill by iron those whom the pastor ought to kill by a word, the pastor in turn must be able to kill by a word those whom the magistrate ought to kill by iron.

For here you again strike the same rock, because you distinguish as different what are the same in nature, and you also assume what is false: namely, that pastors may kill by a word. This is false unless this extraordinary power is perhaps also conceded to them.

Montfort afterward returns to his allegory when he writes as follows:

“God wished to show in Ananias what penalties those will suffer who do not devote their whole selves to divine worship and who lie to the Holy Spirit, saying that they cannot perform more than they do.

“But this was done as a figure, just as Christ’s crucifixion was, which Paul declares when he says that those who

[PDF page 83 | logical scan 82 | printed page 166]

belong to Christ have crucified their flesh with its desires.”

But I had believed that those allegories pertained only to the Old Testament. Yet, so far as I can conjecture, we shall have to grow old amid that Cimmerian darkness.

Since these men set no limit to such figures, I ask: if those magistrates whom he compared a little earlier with robbers wished to seize this man and punish him, what could he finally complain about?

Even if they had no other more suitable reason, they could certainly use as a pretext that this punishment was nothing but an allegory by which God showed what penalties would be suffered by those who contemptuously resist the power he established and lie to the Holy Spirit, that is, use zeal for piety as a pretext for their most shameless falsehoods.

And certainly, lest anyone think us to jest in a serious matter, this allegory would be far more suitable if we considered everything diligently.

For if what he says is true—that those lie to the Holy Spirit who say they cannot perform more than they do—what will happen to those who confess that they can perform absolutely nothing good, that is, all God’s servants?

For they say with Daniel: “To you, O Lord, belongs righteousness, but to us confusion of face.” *[Marginal reference: Daniel 9:7.]* They do this no more modestly than truly.

For if Abraham himself was justified by works, he has something in which to glory, but not before God. *[Marginal reference: Romans 4:2.]*

The faithful therefore confess their weakness (*ἀσθένειαν*), not in order to deny their sin, but to seek their strength (*ἰσχυρότητα*) in Christ. Unless they did this, they would lie to the Holy Spirit. So far is this man from rightly defining what it is to lie to the Holy Spirit.

If anyone joins all these things of theirs together—first, that correction of life which alone opens an approach to Christ, as Bellius says, as though regeneration preceded our union with Christ, that is, proceeded from nature and not grace;

next, his definition of coin that is not golden but leaden and counterfeit, in which there is no word about Christ’s benefit;

third, that definition of those who lie to the Holy Spirit;

and finally, what they affirm concerning Christ’s crucifixion, namely, that it was done as a figure—

if anyone, I say, compares all these things, it can scarcely happen, unless I am mistaken, that he will not discover them to differ absolutely nothing from Pelagius in three respects.

First, insofar as they attribute to Nature, they detract from the benefit of Grace.

Second, they dream that some perfection can happen to a person here.

Third, they propose Christ’s cross to us merely as an exemplar, so that, as the Pelagians formerly said, just as sin passed from Adam to human beings not by propagation but imitation, so it is also removed by imitation of Christ, not by ingrafting.

For these two certainly cohere with each other, as Augustine rightly teaches. I nevertheless confess that somewhere he speaks of Christ’s passion, burial, and resurrection so as to wish the whole life of the faithful to be conformed to them. *[Marginal references: Augustine, On the Merits and Forgiveness of Sins; Enchiridion to Laurentius, chapter 55.]*

But that he understood this in such a way that our mortification is not so much an imitation as an effect of Christ’s death and burial appears from countless other passages.

If these men too think no differently, it is well, and I give thanks to God that they have not added this blasphemy to their other errors.

But I profess that I cannot see how this can agree with the rest of their doctrine.

Eighth.

Moreover, Montfort adds these words a little afterward: “Paul no longer knew Christ himself according to the flesh but according to the Spirit. And shall we know Paul and Peter themselves according to the flesh?”

Here I cannot sufficiently marvel why he says that Paul and Peter are known by us according to the flesh, that is, insofar as they were like other human beings. For that, I think, is what Paul means by knowing according to the flesh.

But in this example we certainly consider Peter and Paul not only as apostles but also as plainly endowed with heavenly power.

He should at least have remembered what he wrote a little earlier: namely, that Peter here used a spiritual weapon, which I nevertheless do not concede to him, and indeed was taught by the Holy Spirit; and, finally, that God himself rather than Peter accomplished this killing of Ananias. I confess with you the latter two things.

Is this really to know Christ and his apostles according to the flesh?

Ninth.

But let us pursue the rest. “Why,” he says, “do we not imitate them in healing the sick or raising the dead? If we cannot, let us not wish to take life from those to whom we cannot give life, especially since the Lord, to whom they stand or fall, forbids it.”

I am ashamed to refute these so frigid calumnies.

I say only that this question proceeds from an Anabaptist spirit.

For if this reasoning has force, that no one may kill unless he can give life, what remains except for all capital judgments to be abolished? Unless perhaps you can adduce a reason why this should have force in the cause of religion rather than in other crimes.

As for his saying that the Lord forbids it, he speaks falsely. For it is established that the passage of Paul which he points out as though with an index concerns private judgments and indifferent things.

Concerning these matters, more than enough.

[PDF page 84 | logical scan 83 | printed page 167]

Examples of ecclesiastical history.

Finally, I come to the examples of ecclesiastical history, which for the greatest part were already mentioned by us when we taught that the care of religion pertains especially to the civil magistrate.

Thus Constantine, under whom as emperor the church first possessed peace, after the Council of Nicaea had been assembled, opposed Arius so far as to command that even his books—which our Academics regard as the height of cruelty—be publicly burned, and that those who neglected to do so be capitally punished; and that idolaters, and indeed governors of the provinces who neglected to punish them, be struck down by the avenging sword. *[Marginal references: Nicephorus, book 8, chapter 24; Codex, On Pagans.]*

The emperors Valentinian, Gratian, and Theodosius also established the same or a not much lighter penalty against idolaters. We read that a horrible example of it was produced in the case of a certain Anatolius of Antioch and the companions of his crimes. They subjected Anabaptists and other heretics to punishments which you could certainly justly think more bitter than death itself. *[Marginal reference: Nicephorus, book 12, chapter 18.]*

Marcian specifically restrains with the last punishment those who attempt to teach unlawful things. Justinian too established capital punishment against those who determined to preserve the writings of Severus. *[Marginal references: Council of Chalcedon; Novels of Justinian.]*

I nevertheless do not deny that it is extant in writing concerning Gratian, which marvelously pleases our Academics, that he delivered a constitution by which each person could follow whatever religion he wished, and everyone could conduct ecclesiastical assemblies after fear was removed, except Manichaeans and those of the sects of Photinus and Eunomius.

It is likewise reported concerning Theodosius that he persecuted no heresy and compelled no one to communion, but rather permitted every heresy to hold assemblies in its own place as seemed good to each, and even that each could think and form an opinion concerning faith in Christ according to his capacity.

But there is certainly manifest vanity among the Greeks in writing these histories.

Let the writers therefore see how those things can agree with the constitutions which we read in Justinian’s Code. *[Marginal reference: Codex, On Heretics.]*

And, to say freely what the matter is, whoever reads their deeds more diligently will readily recognize that we rightly require greater zeal and knowledge in the emperors themselves.

Although I also confess that the tumults of those times were so great, when the Roman Empire was already most wretchedly troubled at home and abroad, the bishops’ ambition so great, and finally the disturbance of all things so great, that princes very often could neither understand nor perform their duty.

That profane princes were far more diligent and severe in protecting their superstition is understood even from what Livy expressly writes: from the beginnings of the Roman commonwealth, magistrates were charged to forbid foreign rites from being performed, to bar sacrificial priests and prophets from the forum, circus, and city, to seek out and burn prophetic books, and to abolish every system of sacrifice except the Roman custom.

The jurist Paulus declares the penalty added in these words: “Those who introduce new religions unknown either by use or reason, by which human minds are moved, are deported if of the more honorable class and capitally punished if of the lower.” *[Marginal reference: Paulus, Sentences, book 5.]*

Consequently, Christian princes should not borrow the rationale of their duty from the times of those emperors, nor do I think I should greatly contend with our adversaries on this point.

Rather, let them embrace God’s Word and imitate the examples of those whose piety and holiness are approved by the testimony of the Holy Spirit himself.

For this is the norm, this the rule, this the prescription of God’s will. Whoever wanders from it cannot have anything to follow in life.

Conclusion of the whole disputation.

Since we have shown from God’s own Word that it is his will that the magistrate punish contempt of religion even by capital punishment if necessary; since all good and holy princes employed this; and since, unless I am mistaken, we have sufficiently suitably refuted what is ordinarily said for the contrary opinion, I profess that I cannot assent to those who judge that there is no room for capital punishment in a cause of religion.

I nevertheless dissent from them only so far as I think extreme remedies should be applied only to extreme evils, and that even in these extreme remedies such moderation must be preserved that love and mercy worthy of a Christian person are never violated.

For in these matters great account must be taken first of persons, then errors, and finally the punishments themselves, as someone rightly explained, namely the man against whom Montfort wrote that distinguished refutation of his.

I have chosen to copy his words partly because they plainly and clearly set forth the whole sum of this controversy, and partly so that everyone may recognize the judgment of the Academics in reprehending this most learned man.

“In persons, then,” he says, “there is great diversity. There are standard-bearers and leaders obstinate in their errors, who are audacious, hypocritical, and talkative, and therefore most prepared to seduce others. They themselves rush without correction into their own destruction and drag others with them

[PDF page 85 | logical scan 84 | printed page 168]

into ruin. All people of this kind must be restrained by every means as plagues of the church, lest their contagion spread more widely like a cancer.

“There are next certain people who have been seduced and made senseless by others. They err, but not through malice or obstinately, and recover their senses in time. They should not be condemned immediately, but the Lord should be prayed to, and they should be borne with and, while erring, instructed in a spirit of gentleness and meekness until they return to a better mind.

“Furthermore, among erroneous doctrines some are graver than others. Certain ones are so blasphemous, ungodly, and unworthy that they cannot even be heard, much less tolerated. Some tend directly and openly toward overthrowing the commonwealth unless they are suppressed in time.

“But it is necessary to prove by the Scriptures and manifest truth that the matters charged are such as they are said to be.

“Once the truth has been ascertained and open testimonies of Scripture produced, blasphemers and overthrowers of the church and commonwealth may be punished with the utmost severity.

“But a lighter and milder penalty should be decreed against those who err in light and not capital errors. For some err, but in such a way that God is not blasphemed by their errors and the church stands; finally, the commonwealth itself is not disturbed.

“Here again let that saying of the Apostle occur: ‘Bear one another’s burdens.’ Likewise, ‘Receive those weak in faith, not for judgments of disputes.’

“Now there is also a conspicuous distinction in punishments and penalties. Those who obstinately err and strive to lead others into error with themselves and retain them in their errors—blasphemers, disturbers, and indeed overthrowers of churches—can lawfully be killed.

“Nevertheless, not everyone who errs should immediately be subjected to capital punishment. Those who can be treated by threats and rebukes should not be purged and punished by bitterer measures. Due measure is best in every matter.

“There is also a financial fine; there are prisons in which those corrupted by the poison of depraved doctrine and faith can be confined, lest they infect others. There are other bodily afflictions by which those who err are restrained both so that they harm others less and so that they themselves may be preserved—not perish altogether, but recover their senses.

“But the judge’s fear of God, equity, and prudence will understand from the circumstances how depraved doctrines must be punished among seducers and the seduced, and obstinate rebellion or foolish and minimally malicious credulity.

“Serious and diligent admonition has no place in abominable crimes already perpetrated, which must be purged by the sword. Therefore, let the magistrate take care to admonish in time those who must be admonished.

“For serious and diligent admonition has been diligently commended to every ruler, to be exercised toward subjects and those already endangered in some matter.

“Prudent and godly magistrates have also not rarely spared those who sinned ignorantly but recovered their senses at serious admonition.

“The Lord in the Gospel commands that one who sins be admonished, that the obedient be spared, and that graver penalties be inflicted upon the rebellious.

“Before Joshua called to arms, he sent an embassy and ordered the Reubenites with their allies to demolish the altar which they seemed to have built contrary to the Lord’s law.

“Emperor Justinian also granted life to those who recovered their senses and embraced a better opinion, in Constitution 109.

“For Josiah too did not kill everyone entangled in errors, but especially the incurable.

“Moderation must therefore be employed by the magistrate in punishing the guilty.”

Thus far that man. His opinion, joined with the preceding arguments, will satisfy, I think, every moderate disposition and lover of truth.

I shall nevertheless add this one thing: the princes’ duty in vindicating religion against obstinate and factious disturbers of the church can suitably be reduced to three heads.

First, they must observe that they contain themselves within the limits and barriers of their power. Namely, the prince must not dare to judge what things are heresy, for this role belongs not to the principate but to the church.

Next, they must take care that judgments of heresies are conducted holily and incorruptly, not from any human authority whatever its source, or the bare prejudice of any time and custom, but from the pure and sincere administration of God’s Word, lest through error they show themselves not vindicators of God’s truth but ministers of another’s cruelty.

Finally, only after a calm and lawful cognizance of heresy has occurred should they, after taking diligent account of every circumstance, as we have said, decree the penalty by which God’s majesty receives its proper honor and suitable provision is made for the church’s peace and tranquility.

This, then, is our opinion concerning this whole question. Those who supplied us the occasion for this writing caused us to be somewhat longer in explaining it.

For I undertook a contention with no one, much less with good and by no means contentious people who perhaps think somewhat differently. Rather, I undertook the church’s defense, which these shameless Academics have long attempted to overthrow through a pretense of clemency and modesty. I did so also by opposing to them the names of those whose reputation and esteem I could not bear to have violated by them so shamelessly and calumniously.

For, I ask, what do those leave us who, although they appear to prohibit rash human judgments, nevertheless judge so perversely concerning God’s church itself? Who, although they wish nothing to be so affirmed in religion that its contrary is condemned, nevertheless assert things than which nothing worse or more criminal can be imagined?

Who confuse God with an idol? Who require no other faith among Christians than among devils? Who begin salvation in us? Who place Christ’s

[PDF page 86 | logical scan 85 | printed page 169]

state and office, the Trinity, predestination, and free will among matters which are neither necessary for obtaining salvation nor, if known, make a person better?

Who think that in controversies of religion even with papists—for example, concerning invocation of saints, justification, free will, baptism, and the Lord’s Supper—possession must be granted according to liberty, that is, that each person must be left to his own judgment, because concerning these supposedly obscure questions, knowledge of which is not so necessary for salvation, nothing certain can yet be established?

Who propose to us as a rule Gamaliel’s opinion, limping toward either side?

Who teach that Scripture is obscure and enigmatic and that new, fuller and clearer revelations must be awaited?

Who introduce opinion in place of faith, probability in place of truth, plausibility in place of necessity?

Who so openly patronize heretics that they assert no class of people is less to be feared by the commonwealth?

Who judge not only that heretics must never be killed, but that they must not even be punished?

Who remove the civil magistrate from every care of religion?

Who compare magistrates with robbers?

Who so shamelessly corrupt so many passages of Scripture?

Who transform the whole of Scripture into dreams through their fanatical allegories?

Who burden the most religious magistrates of so many holy churches and so many faithful pastors of the Lord’s flock with countless calumnies and curses?

Who, finally, no longer obscurely mutter all these things as before, but loudly declaim them openly in published books?

For these are the portents, these the monsters of errors, which, although they had long nourished them, they finally could no longer contain after that Servetus, their excellent little brother, paid the penalties.

Thus, as though undertaking the defense of clemency so as to turn the minds of the simple away from what they principally seek, they were so moved by indignation assumed from the martyrdom of that most innocent Servetus that they spewed all this poison into God’s church.

If they can obtain the license which they have long sought, what crime, what wicked deed will they not undertake?

Therefore, when we ask here whether heretics must be restrained by the magistrate, we do not contend over a matter of no value or dispute an insufficiently necessary matter. Rather, the church’s salvation and safety is at issue for us: the salvation, I say, of that church which God’s Son acquired for himself by his blood, in whose bosom and innermost parts I see these men nourished too long, so that unless they are confronted promptly we will be captured before we sense that we are being attacked.

You, therefore, holy and faithful magistrates of the churches, you vigilant pastors of the churches, and by name you, most illustrious Prince of Württemberg, whose most distinguished name in God’s church these most cruel devastators of God’s church were not ashamed to place before their heresies: you, I say, I, the least of the faithful, exhort and implore promptly to repel with every zeal, care, industry, and diligence these white wolves preparing to attack the flock of God entrusted to you.

Finally, I invoke God himself against you Academics. You do injury here to his Word by denying that controversies of religion can be decided from it. You so despise his church that you falsely accuse its godly magistrates and faithful pastors of savagery, cruelty, and monstrousness. You either attack his truth when well known, or at least willingly ignore it; and, not content with this, in published books you do not cease to ambush the faith and conscience of the simple through a pretense of piety.

For although you studiously conceal your names, the style of the discourse, and the name of the city in which this medley of yours and many other little books certainly not much better were published, nevertheless your pursuit of novelty and your pretense betray you.

Thus everyone understands both who you are and what you are preparing.

Would that the Lord scatter your counsels.

I certainly trust that this will happen.
